Explicatio in Cantica canticorum
by Richard of Saint Victor
Source text: Public Domain. English translation/apparatus/encoding: CC BY-NC 4.0, Wroot Press.
https://migne.app/pl/196/explicatio-in-cantica-canticorum/
----------------------------------------

## PROLOGUE.

[0405A]

After the human race was cast out from the joys of paradise, coming into this pilgrimage of the present life, it has a heart blind to spiritual understanding. To which blind heart, if it were said with a human voice: Follow God, or: Love God, as was said to it in the law, once sent outside and cold through the torpor of unbelief, it would not grasp what it heard. Therefore the divine word speaks to the torpid and cold soul through certain riddles, and out of the things she knows it secretly instils into her the love she does not know. For allegory makes, as it were, a kind of engine for the soul set far from God, that by it she may be lifted up to God: for with riddles interposed, while she recognizes in the words something that is her own, she understands in the sense of the words [0405B] what is not her own, and through earthly words she is separated from earth. For by this, that she does not shrink from the known, she understands something unknown. For by things known to us, out of which allegories are put together, the divine meanings are put together, and while we come to know the outward words, we arrive at the inward understanding. For hence it is that in this book which is written as the Song of Songs, words of a love as it were of the body are set down, so that the soul, chafed back to warmth from her own body by discourses of her accustomed manner, may grow warm again, and through words of the love that is below may be roused to the love that is above. For in this book kisses are named, breasts are named, cheeks are named, thighs are named. In which words Holy Scripture is not to be derided, [0405C] but the ampler mercy of God is to be considered; because, while it names the members of the body, it so calls to love.

It is to be noted that he is wondrously with us, and works mercifully. Who, that he might kindle our heart to the incitement of holy love, came down even to the words of our base love. But whence he humbles himself in speaking, thence he exalts us in understanding, because from the discourses of this love we learn by what power of this love we may glow in the love of the Godhead. Yet this must be watchfully looked to by us, lest, when we hear the words of outward love, we remain at the sensing of outward things, and the engine which is set there that it may lift us should rather press us down so that we be not lifted. We ought therefore in these bodily words, in these outward words, to seek whatever is inward, and, speaking of the body, as it were [0405D] to become outside the body. We ought to come to these nuptials of the Bridegroom and the bride with the understanding of inmost charity, that is, [0406A] with the wedding garment, that is, with a worthy understanding of charity: with which if we are not clothed, let us drive away [cj: *repellamus*; read *repellemur*, "we shall be driven out"] from this nuptial banquet into the outer darkness, that is, the blindness of ignorance. We ought through these words of passion to pass over to the virtue of impassibility. For Holy Scripture is in words and senses just as a painting is in colors and things. And he is exceedingly foolish who so clings to the colors of a painting that he does not know the things which are painted. For if we embrace the words which are outwardly spoken, and do not know the senses, we hold only the colors, as though not knowing the things which are depicted: *The letter killeth,* as it is written, *but the spirit quickeneth [n: (II Cor. III)]*; for the letter covers over the spirit as chaff covers the wheat; but chaff is for beasts to feed on, wheat for men. He therefore who [0406B] uses human reason, let him cast away the chaff of beasts, and hasten to eat the wheat of the spirit. For to this end it is useful, that the mysteries be covered, wrapped up in the letter. For hence it is written: *The wise shall hide understanding [n: (Prov. X)]* [var: Vulg. *Sapiens abscondet scientiam*, "a wise man hideth knowledge"], because assuredly under the covering of the letter the spiritual understanding is covered over. Hence again in the same book Scripture says: *It is the glory of God to conceal the word [n: (Prov. XXV)]*. For to the mind seeking God, God appears the more gloriously the more subtly and inwardly he is searched out that he may appear. But are we not to inquire into those things which God conceals in mysteries? We ought indeed.

For there follows: *And it is the glory of kings to search out the discourse.* For they who have already learned to rule and to search out their own bodies or the motions of the flesh, [0406C] are kings. It is therefore the glory of kings to search out the discourse, because it is the praise of those who live well to scrutinize the secrets of God's commandments. Hearing therefore the words of human converse, we ought to be as it were men out of the earth, lest, if we hear in a human way the things which are said, we be able to perceive nothing of divinity concerning those things which we ought to hear. Paul desired his disciples to be as it were no longer men, to whom he said: *For whereas there is among you zeal and contention, are you not men? [n: (I Cor. III.)]* The Lord esteemed his disciples as it were no longer men, when he said: *Whom do men say that the Son of man is [n: (Matth. XVI)]*. And when they had answered him with the words of men, he at once added: *But whom do you say that I am?* For when above he says *men,* and then [0406D] subjoins, *but you,* he made a certain distance between men and his disciples, because plainly, [0407A] by instilling divine things into them, he made them above men, as the Apostle says: *If therefore there be any new creature in Christ, the old things are passed away [n: (II Cor. V)]*. And we know that in our resurrection the body is so joined to the spirit that all that had been of passibility is taken up into the power of the spirit. He therefore who follows God ought daily to imitate his own reason, so that, as then, he may have nothing passible in his heart, so that according to the inward man he may already be a new creature. Let him now tread down whatever has been old, and in old words seek the force of newness alone. For Holy Scripture is a certain mountain, from which the Lord comes into our hearts to be understood; of which mountain it is said through the prophet: *God will come from Lebanon, and the Holy One from the shady and thick mountain [n: (Habac. III)]* [var: Vulg. *Deus ab austro veniet, et Sanctus de monte Pharan*, "God will come from the south, and the Holy One from mount Pharan"]. That mountain is thick [0407B] by its meanings, and shady by its allegories. But it must be known that, when the voice of the Lord sounds on the mountain, we are bidden to wash our garments, and to be cleansed from every defilement of the flesh, if we hasten to approach the mountain. For it is written: *That if a beast touch the mountain, it shall be stoned [n: (Hebr. XII)]*. For a beast touches the mountain when men, given over to irrational motions, draw near to the loftiness of Holy Scripture, and do not understand it according as they ought, but irrationally bend it to the understanding of their own pleasure. For everyone absurd, or slothful in mind, if he be seen about this mountain, let him be slain by most fierce sentences as it were by stones. For this mountain burns, because Holy Scripture, which spiritually fills with the fire of love, sets alight. Whence it is written: *Thy word is exceedingly fiery [0407C] [n: (Psal. CXVIII)]*. Whence, when certain men walking in the way heard the words of God, they said: *Was not our heart burning within us, while he opened to us the Scriptures? [n: (Luc. XXIV.)]* Whence it is said through Moses: *In his right hand a fiery law [n: (Deut. XXXIII)]*. By the left hand of God the wicked are understood, who do not pass over into the right-hand part. The right hand of God are the elect, who are separated from those on the left. In the right hand of God, therefore, the law is fiery, because in the hearts of the elect (who are to be set at the right hand) the divine precepts are fragrant, and they are set alight with the ardor of charity. Let this fire, therefore, burn away whatever of rust and of oldness is outwardly in us, and offer our mind as a holocaust in the contemplation of God.

[0407D]Nor is it to be attended to idly that this book is called not a canticle, but the Song of Songs. For as in the Old Testament some things are holy, others the Holy of Holies; some Sabbaths, others the Sabbaths of Sabbaths; so in Holy Scripture some things are canticles, others the Song of Songs. Holy were the things which were done outwardly in the tabernacle; Sabbaths were those which were celebrated in each single week; but the Holy of Holies were received with a certain more secret veneration; and the Sabbaths of Sabbaths were kept only on the highest festivals. This Song of Songs is a secret, and a certain inward solemnity, which secret is penetrated in the hidden places of one who understands. For if it is attended to in outward words, it is not a secret. It is also to be known that in Holy Scripture some are [0408A] canticles of victory, others canticles of exhortation and protestation, others canticles of exultation, others canticles of help, others canticles of conjunction with God. The canticle of victory is that which Moses sang when the Red Sea had been crossed, saying: *Let us sing to the Lord, for he is gloriously honored: the horse and the rider he hath cast forth into the sea [n: (Exod. XV)]* [var: Vulg. *magnificatus est … dejecit*, "is gloriously magnified … he hath thrown down"]. The canticle of exhortation and protestation is that which Moses said to the Israelites as they drew near to the land of promise: *Attend, O heaven, to the things which I shall speak; let the earth hear the words out of my mouth [n: (Deut. XXXII)]* [var: Vulg. *Audite caeli quae loquor, audiat terra verba oris mei*, "Hear, O ye heavens, the things which I speak"]. The canticle of exultation is that which Anna sang, the fruitfulness of the Church having been foreseen in herself, saying: *My heart hath exulted in the Lord [n: (I Reg. II)]*, where by herself she expressed the figured fruitfulness of the Church's offspring, when she said: *The barren hath borne [0408B] very many, and she that had very many is become weak [n: (ibid.)]*. The canticle of help David sang after the battle, saying: *I will love thee, O Lord, my strength [n: (Psal. XVII)]*. But the canticle of conjunction with God is this, which is sung at the nuptials of the Bridegroom and the bride, that is, the Song of Songs, which is by so much loftier than all canticles as it is also offered at nuptials of a loftier solemnity. For by those canticles vices are avoided, but by this one each and all are enriched with virtues. For by those the enemy is guarded against, but by this the Lord embraces with familiar love.



## PROLOGUE. (cont.)

And it is to be noted that sometimes the Lord in Holy Scripture calls himself Lord, sometimes father, sometimes bridegroom. For when he wishes to be feared, he names himself Lord; when he wishes to be honored, father; [0408C] when he wishes to be loved, bridegroom. For he himself says through the prophet: *If I am Lord, where is my fear? If I am father, where is my honor? [n: (Malac. I.)]* [var: Vulg. Mal. 1:6 has the father first: *Si ergo pater ego sum, ubi est honor meus? et si Dominus ego sum, ubi est timor meus?*] And again he says: *I have espoused thee to myself in justice and in faith [n: (Ose. II)]* [var: Vulg. Osee 2:20 *sponsabo te mihi in fide*, "I will espouse thee to me in faith"]. Or assuredly: I have remembered the day of thy espousal in the desert. But because he first wishes to be feared that he may be honored, and first to be honored that men may come to him, he both names himself Lord for the sake of fear, and father for the sake of honor, and bridegroom for the sake of love; through fear one comes to honor, but through honor of him one arrives at love. For by how much honor is worthier than fear, by so much the more does God rejoice to be called father than Lord. And by how much love is dearer than honor, by so much the more does God rejoice to be called bridegroom than father. In this book, therefore, the Lord [0408D] and the Church are named not Lord and handmaid, but Bridegroom and bride, so that not only fear, not only reverence, but love also may be served, and in these outward words the inward affection be stirred up. When he names himself Lord, he indicates that we have been created; when he calls himself father, he indicates that we have been adopted; when he names himself bridegroom, he indicates that we have been conjoined. But it is more to be conjoined to God than created and adopted. In this book, therefore, where he is called Bridegroom, something loftier is instilled, since in it the covenant of conjunction is shown. Which names, in the New Testament (because the conjunction of the Word and the flesh, of Christ and the Church, is now accomplished and celebrated) are recalled with frequent repetition. Whence John [0409A] says while the Lord was living: *He that hath the bride is the bridegroom [n: (Joan. III)]*. Whence the same Lord: *The children of the bridegroom shall not fast so long as the bridegroom is with them [n: (Matth. IX)]* [var: Vulg. Matt. 9:15 *Numquid possunt filii sponsi lugere*, "Can the children of the bridegroom mourn"; the "shall not fast" form is that of Mark 2:19 and Luke 5:34]. Whence also it is said to the Church: *I have espoused you to one husband, that I may present you a chaste virgin to Christ [n: (I Cor. XI)]*. And again: *That he might present to himself a glorious Church, not having spot or wrinkle [n: (Ephes. V)]*. And again in the Apocalypse of John: *Blessed are they that are called to the supper of the Lamb of the marriage [n: (Apoc. XIX)]* [var: Vulg. Apoc. 19:9 *ad coenam nuptiarum Agni*, "to the marriage supper of the Lamb"]. And again in the same place: *And I saw the bride as a new spouse coming down from heaven [n: (Apoc. XXI)]* [var: Vulg. Apoc. 21:2 *Et vidi sanctam civitatem Jerusalem novam descendentem de caelo a Deo, paratam sicut sponsam ornatam viro suo*, "And I saw the holy city, the new Jerusalem, coming down out of heaven from God, prepared as a bride adorned for her husband"]. Nor does it depart from a great mystery, that this book of Solomon is set third among his works: for the ancients said that there are three orders of life, the moral, the natural, and the contemplative. Which lives the Greeks named ethic, physic, and [0409B] theoric. In the Proverbs, too, the moral life is expressed, where it is said: *Hear, my son, my wisdom, and incline thy ear to my prudence [n: (Prov. II)]* [var: Vulg. Prov. 5:1 *Fili mi, attende ad sapientiam meam*, "My son, attend to my wisdom"]. But in Ecclesiastes, the natural. For there it is considered that all things tend to an end, when it is said: *Vanity of vanities, and all is vanity [n: (Eccle. I)]*. In the Song of Songs the contemplative life is expressed, while in it the coming and the sight of the Lord himself is desired, when it is said in the voice of the bride: *Come from Lebanon, come [n: (Cant. IV)]*, or else he signified the orders and the lives of the three patriarchs, namely of Abraham, Isaac and Jacob. Abraham indeed held morality through obedience, while Isaac figured the natural life by digging wells. To dig wells in the depth is to search out by inquiry, through natural consideration, all the things which are below. [0409C] But Jacob held the contemplative life, who saw the angels ascending and descending. But because natural consideration does not lead to perfection unless morality be first held, rightly is Ecclesiastes set after the Proverbs; and because the supernal contemplation is not beheld unless these things which slip away below be first despised, rightly is the Song of Songs set after Ecclesiastes. For first it is to compose one's manners, afterwards to consider all things which are present as though they were not present. But in the third place, with the cleansed edge of the heart, he beholds the inward and supernal things. And so by these steps of books he made as it were a ladder to contemplation, so that while first things are well conducted in the world, afterwards even the honorable things [0409D] of the world be despised, and at the last the inmost things of God be beheld. Now thus, in general, the coming of the Lord in this time is awaited from the voice of the Church, so that also, in particular, every single soul may behold the entrance of God into her own heart, as it were the approach of the Bridegroom into the bridal chamber.

And it is to be known that in this book four speaking persons are introduced, the Bridegroom and the bride, the young maidens with the bride, and the troops of companions with the Bridegroom. For the bride assuredly is the Church herself, the Bridegroom the Lord; but the young maidens with the bride are souls beginning, and coming to their youth through new zeal; while the companions of the Bridegroom are either angels, who often, living from him, have appeared to men, or else all perfect men in the Church, [0410A] who have known how to announce the truth to men. But those who singly are young maidens or companions are all together the bride, because all together they are the Church, although according to each one all these three names may be taken. For he who loves God perfectly is the bride; he who preaches the Bridegroom is a companion. He who, as yet new, follows the way of good men is a young maiden. Let us strive, therefore, that we may be the bride: if we do not yet prevail in this, let us be companions: if we have attained neither of these, let us at least come together to this bridal chamber as young maidens. Since therefore we have spoken of the Bridegroom and the bride, the Lord and the Church, let us, as young maidens or companions, hear the words of the Bridegroom, hear the words of the bride, and from their discourses learn the fervor of love. And so let holy Church, [0410B] long awaiting the coming of the Lord, long thirsting for the fountain of life, say how it behoves her to see the presence of her Bridegroom, how she desires him.



## CHAPTER ONE. *How God is sought in stillness, and on the greater desire for grace.*

*In my little bed by night I sought him whom my soul loveth [n: (Cant. III)]* [var: Vulg. Cant. 3:1 *per noctes*, "by nights," plural; the exposition below turns on the single night]. The soul which seeks God, and desires to attain more fully to the love and the knowledge of God, ought to seek him in the little bed, that is, in the stillness of the mind. For she ought to be at leisure, and to be still from the occupation and disquiet of earthly things. For unless she has been still from the care and occupation and [0410C] love of outward things, she cannot seek God, nor does she deserve to find him. In the little bed therefore, that is, in the stillness of the mind, God is to be sought. And by night, that is, through the adversities of temptations and of labors, and in the battle of the virtues and the vices. For the soul must labor in many adversities, and be held back by many obstacles, and be wearied by heavy wrestling, before she perfectly corrects her manners, and obtains cleanness of heart, and deserves to see God. Whence deservedly she is said to seek God by night, namely through adversities and labors. And not in the bed, but in the little bed, because the stillness had from such things is scant, and narrow, since vices assuredly assail the soul, and former habit, and the memory and the delight of past sins. For which cause she also calls it her own little bed, [0410D] and not ours, because she cannot have this in common with the Bridegroom, nor perfectly enjoy him in so scant a stillness, because his place is made in peace, and not in the battle and disturbance of vices. In this manner God is sought in the little bed by night by those beginning and not yet perfect. After this he is sought in another manner in the little bed and by night, namely when the soul, her vices more fully mortified and her temptations lulled, and her conscience purged, shall have obtained a fuller stillness, the grace of the Holy Spirit coming into the mind and quieting the vices and the temptations. According to that which is written: *The bow he shall break in pieces and shatter, arms and shields he shall burn with fire [n: (Psal. XLV)]*. Then the soul will be able to be at leisure, and to see that the Lord is sweet. Thus [0411A] the peace and tranquillity of the mind is the little bed in which the bride rests. In this little bed she seeks the beloved by night, namely when the desires of the flesh are lulled, and after the battle and the labors victory has followed, and stillness has been given. For in the day there is laboring, and in the night there is a ceasing from labor, and in the night there is stillness. When therefore, after the burden of the day and the heats, temptation namely, the soul comes from labor to the little bed of stillness, and to the night of resting from weariness and disquiet, then, now unencumbered and at leisure, she will be able better to seek the beloved. In this little bed and in this night she sleeps, but her heart keeps watch. For she sleeps outwardly, but inwardly keeps watch. And as much as she is still and at leisure outwardly, so much is she occupied and unquiet inwardly in seeking the beloved. But she seeks him in the little bed and not in the bed; because [0411B] she does not possess full stillness, but narrow, so long as she does not enjoy the beloved to her wish, nor has him with her. Whence also she calls the little bed itself her own, as being one which she alone has, and in which she does not yet rest with the Bridegroom. And therefore it is well said that she seeks by night (namely of blindness) in which she is held, so that she does not fully enjoy him, nor feel his presence. For he is indeed present by essence, present by desire, and he is already had who is desired, because one not had could not be desired; but he is not at hand by the revelation of his presence. For he has not yet put her blindness to flight, nor illumined her to feel his presence. Of this night, therefore, she complains, and pleads that through it she seeks the beloved, and does not find him. She desires [0411C] therefore to be illumined and to have the eyes of her heart opened, that she may find him, and by the grace drawing near may understand his presence, and may feel both that she is seen by him and that she sees him. For she seeks the illumination of an ampler grace, the understanding of his presence, and the experience of spiritual sweetness: *I sought,* she says, *him whom my soul loveth.* In the love of the soul we can note a spiritual and also an affectionate love. But when we love someone affectionately, we say that we love him as our own soul, as if she should say: Whom I love affectionately and spiritually, that is, unto the salvation of the soul, not unto temporal or carnal advantage. But the Bridegroom does not always at once offer himself to her who seeks him, nor show himself to her who desires him, but often defers to fulfil her wishes that he may increase them, and afterwards reward them increased and more perfectly [0411D] fulfil them. He also sometimes defers these things, that he may prove the constancy of her who seeks, and grant the things desired the more profitably the later. For what is acquired with labor is held more closely and guarded more warily. And sometimes desires more quickly fulfilled fail, and deferred ones grow the more. Whence the bride, feeling the delay, and not knowing the dispensation, and impatient of love, complains, saying: *I sought, and I found him not [n: (Cant. V)]*. For she does not yet perceive the grace sought for, she has not yet felt the presence of the Bridegroom.



## CHAPTER II. *How the soul considers the examples both of the perfect and of the imperfect.*

And so the bride, having suffered difficulty and as it were a repulse, in no wise is still from seeking, but girds herself [0412A] more amply for seeking. For she shows her own importunity and constancy, saying: *I will rise, and I will go about the city [n: (Cant. III)]*. She turns herself to humility, that whom she does not find through herself, she may seek through others, if perchance through them she may find him. With much labor she had prepared a bed for herself, first ceasing from sins, and afterwards contending more strongly against vices, and after these things withdrawing from occupations and cares, and at the last being at leisure from harmful things, and truly also from very many good works, so that, made less in her deeds, she might perceive the wisdom which is God. But now she turns to going about and searching out the good works of others, that by this labor she may find the Bridegroom, whom being still she had less found. She rises from stillness, going about, namely, the city. She goes about in thought the assembly [0412B] of good men, that in them she may take examples of a better life and lessons of the virtues: for through humility she reckons that there is lacking to her the adornment of virtues, and cleanness of heart, through which she might deserve to find the Bridegroom, showing the house of her mind, though free from vices, yet less adorned with virtues. Which house, that she may adorn it more becomingly, going about and traversing the virtues of the saints, and exploring the special grace of each single one, and plucking flowers from one of humility, from another of patience, from another of obedience, from another of abstinence, from another of charity, from another of purity. These she strives to transfuse by imitation into her manners, and with them to adorn the house of her mind. For she measures the fabric, that is, she weighs the life of the saints, and considers in them the construction of the virtues, that she may blush in herself for her own imperfection. [0412C] And, as is read in Job, she looks upon men, she attends to the holy [n: (Job XXXIII)], that she may reckon herself a sinner in comparison with them; whence, humbled by their examples and kindled to advancement, she gives her effort anew to the cleansing of her heart and to the cultivating of the virtues, that with her house more fully cleansed and adorned she may become worthy for the finding of the Bridegroom. The bride herself goes about the city (good men, namely) that she may search out their virtues and holiness: *And she seeks the beloved through the streets and the broad ways.* The streets are narrower, and the broad ways wider. Whence by the streets are designated those who, leaving all things, and naked following the naked Christ, have chosen for themselves a narrow way of living. But by the broad ways, which are wider, we understand the perfect of secular life, who, walking by a wider way, [0412D] and not leaving all their own things, but using their own well, have pleased God by hospitalities and alms and the other good works. Who, turning aside from the broad way which leads to death, have held the strait way of the commandments and of life. She seeks therefore the beloved first through the streets, and afterwards through the broad ways.

For because she is borne by the desire of perfection, she seeks examples first from the perfect. She considers by how strait a law of living they bound themselves, and how slender was their food, how poor their clothing, how brief their sleep, how heavy the labor of the body, how strict the discipline of the heart, how in all things they cut away the delights of the body, drew out the wills of the heart. She weighs after these things how great was in them the loftiness and the liberty of mind. And how, dwelling in the flesh, they had their converse [0413A] in the heavens, how they passed beyond all earthly things and trod them down. Some of these were sustained by a little bread and water, some lived not on bread but on herbs or roots, and some received their food from heaven, some led their life almost without any food at all, some dwelt without any clothing at all under the naked air. Who, casting away not only the acts of the world, but even all its matter, were as it were outside the flesh, and by as much as they passed beyond the world and went beyond the vices of the flesh, by so much, filled with grace and swallowed up by the spirit, they searched out the hidden things of the Godhead, and knew them. And so the soul, made fervent, runs through these so heavenly ones, filled with so great grace, and considers them, and what she has less in herself, in them she loves. Through these she seeks God, that in their [0413B] examples her love, being nourished, may at some time rise up to God. For since in them the image of God, and in great part the likeness, shines out, and by as much as she has been able to imitate them and to be made like them, by so much does she deserve to be united to God, and by so much does she receive God into herself, as she has taken on his likeness. For nothing is so near to God as his character and likeness. But when the soul has been made just, true, merciful, wise through grace, which God has by nature, by as much as she has formed these virtues in herself, by so much will she reform in herself the likeness of God. Wherefore, when the bride attends to the perfect, and labors to be made like them, and walks by their examples that she may find the beloved, through the streets she seeks him whom her soul loveth. She seeks [0413C] him also through the broad ways, namely in certain seculars or even in married persons. For nowhere does she hold the examples and incitements of salvation to be despised. And so many of this sort, although they held a lighter and wider way of converse, nevertheless spread abroad the odor of a good report of themselves, and afforded examples of imitation. What examples of sanctity are there in Abraham? What in Job are not found, by which a soul seeking God might be kindled and built up? Who were filled with so great grace, and endowed with so great love of God, that the one of them offered his only son to death at the Lord's command, and the other bore with even mind the death of all his children, and rendered thanks to God. The one deserved to be blessed by the Lord for his excelling merit, the other, in the loss of all that he had had, [0413D] blessed the Lord. The one deserved to enjoy the converse of angels, whom also he received in hospitality, and fed. The other heard the Lord himself speaking, and saw him with the eyes of his heart illumined. With how great a light of divine grace was he illumined, whose mind the night of so many adversities and of the enemy's temptations did not darken, and whom his whole quiet bed, turned about, did not overturn? Not undeservedly, therefore, does the bride run from her night, and from the bed of her stillness in which she did not find the Bridegroom, to this man, whom she knows to have found an ampler grace in his own night and in his troubled bed.

Moses and Samuel, among the other virtues in which they shone excellently, set forth that surpassing example of charity, that they devoutly interceded for their enemies. The one indeed for the people that was stoning him, the other [0414A] for the people that was casting him out from his princedom. The one with so great charity that he asked of the Lord either that their sin be forgiven them, or that he himself be blotted out of the book of life. The other declares that he prays no less devoutly for those casting him down. Who would not wonder at the humility of David, the man, namely, whom God found according to his own heart? Who after so great and so glorious deeds did not lift himself up into pride, but rather called himself a dead dog or one single flea. Who manifests that he had had both outward and inward humility by these words, in which he says: *I will play and become viler than I have been, and I will be humble in my own eyes [n: (II Reg. VI)]*. For he did not refuse outward vileness, and inwardly he was abject in his own judgment. How many such were there under the law, or even in the time of grace, who afforded of themselves wholesome [0414B] examples of the virtues? Through these, therefore, the bride also seeks the beloved, and through them kindles herself into the love of him.



## CHAPTER III. *How the examples of good men, once considered, humble and kindle.*

*I sought, and I found him not [n: (Cant. III)]* [var: Vulg. Cant. 3:1, the place cited, reads *quaesivi illum, et non inveni*; the wording printed here is that of Cant. 5:6, which the previous chapter cites as (Cant. V)]. The bride sought the beloved through the streets and the broad ways, and did not find him to her wish. For she has not yet felt his presence, has not yet received the grace desired. Nor must I doubt but that in her going about she acquired much, and increased much grace in herself. Since indeed from the knowledge of good men and of their virtues the desire of justice was kindled in her not a little. For she came near [0414C] to those burning with this fire which the Lord Jesus sent upon the earth, and willed that it be vehemently kindled. To whom, while one lukewarm draws near, he grows warm, and one fervent pardons the more [cj: *ignoscit*; read *ignescit*, "catches fire the more"]. But not because she has acquired grace is she still from seeking an ampler grace: her desires have been kindled, not fulfilled, stirred up, not stilled, and by as much as they have grown, by so much do the things she has received suffice her less. The increase of grace has stirred up hunger, so far that by as much as she has received more, by so much does she desire more. The bad or the lukewarm man, because he does not love or desire the good, knows not his own defect, nor to how great things he is held. Whence, though he be needy, he reckons himself rich, because he knows not the spiritual riches. For he says, "I am rich, and I need nothing," when he is wretched and miserable, and poor, and blind, and naked; but the good man and one advancing [0414D] does not attend to what he has, but stretches himself always to the things before. And because he thinks that he has never laid hold, he is always humbled out of desire. In one advancing, therefore, with grace growing, humility also grows, and by as much as he is rich, by so much does he believe himself needy, because grace, once tasted, kindles him the more. And so the bride advances: for by as much as she is kindled to love, by so much is she humbled. She believes that she has not yet found grace, while she attends to the virtues of the perfect, whence she says: *I sought and I found him not.* She says that she has not found him whom she found so perfectly in others. So Antony, the greatest of monks, after he had found Paul whom he had sought, judged himself to be nothing in comparison with him, saying: "Woe to me a sinner, who bear the false name of a monk." This [0415A] the finding of Paul afforded him, that he who before had stood forth as the greatest of monks now said that he was not worthy even of the name of a monk. Which is wonderful in imitation, since one highest in the merit of sanctity became by his own judgment the lowest, and one so lofty before God became so cheap to himself. But how lofty do we think this vileness of himself made him? I know not whether any virtue in Antony, however lofty, exalted him before God as much as that casting-down of himself, and that annulling of all the merits which he had had before. And so he believed that he had not yet found Christ, whom he beheld dwelling so excellently in Paul. Behold how much the finding of Paul, and the knowledge of his sanctity, conferred upon Antony. So the bride believed that she had not found the beloved, while by searching out she came to know the virtues of the perfect. Whom [0415B] she seeks also not only in the more perfect, but in her equals, or even in her inferiors. Since indeed these too can likewise be called streets and broad ways, the good, namely, and the middling. In these she explores and considers the gift of some grace which they have specially received, and venerates it, and strives to imitate it. For he who fears God neglects nothing; he holds nothing to be despised whereby he may receive an advancement of salvation. By how much therefore she is great, she humbles herself in all things, not refusing to be amended even by the lowest. For in these she sometimes finds examples of imitation and of advancing, which she may profitably set before herself. For good men are wont to kindle their virtues in turn, and the one to take from the other advancement and the incitements of humility [0415C] alike. Whence it comes about that, while this one considers that one's patience, and that one this one's obedience; this one that one's charity, and that one this one's cleanness, always the one takes from the other whereby he may grow into good. For they are much girded to the zeal of amendment when they see in others the good which they themselves have less. And while in turn they think each other their superiors, and the one reveres and loves the other's good, by loving it he makes it his own, and keeps himself in humility. So Paul did not attend to it that he had labored in preaching more than all the apostles, but looks upon the innocence of others, and because he himself had persecuted the Church of God, he calls himself the least of the apostles [n: (II Cor. XV)]. But Peter does not consider that he is the first of the apostles, but wonders at the wisdom in Paul, [0415D] and in a certain place commends this concerning him [n: (II Petr. III)]. For neither was Peter less than Paul, who bore him up with praises; nor was Paul less than the apostles, who judged himself the least. But it was necessary that they who were great before God should seem small to themselves. And they who were adorned with all the virtues ought to be graced also with the virtue of humility. For it is the greatest beauty of the soul, if after the perfection of all the virtues it possess humility, and, being of great merit, know not that it is great. Blessed Job knew not that he was great, who said: *If I shall be simple, of this very thing shall my soul be ignorant [n: (Job IX)]*. For he esteemed his own goods small, because he was always fervent toward greater ones. And so the perfect, that is, the truly humble, know and judge their own reprehensible things, and, dissembling the evils of others, [0416A] rather weigh their goods, being strict judges of themselves, but merciful of others: whence, when they behold some small goods in others, they hope for greater in them. But when they themselves have done many things, they reckon themselves useless servants. And so it comes about that they think well of others, but grow cheap to themselves, and believe themselves inferior to their lesser ones. So the bride, not considering her own merits, nor setting them before her eyes, fixes her consideration upon the grace conferred on others. She rejoices together with those receiving the gifts of grace, she rejoices together with God the bestower, she is reminded of the beloved through his members, and through his members is the more inflamed toward the beloved. And when she considers the gifts of his liberality, which he has bestowed on divers persons, she kindles her own desires in herself anew: whence she repeats her devout complaint, saying: *I sought and I found not.*



## CHAPTER IV. *How the soul knows the presence of the angels, and lays open her desires to them.*

[0416B]

*The watchmen who keep the city found me [n: (Cant. III)]*. The soul thus desiring, thus seeking, is found by the watchmen who keep the city, namely the holy angels, who keep the city, that is, the Church or the faithful, and by whose keeping the mercy of Christ fortifies its own. And they are rightly called watchmen, because they watch and are solicitous about the elect, that they may be defended from temptation, that they may make progress in good, and that they may be saved. For they are ministering spirits, sent forth in ministry for the sake of those who receive the inheritance of salvation. By their ministry and vigilance God protects his faithful; he that keepeth Israel does not slumber, neither does he sleep. Our adversary, [0416C] however, goes about like a lion, seeking whom he may devour. Some he devours as a lion, whom he casts down by open battle, and others he seduces by hidden fraud; for these, as is read in Job, he hides a snare in the earth, and sets a trap upon the path [n: (Job I)]; because he both deceives them through earthly desires, and sets there the nooses of his deception where he sees the path of their heart to be, and sees them prone to sinning. Thus the enemy goes about to deceive. But the holy angels run to and fro to defend. This discord is carried on between the angels of peace and the angels of discord. Thus is the contest waged for man, and he does not watch for himself, but grows torpid. For it behooves him to see, to watch and to pray, both to see and to consider his own peril, to watch over his own salvation, [0416D] and to pray and implore help against temptation. For how many does that lion devour, how many does the destroying angel put to death, of those who do not have the sign of the living God on their foreheads, who do not have both their doorposts and the lintel of the house anointed with the blood of the Lamb of God; who do not have faith working through love, and do not deserve to be fortified and defended by the banner of the cross of Christ or by the power of his passion. Nor does Leviathan wonder if he swallow these down: for he shall swallow up a river, and shall not wonder, the evil, that is, and the carnal, who flow away after the manner of water and run down from sin to sin, and by the slipperiness of vices descend to the depths of torments. Even, alas! he has confidence that the Jordan may flow into his mouth, that is, the elect, over whom he trusts to prevail. O how great [0417A] a multitude of the swallowed, and how small a number of the rescued. But the merciful Lord hides his own in his tabernacle (that is, in the Church) in the day of evils, and protects them in the hidden place of his tabernacle (that is, in Christ), who is called the hidden place of the Church, because he dwells secretly and invisibly in his elect through charity; inasmuch indeed as it is joined to them, and they are protected through faith and through the power of his passion from the tempter, the devil. For because Christ by his passion conquered the enemy, by the power of that same passion he fortifies his own, that they may not fear the foe, since they are made members of him who triumphed over him. God is indeed faithful, who does not suffer his own to be tempted above that which they can bear, but with the temptation makes also an issue. So ordering and tempering temptations, [0417B] that from the very thing by which the enemy seeks to cast them down they may be tried, and being tried may the more be crowned. It happens besides that, in assailing them (so far as to separate them from God), he rather joins them to God, because, while they are tempted, they are more humbly made subject to God that they may be delivered from temptation. Whence blessed Job says that the beasts of the earth (namely, the demons) are made peace-making toward the good [n: (Job V)]. He does not say *pacatae*, because they do not have peace, but *pacificatae*, because they make peace for them while by assailing them they afflict them. God therefore turns to a crown for his own that which the enemy works for their ruin. But he does not so reckon it, but his heart is set on crushing, and on the destruction of not a few nations. Thus God hides the elect in the Church, in the day of evils he protects them in himself, he fortifies them by the keeping of angels. These he shows forth [0417C] to his own as ministers and messengers, that they may minister to them for salvation, and announce their necessities, and carry their prayers. And although he himself sees and knows the causes of every one, he yet wills that this be announced to him through his angels, so that he may commend his charity and his condescension toward man, and for the sake of messengers so worthy and so beloved may bestow more fully.

Nor is it a wonder that he shows forth his angels as ministers to the elect, and himself as well. For he too is himself the angel of great counsel, namely of our redemption and salvation, which he was sent to work in the midst of the earth. He ministers to us indeed by his manner of life and his humility, offering in himself an example of living, and setting himself as a little one in the midst of the disciples (that is, as humble), that we may be made little ones as he is. He ministered [0417D] to us also by his death, in which he labored for us that we might not labor, and bore a temporal death that we might not bear an eternal one. Therefore the Lord ministered to us in this life, and passing over out of this life also he will minister to us in that refreshment of twofold sweetness, because he will feed us with the milk of his humanity and the honey of his divinity. The ministry of the angels too is not only temporal to us, but also everlasting, because through their present help we shall receive the inheritance and eternal salvation, and shall enjoy their joy with them without end. But how do we suppose they love our salvation, and how greatly do they desire to have us as partners with them? Who could estimate with how great charity and care they watch over those committed to them? how they rouse the torpid, and kindle the more the solicitous and [0418A] fervent? How on this side they excuse evils, on that present good things to the divine gaze; how they defend, or obtain grace for these? And when they have looked upon a soul fervent with great desire, and sighing to God with pure intention, how do we suppose they love her, and rejoice together with her, and turn aside to her? How do they run to and fro between her and God? Since indeed they are the friends of the Bridegroom, they listen to her voice, which they make the Bridegroom hear; her voices are her desires: these cry out vehemently in the ear of the Bridegroom, these the friends listen to, namely the angels; in these they delight, these they announce, and they invite her to come, they console her, and admonish her to seek and to knock, for the reason that he who seeks finds, and to him who knocks it is opened. [0418B] And so meanwhile they frequent and visit the soul thus fervent, until the Bridegroom comes, and by the increase of a fuller grace they prepare her more fully for the coming of the Bridegroom himself. They draw her thought to understand their presence and to learn their familiarity, so far that from this acquaintance she may grow and advance to the divine. The soul therefore, seeking God, is found by these, and after the circuit of the city, after her search, she merits the coming of the blessed angels, she perceives their meeting, by these she is received; for these come before the Bridegroom, they show forth their presence, they reveal themselves: for they are angels of light, they come with light; and being suffused with it, she is at once illumined and touched, that she may understand their coming and feel their presence. And while she perceives this grace, she confesses that she has been found [0418C] by the watchmen, although she complains that she has not found the beloved. And so for the soul to have been found by the watchmen is for her to have found grace to feel their presence, she who before, suffering the darkness of her own blindness, felt nothing but herself. Then she directs her desire and her thought upon them, and forms a familiar colloquy as it were with acquaintances, she insinuates her vows, announces her love, asks about the Bridegroom. Have you seen him whom my soul loves? She seeks them as witnesses of her love, and equally of the longed-for presence of the Bridegroom. For certainly she knows that the angels of God enjoy his vision, and are not ignorant of her love and her desires, and she wonders and reckons it as it were undue, that they should see God and leave her destitute of that vision, she who would say: Have you not already long since seen him whom you have the desire [0418D] to enjoy, and in him my desires? The beloved declares that he stands at the door of the soul and knocks; and if any one shall open to him, he will come in to him, and will sup with him. I have already openly opened my door through desire of him, and he does not enter. Moreover, I have entered in to him: and I knock at his door, which he promises will be opened to him who knocks. And forgetful of both promises, he neither enters into mine nor opens his to me. I stand, and he does not look upon me; I knock, and he does not open; I cry out, and he does not answer; I seek him, and I do not find him. Whither then has he gone? Have you not seen him, you who are angels always seeing him, since you always see the face, and he and the Father are one, and he who sees the one sees also the other? Therefore him who does not enter my door, nor open his own, and [0419A] accordingly is found neither with me nor with himself, I shall seek with you, of you I shall inquire, to you I shall pass over, that with you I may find him whom my soul loves, the Bridegroom of the Church, etc.



## CHAPTER V. *How the soul, after the contemplation of the angels, passes over to the contemplation of God and to discretion.*

*When I had passed by them a little, I found him whom my soul loves [n: (Cant. III)]*. In his threefold creation God can be searched out and known: in the world and in the things created for its adornment, in man, and in the angel. These the soul that thirsts for God goes round one by one, and passes by, that she may find him whom she seeks. From the visible appearance of the world, once beheld, she considers the invisible Creator. For these things, when beheld, give her to know that the Lord himself [0419B] is God, and that he made this world, and not the world itself. But this vision is common to all, that is, to the evil as much as to the good. For in this manner all men see God, as is read in Job, so that every one beholds him afar off [n: (Job XXXVI)], because God is seen outwardly, and not in himself. In this manner the philosophers saw him, who nevertheless did not find him through love. Whence from this vision she passes over to man, who also passes by in the image, although he has lost the likeness, and in whom God is more familiarly seen, inasmuch as in the image of his soul, which represents him in itself through reason and understanding. But she does not pass over to all men, but only to the good and the spiritual. For she does not direct herself to those walking the broad way, which leads to death, but to those who straiten themselves for the sake of the [0419C] kingdom of heaven, whom above she calls streets or lanes, and by whom she designates the less perfect or the more perfect. In these she finds not only the image of God, but in part the likeness, in so far as they have advanced in the virtues, or have climbed up to perfection. But because she does not find in these a full likeness (although she finds some), in order that she may comprehend more fully and contemplate more nearly, she passes over to that which contemplates more nearly, namely to the angelic creation, in which she considers the likeness fully, inasmuch as it is the seal of the likeness, and in which the likeness signed from the beginning perseveres. Men, however good, however holy, nevertheless see in the night, that is, in obscurity, but the angel at noonday, that is, in full knowledge. For as [0419D] blessed Job says, the stars are darkened with the gloom of this night [n: (Job III)], because even those who shine with virtues and with holiness of life are darkened by the shadows of human blindness. The angel is suffused with the light of perfect contemplation. For he is the brightness of the eternal light and the mirror without stain of the divine majesty, who, as he is in no part distant from God, so seeing God perfectly and as he is, represents him also in himself, since he is a clean and transparent mirror to him who directs the eyes of his heart upon him, eyes cleansed, however, more fully from sins and vices, and even from the images of them. For vices darken, and their images also beat back, and hinder contemplation and hide knowledge: he therefore who looks into this mirror, so as to consider the cleanness of these, sends clean [0420A] eyes of the heart into it. In this mirror the likeness of God shines back to him. For they are clean as that One is clean; they are holy, as the Lord their God is holy. Finally, from that clean one he passes over to the author of cleanness, and from the holy to the Holy of holies, from the contemplating to him who is to be contemplated, and at once he finds the beloved. For these are so near to him that there is scarcely any middle between these and him, and after these there is nothing to find but him. Therefore, the beloved having been found, congratulating the angels and calling to mind the benefit obtained through them, she says accordingly: *When I had passed by them a little, I found him whom my soul loves.* Because straightway, as soon as I passed these by, I found the beloved; these I passed by that I might come, and through these I passed over that I might find: for by their meeting and their help I obtained what I longed for, [0420B] who both provoked and kindled my desire, and obtained the meeting of the beloved. The finding of the Bridegroom, therefore, is the experience and revelation of his presence.

To the soul that seeks, indeed, and does not cease from her intention, he shows himself at last and comes to meet her vows. And with him present the soul is made new, and, as it were cleaving to him, she feels the sweetness of an inward taste, spiritual understanding, the illumination of faith, the increase of hope, the incitement of charity, the affection of compassion, the zeal of justice, the delight of the virtues; she has in prayer a familiar colloquy with God, feeling that she is heard and for the most part graciously heard, speaking face to face with God, and hearing what the Lord God speaks in her, in prayer constraining God, and at times convincing him. Illumined by this [0420C] grace, she begins to see the darkness of her own heart, to know herself, to discern in what manner vices hide themselves under the appearance of virtues, or secretly ally themselves to them. For a new day arises in the mind after the night of ignorance, and there comes the daybreak in which she rises to offer the holocaust of contrition for each of the virtues, lest perchance they have been imperfect, or less pure, and to burn up whatever of the carnal has been mingled in them. For the spirit of man is at times carried toward another good to which something carnal is annexed, which delights his humanity and secretly draws him, but he does not know it. Again, how often does someone believe himself to be moved by the spirit to do something, when he is moved by carnality, or by natural things, or by levity, or by some cause [0420D] or occasion. Finally, at times a man does something before the time, or too late, or without discretion. These shadows and hidden evils she detects and judges, and the daybreak of compunction shows the man to himself, namely that he is less in good than he believed, that he has received only the beginning of grace, that he has lain in the night, that he has come to daybreak [sic: *diliculum*], and not to the full day. Therefore, just as, being illumined through the grace received, he knows himself, so through that same grace he understands that what he has found is but little. Elias, after he had heard the whistling of a light air, in which he learned that the Lord was, covered his face with his cloak, and stood in the mouth of the cave [n: (III Reg. XIX)]. So a man, grace having been perceived and, through it, known in his perfection [cj: *in perfectione*; read *imperfectione*, "his imperfection"], covers his face with his cloak; because he recognizes with how great ignorance he is covered, and he stands in the mouth of the cave, [0421A] because after the night of his blindness now known he desires to go out from the vice of corruption and to come to the day of grace that is dawning upon him. The slack and the blind man neither sees himself nor loves grace, because he is wrapped in his own shadows, and is ignorant of the light of grace. The devout soul, illumined by grace, just as she sees herself, so, thinking little of herself, is humbled; and just as she understands grace, so she is the more inflamed to desire it. Humbled therefore, and desiring, and pleading with herself about her poverty, she recalls that she has found the beloved a little, not perfectly, nor to her vow.



## CHAPTER VI. *How the soul receives Christ into herself, and on the true love of God.*

*I held him, nor will I let him go, until I bring him into the house of my mother, and into the chamber of her that bore [0421B] me [n: (Cant. III)]*. The soul, although she has received this grace, is nevertheless not forgetful of her imperfection, nor overmuch confident of the gift of God, and she recalls that she has many times been carnally affected by her own will and borne by the spirit, that she has not yet perfectly corrected her manners, nor laid hold of the virtues of Christ. She desires therefore that her effect be purged, her will mortified, her own spirit fail, and that she put on Christ, as the Apostle says: *Put on the Lord Jesus Christ [n: (Rom. XIII)]*. For although she has found the beloved, she does not yet bring him into her lodging, and what she has received was as it were outside, not within, in desire and not in effect. She understands that she has been visited and consoled, but that she is not yet such a one with whom the beloved would tarry, and have a dwelling until she perfectly fail from herself. To [0421C] this therefore she asks to be helped, and to advance so far that Christ may be formed in her, and that she may now not herself live, but that Christ may live in her. But until she comes to this state, she declares that she will not rest, nor cease from insistence in prayers, saying: *I held him, nor will I let him go,* etc. God is held by the devotion of prayer, by desire, by importunity, by memory, by entreaty, by faith and by the expectation of being heard, and he is not let go if there be no ceasing from the intention, nor any further turning of the face to other things. And so she holds the beloved, although it be the dawn, and she does not let him go until he shall have blessed her. She wrestled with him all the night in which she slept, but her heart kept watch, because, that is, she ceased from outward occupation and labored in seeking the beloved. Also, [0421D] although through the night she has come to the dawn, she does not cease from the wrestling, namely from insistence in prayer, nor does she let the beloved go unless he give a blessing, that she may go from virtue to virtue, and see the God of gods in Sion, that is, in beholding through a mirror and in a riddle him whom she cannot see as he is while she lives in the body. This blessing she seeks, and the changing of her name, that she may now be called not Jacob, but Israel, and that she may have no more need to supplant vices. For Jacob is interpreted *supplanter*, but that, the vices being overcome and driven out, she may enjoy the purity of the virtues and contemplate God; because Israel is interpreted *seeing God*. For to purity of heart God is promised as to be lived [cj: *vivendus*; read *videndus*, "to be seen"], as it is written: *Blessed are the clean of heart, for they shall see God [n: (Matth. V)]*. This blessing [0422A] having been received, she will limp on one foot, because she who walked with double desires, feeling indeed carnal things but consenting to spiritual, will now, the vices being more perfectly mortified and carnality withering away, be borne by spiritual desires only. Sighing therefore for this purity and perfection, she says: *I held him, nor will I let him go, until I bring him into the house of my mother.* Our mother is the grace of the Spirit, which spiritually regenerates us. Whose house is the human mind, in which that same grace is received. Into this house she desires to bring the beloved, that as she has found him, so she may abide and dwell with him. For she desires to retain in herself the grace received, to pour it into her manners, and to be transformed in her manner of life. Christ abides in that soul which has possessed his virtues, [0422B] humility, meekness, patience, obedience and charity. And likewise she who keeps his commandments, and as he walked so she also walks: *If any one loves me,* he says, *he will keep my commandments [var: Vulg. *sermonem meum servabit*, "will keep my word"], and my Father will love him, and we will come to him, and will make our dwelling with him [n: (Joan. XIV)]*. Such a one therefore both truly loves, and brings Christ into the house of the mind. That affectionate love, by which we said above that the love of the soul is signified, at times more affects him who loves less and is less more perfect. For each one does not love so much as he feels this affection, and so much as in that state he seems to himself to love, but so much as he has been grounded in the virtues and in charity, and is held faithful in keeping the commandments. A sweet affection toward God is in a certain way carnal, [0422C] and deceptive, and at times rather of humanity than of grace, of the heart than of the spirit, of sensuality than of reason; so that it sometimes goes rather toward the lesser good, and less toward the greater, and toward something which has more savor than is expedient. By this affection the disciples went astray, and loved God carnally, whose presence they were unwilling to be without. Whence they were even charged with not loving; what of it, that they embraced what delighted rather than what was expedient. Thus some carnal and imperfect person is at times affectionately affected toward God, not because he greatly loves, but because he tastes the sweetness of grace, which helps just so long as it lasts; as long as the sweetness lasts, so long the love also lasts. But a friend will not be known in good things. For in that day the Lord [0422D] indeed commands his mercy, but in the night of temptations and labors he declares in his precepts how much each one loves. By the visitation of grace God consoles our faintheartedness, helps our infirmity, arouses our will. And what wonder if the infirm man be anointed with grace, when even the evil man confesses to the Lord when he has done well to him? And so it happens that the fainthearted man, greedy of love, is more moved by this love, and he in whom sensuality and carnal appetite rather than reason bears rule. At times he feels it the more who is light of heart and poor in grace: for he is more easily moved who has been the lighter, and one who is without consolation receives more delightfully what is offered. The cause therefore of this sweet affection is at times not an abundance of grace, but a poverty of mind. For small things gladden [0423A] the poor man. Not every one who says: Lord, Lord, shall enter into the kingdom of heaven. Not every one, because again and again he says: Lord, sweetly (namely, affectionately) shall enter, but he who shall have done the will of the Father, who shall have kept the commandments. *Call me and I will answer thee [var: Vulg. *Vocabis me, et ego respondebo tibi*, "Thou shalt call me, and I will answer thee"],* says blessed Job [n: (Job XIV)]. God calls through visiting grace, and man will answer through the fulfilling of the commandments; the calling does not make perfect, but obliges; the answer through the commandments justifies. For sometimes this sweet affection comes from an evil spirit, so that while too much is believed of it, and while it greatly delights it is clung to, a man is brought to weakness of heart. Again, so that through occupation with it he may be called away from more useful work. And again, so that someone, relying on the abundance of it, may believe himself perfect, and be less exercised toward the perfect. [0423B] Sometimes too a man of himself abuses grace, by receiving it in vain, or by omitting some more useful work, or by setting in it the end of his own advancement. For as the spirit of the prophets is recorded to be subject to the prophets, so this grace is made subject to man, that he may use it either well or ill: through it therefore he offends who does not know its use, just as virtue too is turned into [sic: *id*] vice, if it be exercised too much, or out of season. The bride therefore sought the offence [cj: *delictum*; read *dilectum*, "the beloved"] affectionately and likewise longingly, but when he was found she was no less solicitous to bring him in to herself. For a fuller grace is to be sought diligently, and there must be no ceasing, but progress must be made through it, until one comes to [sic: *ac*] perfection. *Before I eat I sigh,* says blessed Job [n: (Job III)]. [0423C] Before anyone sighs he does not eat, because unless he desire and seek this grace, he does not receive its refreshment. Nor, unless he advance through it, does he attain to perfection. This grace is food, because it refreshes; it is light, because it illumines. For it reveals to a man his own evils, and strengthens him that he may be able to correct them; his own peril having been known, he receives it, who neglects to correct himself through it and to advance to inward things. But the fervent soul, lest she receive it in vain, labors always to make progress, and diligently tills her own field by bettering her manners and by cultivating the virtues, that she may build the house of the mind into which she may bring the beloved.



## CHAPTER VII. *How in secret the mind enjoys God, and is instructed by him concerning hidden things.*

[0423D]

But it does not suffice the bride to have brought the beloved into the house of the mind, unless she bring him also into her chamber, where she may enjoy him more familiarly and more secretly, and be alone with him alone, and converse with him, and, all others being removed, be present at his counsels, know his secrets, learn his hidden purposes, and where God may make manifest to her the uncertain and hidden things of his wisdom. There she desires to hear the hidden word, that is, the inward and hidden inspiration, which in Job is called a hidden word [n: (Job IV)], because it is granted to few, or because it is enjoined silently to the ear of the heart. And this word she is declared to hear in the night, when deep sleep is wont to seize upon men, that is, when, the desires of the flesh being trodden down, [0424A] the stillness of the mind is possessed. In the silence of the night, therefore, when God is secretly with her in her chamber, when all things are pacified and still, when peace is had within from the disturbance of vices, and without from every unquietness and occupation, when the vices are silent, and the memory of all sins, and the outward senses too are lulled to sleep, and the memory of worldly things. And so, when there has been so great stillness, and all things are already silent, then at last the voice of God can be heard, which does not only speak, but whispers, that is, thinly and secretly makes something manifest to the human mind. And not only the whisper of God, but also the veins of his whisper does the soul then receive by stealth, because she understands the subtlety of the divine inspiration, and the origins of the causes by which grace comes to the mind. For in diverse ways [0424B] God visits, because now he pricks with the mind by the desire of divine love, now by the hope and joy of future recompense, now by the delight of some virtue, now by the knowledge of her own imperfection, now by the fear of punishment, and by the recollection of sins, or by the manifestation of some peril or negligence, now by the memory of a good thing, now by the example and grace of some perfect man. In these and other ways grace visits the mind, and not always in the same way, but now in this, now in that. For as in diverse persons there are diverse gifts, and distributions of the Spirit, so that this one receives this grace, that one that: so in one and the same man there are diverse visitations of the Spirit. The soul therefore with whom God whispers in silence knows and discerns these things, and according to the manner in which she is visited [0424C] she conforms and directs herself. For she knows the discretion of grace and its use, she understands too its presence or absence, its coming also and its departure, the causes also of its coming, the motions and thoughts, the forerunners and the heralds of grace. And that humility above all things merits it she has learned by frequent experience, and she understands the manifold and hidden manners, or motions, of humility. Nor is it doubted that she offers a sacrifice pleasing to God, and heaps up grace for herself, as often as she abases and humbles herself for God's sake. In order therefore that she may obtain it more abundantly, she follows the footsteps of humility as much in thought as in work. In mind she abases herself by her own judgment, and outwardly for God's sake she subjects [0424D] herself to men and puts herself after them, she chooses abjection, she makes herself contemptible, that she may be precious with God, she humbles herself that she may be exalted, she rejoices in reproaches; for she knows that for contumely she will receive grace from God, for reviling a blessing. David understood this, who, when Semei cursed him, chose rather to suffer him than to be avenged through his soldier, foreseeing that for that cursing good would be rendered him by the Lord. Thus God gives grace to the humble, but resists the proud. But who could know an old and ingrained pride, who its hidden roots and its subtle thoughts, by which for the most part it creeps in latently, and steals upon those who do not even know it, save when God whispers (that is, secretly reveals it)? Which, the more it is spread abroad in the mind, so much the less is it known, because through it the mind is the more [0425A] blinded. So also in words and in action it lies hid in manifold ways, nor is it known, unless one be instructed by grace to know it. And so pride lies hid in the soul, and the soul herself does not know it; but God sees this, because he beholds hidden things, and therefore he withdraws his grace, or does not grant it. Not only therefore for manifest, but also for hidden, and not for greater only, but also for lesser pride is the mind dried up of grace: *I will make desert,* says the Lord, *the mountains and the hills, and I will dry up all their shoot [var: Vulg. (Isa. 42:15) *gramen*, "grass"] [n: (Psal. XLII)]*; designating by the mountains the greater, the proud, and by the hills the lesser. These mountains are dried up and grow parched, but the valleys, that is, the humble, are watered with the water of grace, and abound with spiritual grain. To understand latently these mountains, and the origins of visiting grace [0425B] and the causes of meriting or demeriting it, is to receive by stealth the veins of the divine whisper. These veins creep silently and with a light murmur, and are not heard by an unquiet mind, and one in which vices, or any disturbances, make an uproar and cry out. And so the understanding of these things is perceived and learned in the chamber (that is, in the secret place of the mind), where all things are shut out but God, where the soul alone whispers with him alone; this is the solitude into which God leads the soul, that he may speak to her heart. There she can hear what the Lord God speaks in her. There he opens to her the inward ears only: for the ears of the heart lie open; the tumult of secular business closes them, and the stillness of secret consideration opens them. Then [0425C] the mind pierces the words of God more keenly, when it refuses to admit to itself the tumult of secular cares. This chamber is a school, where she may learn in silence, where the secrets of the precepts may be taught, where she may be instructed in the knowledge of the divine will, in the knowledge of manners, in the discretion of virtues, in the contemplation of spiritual things. To this chamber the soul ought to withdraw, as Moses to the tabernacle of the covenant, to consult the Lord concerning doubtful matters, concerning the ordering of the house of her mind, concerning the things to be done in it, concerning her own state, concerning the spiritual and bodily necessities of others, concerning the love of God, concerning the love or the defense of justice, concerning the discretion of zeal to be exercised, concerning the time for overlooking excesses, concerning compassion for offenders, concerning strictness. Here she daily examines and searches out [0425D] her inward and outward things, and what her meditation is, or about what it especially is or ought to be; what her speech is, moderate or dissolute, humble or proud; and likewise what her working is, strenuous or slack; finally what the disposition of the whole house is, and what things in it are to be corrected, what to be borne with. But in so far as she comes to purity of conscience, in however great a measure by mortifying her own will she has joined it to the divine, and made herself one with God, in so far does she know spiritual things and understand the will of God. In order therefore that she may perceive this grace, she rightly desires to bring the beloved into the chamber, that is, the secret place of stillness, of meditation, of prayer, where she may be still from outward things, may learn by inward and mystical meditation, and may obtain by prayer.



## CHAPTER VIII. *On the merits and the usefulness of the contemplative life, and when one ought to come to rest.*

[0426A]

*I adjure you, daughters of Jerusalem, by the roes and the harts of the fields, that you stir not up, nor make the beloved to awake, until she herself will [n: (Cant. III)]*. With much labor, insistence and prayer the bride found the beloved, and at last brought him into her chamber. Whence it is fitting that after her weariness she should at last rest, and enjoy the beloved whom she has laid hold of with so many sweats and runnings to and fro, and not easily be torn away from love of him. God therefore pleads on her behalf and admonishes that she be not roused from her sweet sleep (that is, from the stillness of divine love), nor drawn away from the secret of the chamber to the care of governing. For in this chamber she not only enjoys the embraces of the beloved and [0426B] carries through her own affairs, but also attends to the necessities of others. And in diverse ways she here enjoys God, namely now as Bridegroom, now as teacher, now as father; now she entreats him for herself, now by prayers she bends and appeases him for others, now she sleeps in his love, now she contemplates, now she orders and arranges certain things with him, now she hears from him and is taught, now she enjoys his embraces. For she has with him embraces and kisses, she has offspring and breasts, but all these things not material or carnal, but chaste and wholesome. In these embraces God clasps and is clasped, because he first brings in and insinuates his will to the soul with a certain sweetness: and when the soul has been aroused by this charity, while she joins herself to him who calls and draws her, she as it were clasps him who clasps [0426C] her. For in these embraces God clasps first, and thus in turn the soul clasps, because it belongs to the worthier or the more ample lover to clasp first; whence he also first loved us, that we might be able to love him, and he showed forth besides that charity than which no one can show forth a greater or an equal. And so God embraces the soul when he draws her to himself through grace and joins her to himself. He kisses her when he refreshes her with the sweetness of his grace; he makes her fruitful when he increases charity. Her breasts grow fat with milk when she is filled with the sweetness of compassion. There is there a spiritual wedlock, namely a concord and a conjunction of wills. There it is not a carnal coupling that makes the womb fruitful, but the Holy Spirit that impregnates the mind with twin offspring (that is, with a twofold charity), namely the love of God and of the neighbor. [0426D] Hence the soul becomes a wife; she has received the breasts of piety which before she did not have, when she was still a little one, but now she has learned to have compassion on sinners, yet to be indignant in a youthful manner. Here she sleeps with Christ, while she closes her eyes to outward things and knows inward things through contemplation. In which sleep, with Jacob, she sees the angels ascending and descending [n: (Gen. XXVIII)], ascending to the contemplation of God, descending to the visitation of man. She contemplates in the angel this alternation of the vision of God and of the visitation of man, how they both cleave to God and condescend to us, and carry through our cause with God. She contemplates and considers also the souls now reigning with Christ, namely what affection they have toward us, and how they desire to have us as partners [0427A] with them, and bestow upon us their help and intercession, and bear solicitude for us. For through these are the daughters of Jerusalem adjured, that they stir not up the beloved; because, just as they cleave to the love of the Creator and yet bear and carry through the care of man's salvation, so the perfect soul both rests in the love of God and attends to the salvation of her neighbors. She is also adjured through these; because with these and among these she runs to and fro for those to be saved, and prays not only to God, but also to these, and implores their help. To these she announces man's necessity, with these she labors in this business. Well therefore is she adjured through these, inasmuch as they are privy to and witnesses of her charity, and of her industry, and of her necessary occupation about her neighbors. For these are the roes and the harts of the fields (namely the angelic creation, and men) [0427B] whose souls have already passed over to the kingdom of heaven. These roes both seek the heights and look out from afar; because, being lifted up by lofty contemplation, while they behold God, they know in him whatever is done lower down in his creation. The harts too pass through thorn-brakes, and overcome venom. So the souls of the saints, leaping over the thorns of sins, and overcoming the venom of the enemy's suggestions and the malice of the world, have already leapt over to the kingdom.
