And the field of the roes indeed is the happiness of that life. But the field of the harts is the spaciousness of this world. Yet the roes pass over to this field by the condescension of charity, and the harts have leapt over to that one by desire and by faith. These harts, still dwelling in the body, have leapt over thither, because their feet, that is their affections, were standing in [0427C] the courts of Jerusalem. But now in soul they have passed over thither, and afterward they are to come thither in body also. To these the bride too leaps over by contemplation, with these she has carried over with herself the care of her neighbors, with these she pleads their cause inwardly before God. Rightly therefore does the Bridegroom admonish that she be not stirred up, for the reason that she is well occupied about the salvation of her neighbors.

And the carnal solicitude and trouble is indeed good, but the spiritual occupation and care about many is better and more sublime, inasmuch as it is familiar both to God and to the citizens of heaven, and abiding, and more secure. The outward occupation indeed, that is servitude, because bodily labor too is performed in it. Inward solicitude and prayer is a more sublime and worthier office. The former [0427D] serves, while it renders a ministry; the latter obtains with God by merits and by familiarity. The Lord says that the harvest is great, and admonishes that he be entreated to send laborers into it. Which the perfect soul is most of all able to accomplish, who cleaves to God by the familiarity of devotion; she now beseeches God to send laborers into the harvest, now that he grant to this harvest the grace to work usefully. The active man occupies a part of this harvest by outward ministry, but the contemplative occupies the whole harvest by the help of prayer. For there are two lives, the active namely and the contemplative, which are designated by Martha and Mary; Martha labors without, Mary prays within. Tell her, says Martha, that she help me, [n: (Luc. X)]; well does she help her who implores the divine help for her, because in vain would she spend labor, [0428A] plant and water, unless God gave the increase. Magdalene therefore chose the best part, who exercises the grace of contemplation, and yet is occupied in her sister's part, so that she may be crowned also with the reward of the active life, in which by an equal charity she merits an equal reward. Martha labors in one place with the body about certain things, Mary in many places by charity about many things. For in the contemplation and love of God she sees all things, is enlarged to all things, comprehends and embraces all things, so that in comparison with her Martha may be called solicitous about few things. Greatly therefore is this one thing necessary, and deservedly to be preferred, in which the soul both cleaves to God through love and shows charity and solicitude to all. Well therefore does the bride watch in mind for her neighbors, although she sleep, that is [0428B] be still, from outward things in the body; nor ought she to be stirred up that she may awake, that is, watch outwardly, who thus watches inwardly by charity and prayer. And so the bride is well occupied, although she work not outwardly, because she strives at least to obtain for many, nay for all. The Bridegroom therefore forbids the daughters of Jerusalem (namely, the souls desiring to come to the vision of eternal peace) to stir her up and to draw her away to outward occupation. And she is for this reason also called beloved by him, because she rests in his love and carries through the salvation of many; but it is forbidden that she be stirred up until she herself will, for the reason that she is so joined to God and so thoroughly taught in the knowledge of his will, that for going out and returning at the divine judgment, a just and necessary cause urging it, she is prepared, and her charity is prudent and ordered, so that [0428C] she obeys the divine will, so that, according to the saying of the wise man, she has mercy on herself, pleasing God, nay, according to Wisdom itself, she first weeps over her own self. Nor only does she not entangle herself in secular business that she may please God, to whom she has proved herself, but she also withdraws herself from the necessities of her neighbors, by which she knows that she is weakened or entangled away from perfecting; yet she does this not out of unkindness, but out of prudence. Rightly, so long as the imperfect man has but a little of the oil of charity, whereby he himself only may be anointed, and has not yet deserved to be made solid through the inward gifts of grace, he does not wish to be entangled outwardly, nor let him seethe with worldly cares, nor let it befall her, by the wind of human converse and of mutability, to be dried up from that little which he has received. For outward occupation quickly changes and casts down the imperfect, because dying flies, [0428D] that is, worldly thoughts, spoil the sweet ointment of grace less firmly grounded in the soul [n: (Eccle. X)]. He desires therefore to abound first for himself, that so he may be able to pour into others; with this oil he wishes rather to be anointed than to anoint, to pour out of a full vessel rather than to pour away, to flow rather than to flow off. For he who desires without discretion to profit others, and pours out before the time, remains always poor and empty of grace, and hinders his own progress, and profits others less. And so the prudent soul does not wish to be called away from the stillness and silence which cultivates her in justice, because the cultivation of justice requires silence, until that cultivation shall have come to fruit and maturity, namely until she shall have perfectly advanced; and until she be clothed with strength from on high she studies to abide in the city, [0429A] that is, within the cloisters of conscience. For some men affect governing before the time, namely when they are still imperfect, and are drawn rather by carnal appetite than by the instinct of usefulness. But for the soul to feel this appetite and this will is not for her to will it, because that will is of the flesh, not of the spirit, and not her own but another's, if she herself does not love or follow it, but hates and detests it. She does not therefore will this, when carnality wills it. But when, carnality being mortified, she shall have willed only spiritually to profit her neighbors, then at last she is judged to will this; for this will is her own, because she consents to it. But the carnal will is not her own; because, while she strives against it, it is not she herself that works this, but the sin that dwells in her. Until therefore she shall have come to that state, that she will this spiritually [0429B] and not carnally, she ought not to be stirred up, nor taken up for the governing of others. But although the beloved shall have come to this spiritual will, Christ nevertheless admonishes that she be not stirred up until she will, because he knows her to be such that she will will it when it shall be opportune.



## CHAPTER IX. *On the spiritual assent of the soul, and on discretion and fervor.*

*Who is she that goeth up by the desert, like a little rod of smoke from the spices of myrrh and frankincense, and of all the powder of the perfumer? [n: (Cant. III.)]* The bride goes up by the desert, that is, by the lovers of this world, who are a desert, who are deserted by God, who desert him, [0429C] when by their deeds they say to them: *Depart from us, we desire not the knowledge of thy ways [n: (Job XXI)]*. Therefore they are deserted by him, and their house is left to them desert, so that they may not have God as an indweller within themselves. By these the bride goes up, because, dwelling with them in body, in mind she transcends them, seeking the things above; and spurning their lowest delights, she stays among carnal men, and does not imitate their perverse manners and life. The good, whether set far off or near, she heeds; to them she draws near in mind, and is joined to them in thought; she considers their examples, and conforms herself to their manner of life. Thus she is saved from this perverse generation, when she is separated from it, and passes over and transcends to the generation of those who seek the Lord: for it is difficult to be saved among the perverse, unless in this manner they are transcended [0429D] and deserted, because their perverse manner of life draws and attracts, and their conversations and their talk corrupt. For to that which is frequently seen and heard the mind is easily conformed, because, as is commonly said, beside whomever a man sits, from him he also learns. To be set apart, then, from the fellowship of the evil, and to pass over to the good by love and to imitate them, this is to ascend, because it is to be raised on high spiritually. For as much as a man loves the good, so much does he become good, and so much does he ascend into God. The evil descend daily, because they fall headlong from sin into sin, and, being in filth, they grow yet more filthy. Again, the tempest of water, that is, sin, drowns them, and the deep swallows them up, that is, their iniquity enwraps them, and the pit shuts its mouth upon them, that is, the depth of iniquity, [0430A] when not only are they sunk in sins, but they also lose the entrance of confession. But the good ascend, because they go from virtue to virtue, and daily advance in charity, because charity is increased in them through interior goods, through the will and through works. While therefore the bride advances among those deserted by grace, and failing and descending, she goes up by the desert. This world is also called a desert, either because it is deserted and despised, or because it deserts itself and fails in itself. For daily, with time, the joys of time pass away, and as many as the days are, so many joys of the days go by and fail. As many days as a man has received for living, so many are cut away from him from living and from rejoicing; this, then, is for a man to live and to rejoice: to fail from life and joy. And he who enjoys [0430B] a transitory joy is to be said not so much to rejoice as to learn joy [cj: *discere*; read *deserere*, "to forsake"]; because his joy passes and perishes together with time. The world, therefore, is a desert, because it deserts and loses its own joys. Whence, in that the soul perfectly despises these joys and stretches toward the joy above and true, she goes up by the desert. This desert not only deserts its own delights, but is also barren and dries up, and has no fruit; for it cannot bear fruit to Christ, because it cannot receive the Spirit which is of God, because it has not known him, nor does it know him. But the devout soul, while she labors for the true joy, bears fruit in the barren and dry desert, and among things withering and slipping away brings forth fruit that may remain. Therefore, in this desert and in this land desert, pathless and waterless, to bear fruit and to bring forth [0430C] that which remains, this is to go up by the desert. And the bride goes up like a little rod of smoke from the spices of myrrh and frankincense, and of all the powder of the perfumer.

By myrrh we can understand the mortification and the contempt of earthly love or consolation, and by frankincense the desire of the things above and prayer. Every man is bound to this, that he mortify his members which are upon the earth, fornication and uncleanness, evil concupiscence and avarice, and the other deadly things, and likewise that he keep the commandments: for these belong to eternal life, and this is the justice which, if a man do it, he shall live in it. But the soul that wills to climb to a loftier degree [0430D] ought also to beware of the multitude of venial sins and of the lesser concupiscences, that she may deserve to perfect and to receive a fuller grace. For she ought to avoid carnal and vain thoughts, and likewise thoughts of suspicion, of anger and of bitterness, and the like; and earthly consolations too, namely in food and drink, in conversation, in seeing and hearing, in curiosity, in clothing, in sleep, in any liberty whatsoever; because, as much as she mortifies and spurns these delights and refuses to be consoled carnally, so much will she deserve to be consoled spiritually and to be quickened in grace. As much as grace finds the heart empty of these things, so much will it fill. As much as a man despises these falling things and steps over them, so much does he ascend, because so much does he pass from the lowest things to the things above. And so, after the mortification of the greater [0431A] sins, the soul that is advancing, and that wills to climb to the spiritual life, must mortify and tread down the lesser ones also. So blessed Job, after he had girded the loins of the flesh, was bidden also to gird the loins of the mind, that is, evil thoughts, by the Lord [n: (Job XXXVIII)], when he was to have converse with him, and was to make his command manifest to him. With blessed Job, therefore, the devout mind must gird these loins: for this myrrh, that is, mortification, belongs to the advancing or the perfect soul. With this she ought also to burn frankincense, because she ought not only to mortify earthly delights, but also to desire the things above, and to seek them with devout prayers. Assuredly the smoke of mortification rises together with that of desire and of prayer; [0431B] because the one is propped up by the other, so that neither by itself can rise or please God. For we cannot desire the things above unless we despise earthly things, and we do not despise earthly things unless we are drawn by the desire of the things above. For the heart cannot be without delight, but it must therefore be drawn by some delight, because each man's own pleasure draws him: whence, when one delight is withdrawn from it, it is the more easily inclined to another, and because it is empty, it is the more disposed to take hold of something. So much the more, then, does it admit spiritual delight, as it is both without share in earthly consolation and does not desire to be delighted in anything.

Again, as much as we know eternal things, so much do we despise and condemn transitory ones: for this is that precious pearl, and he who finds it willingly forsakes all the things which he had before [0431C] loved. The soul, therefore, burns myrrh when she despises earthly delights; but frankincense, when she desires and seeks the things above. But when with myrrh and frankincense the smoke of all the powder of the perfumer is declared to rise, he does not say "of all the pigment," but "of all the powder of the perfumer." For a pigment [sic: *Pigmen*] is any virtue whatsoever or any work of virtue whatsoever, which is ground into powder when it is subtly examined and discerned, lest anything of evil lie hid in it, or it be done indiscreetly. The perfumer is the man who grinds this powder, that is, who examines his single works; for provision must be made that good things be done without an admixture of evils, because the greater evils slay good things, the lesser stain a man. Manifest evils, therefore, [0431D] must be guarded against. But because we cannot know all things, a general and hidden contrition must be had for these. For sometimes there is some sin in a man which lies hid from him, or which he does not know to be mortal. Let contrition, then, be the first pigment which we make and offer to God, because a general contrition blots out all deadly sins, hidden and manifest, and restores charity, if it has been lost. Whatever we do outside charity will by no means please, nor, however much we grind or crush it, will it be fragrant before God. Therefore, that all our works may be done in charity and that we may grind a powder pleasing to God, let us in the first place be contrite. Then the single works must be examined, alms, vigils, prayers and the other labors of the body: whether they are done purely for God's sake; whether something else is in [0432A] the case, principally or secondarily; whether they are done with discretion and with due measure; whether the end is advancement and perseverance; whether there is the possibility of completing them; whether the time is one at which it is expedient that this be done; whether the manner, by what means and how much and how often, and so forth. Again, when something is to be left off, and when it is to be pressed more vehemently. Again, whether it be done with scandal to others or with loss; whether levity, inconstancy, curiosity drive to it; whether praise is sought, or steals in secretly; whether human delight, whether earthly advantage is to be examined; lastly, whether the virtues are pure, whether they are perfect; whether God is loved above all things; whether anything is set equal to his love or before it; whether love of one's neighbor is had; whether it is shown sincerely for God's sake; whether the friend is loved in God; whether this love is bestowed spiritually and [0432B] not carnally; whether the person is not received; whether the cause is considered.



## CHAPTER IX. *On the spiritual assent of the soul, and on discretion and fervor.* (cont.)

For sometimes a man is moved by love, but by a carnal one, or he is led by a private love, so that it is not purely shown forth in God, or a man loves more that which is less to be loved, or the contrary. Again, it must be seen whether humility is true and not feigned or imperfect; whether a man is so cheap in his own eyes within that he does not bear proudly or impatiently an injury or a contempt inflicted from without; whether he so presses down and humbles himself outwardly that he is not proud within of this, but holds a lowly judgment of himself with himself, and truly feels himself within to be such as he shows himself without. Again, whether he is humble there where humility is honored, and shuns humility there where it is held in contempt. Again, whether he practices humility when he ought freely [0432C] to set himself against injustice, and holds to patience when rather, being impatient of evils, he ought to be kindled with the fire of divine zeal, and to fail, not being able to bear that justice be despised and perversity prevail. For we ought not to endure it if any man devours, if any man is lifted up, if any man strikes us on the face. If he is lifted up and grows insolent against justice, or he who strikes us on the face with reviling, so that we are the cause, in our own fault or vice. If, sinning and buffeted, we bear it, we have no reward; but if not sinning, this is a patience pleasing to God. Again, it must be considered whether a man so shows patience outwardly that he does not grieve within, nor think to repay the evil; and likewise whether in the very hour of the injury received he answers words of impatience or of reviling, [0432D] or after that hour keeps a heart of envy or of rancor. Again, a man ought to judge whether he keeps obedience for some advantage of his own, or for gain, or purely for God's sake and for the sake of the precept; whether he obeys in adversities, whether promptly, whether devoutly. Again, he ought to heed whether he rebukes modestly, whether he corrects temperately, and whether in keeping discipline he keeps gentleness, and avoids vehemence, anger, bitterness, indiscretion. And whether he is perhaps more inclined to rigor than to compassion, and whether he shows remission where rigor is to be exercised: in these things the mind is often deceived, and what it does carnally and viciously it believes to be a work of virtue. Zeal too is false if we avenge our own injury rather than the divine, and if against those who are ungrateful to us or less dear [0433A] we are the more inflamed. Again, the bestowing of alms is less acceptable if a man gives late or grudgingly; but it is stained if it is done with pride or with reviling. Again, a man is deceived if under the occasion of sustaining the body he serves delight, or withdraws from himself the sustenance that is necessary. Again, continence is defrauded if a man is chaste in body and in mind, and yet under the appearance of usefulness or of counsel it is suggested to him that he have familiarity with certain women; for to that to which he is led by a pious intention nature also is prompt and easily acquiesces, and in that business vice secretly joins itself, whereby also by use it brings him to this, that a man does not understand himself, and does rather that which delights than that which is expedient. So it happens that sometimes a man is borne to [0433B] this by a carnal appetite rather than a spiritual one; and from the peril of that temptation each man will be so far free as he shall have been able, with a free and unsaddened mind, to withdraw from this familiarity. Vigils and prayers are useless to thee if thou so practice them that thou sleepest and growest torpid at that time at which thou oughtest to pray and to watch; if thou holdest a certain singularity in them, through which thou omittest the common things, and those to which thou art the more bound: to admonish thy neighbor, to console him who grieves, and the other works of charity which please God. And yet it happens that they are not always done purely in God: for sometimes a man is prompt to these things because there is a laboring under sloth, and he is affected with weariness, and it is delightful to him when he is entangled in outward things, or he pleases the persons to whom he speaks, or he is pleased with himself in these things, and glories in such [0433C] favor. Because upon those who lead even a still life torpor or error sometimes steals, so that either through torpor they become useless, or through too much examination of spiritual things they fall into error.

And likewise in this life a fault is committed, if for the sake of stillness the outward care of one's neighbor is utterly abandoned. For to examine all these works of the virtues subtly, and to reconsider whether they are truly good, is to grind all the pigments into powder. And when the man who is a perfumer mixes this powder with myrrh and frankincense, that is, with mortification and with devotion, he composes a precious spice, which, when it has been burned with the fire of charity in the censer of the heart, will give forth from itself upward a smoke of sweet odor. But this smoke ought to rise like a little rod, which is straight and grows on high; [0433D] so directly and not crookedly ought grace to stretch upward, so that it seeks nothing from the side, nothing carnal or earthly. The more it seeks anything earthly, or is delighted in anything, the less does this smoke rise, because it is drawn crookedly and from the side; whence it is deservedly compared to a little rod, that the swiftness of ascending may be signified: for those rods which thicken as they grow are not drawn too high, but those rods which grow more swiftly and upward are slenderer and smaller. This smoke, therefore, of mortification, of devotion and of discretion ought to rise swiftly after the manner of a little rod, that we may not be slothful in doing good things and in the zeal of advancing, but eager, so that we may not thicken through slowness and idleness, but may grow slender and lofty. And through the fervor of the mind [0434A] let us stretch upward in haste; for in haste must the ascent be made, and manfully must we press on, because if a man tries slackly to ascend upward, he is pressed down by the weight of the body and of human weakness. For the body that is corrupted weighs down the soul, and unless she advances, she must needs fail, because nature draws downward, and always seeks the lowest things. For we see in heavy men that if they wish to climb a tree and to creep upward, whenever they go about the climbing more slackly, they are at once weighed down and fail; but swiftness of climbing and persistence overcomes the heaviness, and carries them through. In this manner those who eagerly and carefully stretch toward what is perfect, and fulfill the commandments, attain the summit of perfection; but the slack fail, and daily become more inclined to a fall. The bride, then, is compared to a little rod [0434B] because of the purity of her intention and the swiftness of her advancing; but because of all the foregoing it is deservedly said of her: Who is she, that is, how much to be marveled at, how worthy of praise, etc.



## CHAPTER X. *On the rest above, and how the perfect desire it.*

*Behold the little bed of Solomon: threescore valiant men go about it, of the most valiant of Israel [n: (Cant. III)]*. Solomon is interpreted *the peaceable one*. And he signifies Christ, who made peace with the things that are in heaven and on earth, who reconciled sinners to the Father, and reformed concord between angels and men, and joined converted sinners to the just. And those whom he here joins in concord, he also joins to himself in the kingdom [0434C] in perpetual charity and peace, and leads them to perpetual rest. Which rest is called a little bed, because in it there is a pausing from all the unquiet and labor of this life; in that rest they pause who here have labored for God: *For there,* as is read in Job, *they that were wearied in strength rest, and they that were once bound, without any trouble [n: (Job III)]* [var: Vulg. has the perfect *requieverunt*, "have rested," and *pariter sine molestia*, "alike without trouble"]. There the eternal rest refreshes and rewards those whom here the labor of the commandments and the advance of the virtues wearied; and those who here were bound with the bonds of human infirmity, there, the bonds being burst, sacrifice to God the victim of praise. These bonds are hunger, thirst, sleep, weariness, and the rest of this kind; all of which will cease there, because they shall not hunger, neither shall they thirst any more, nor will there be labor there, nor sorrow, nor shall this material sun fall upon them to burn them, [0434D] nor yet the heat of temptation: for then already the Spirit says to them that they may rest from their labors and from their temptations. The Spirit, who strengthened them to obey the commandments, then says that they may rest from labor. For those who have entered into that rest have rested from their works and from their labors, as God also from his own, wherein he labored for man and redeemed him. From these labors he rested in himself; for these labors his members rest in him. None ever labored as he did, because there was no sorrow like his sorrow. And according to the multitude of his sorrows the eternal consolations have gladdened his members, because his great sorrows conferred great joys upon his members, his great labor perpetual rest, and his bitter death the sweetness of everlasting life. For these things [0435A] they love him incomparably, and rejoice in his love: for they have him whom they love, they know him and see him, all of which is eternal life. For it is the same thing to rest with him, and to rejoice with him, and to have him; likewise to love is to know, because love is knowledge, and to know is to see. And this is eternal life, to know the Father, the true God, and Jesus Christ whom he sent: this is that beatitude, and these are the dowries of the soul, the knowledge of God, love, and enjoyment. Who could suffice to think upon that mutual love of the Savior and of the saved, and how he loves those whom he redeemed at so precious a price, how he rejoices together with them at their salvation, how they themselves love him, and rest in his love, and rejoice in him through whom they know and always see that they have been redeemed and carried over to rest? And although [0435B] they love him with an eternal charity for their redemption, they are loved by him incomparably more. So greatly did he love his own in the passion, and so great was the fervor of charity in him for redeeming them, that he could have taken away the bitterness of his passion altogether, but he would not, and rather took upon himself a more bitter pain of martyrdom, and would suffer more than no man ever suffered or could suffer, that he might show forth an incomparable charity. Which charity toward their salvation was so great that, when he had redeemed them at the price of his own death, he calls them to mind not so much as redeemed by himself as given to him by the Father, saying in a certain place: *Father, those whom thou hast given me, I will that where I am, there also they be with me [n: (Joan. XVII)]* [var: Vulg. *illi sint mecum*, "they may be with me"]. So greatly does he exult over their redemption that he would not reckon great that which he bore for them, [0435C] but he seems to have those whom he received as given to him rather in the way of a gift than as bought at a price or by recompense. He, therefore, who so loved them in exile, will love them incomparably also in the kingdom; he had also a greater charity toward these than toward his own body, in dying for them; and there also he will have a greater charity toward them than they can have toward him, and he will rejoice more over their salvation than they themselves can rejoice in it. And so he will rejoice over their salvation, and he himself will be their joy; him they will taste, him they will have in memory, him in desire; in these three the soul consists, and these three the Son of God will perfectly fill, who also is the Wisdom of God. For these are the streets of Jerusalem, that is, the broad ways [0435D] of the citizens of Jerusalem, who see the peace of God; these streets are reason, memory, will; and they are broad, because many things flow into these streets, and enter in and go out. For how many things does reason comprehend, how many memory, how many the will? But these streets shall be paved, that is, made level, that is, perfectly filled with pure gold, that is, with divine wisdom and with the enjoyment of God. For God will occupy each of them, will gladden, beatify and perfectly satisfy them. For whatever reason will be able to taste, whatever memory to recall, whatever the will to desire, God will be, and he will fill each of these, because he will be all things in all; and there will be sung to him a canticle of gladness for the happiness of eternal beatitude. There, while the just without end behold the torments of the damned, they grow in the praises of God; and because they discern both in themselves [0436A] the good with which they have been rewarded, and in those others the punishment which they have escaped, there will be thanksgiving and the voice of praise. They will give thanks to God their deliverer, and will love him with perpetual charity, in whom also they will unceasingly look upon the signs of their deliverance, namely the scars of his wounds. And the more they shall remember the perils and the misery which they escaped, the more, exulting, will they say: Our soul hath passed through the torrent; perhaps our soul had passed through the water intolerable. But, praising God, they will add: Blessed be the Lord, who hath not given us to be a prey to their teeth, namely the demons', from whose snare, the snare of hunters, their souls have been snatched away; and the snare is broken, because the temptation has ceased; and they themselves are set free, but the enemies are bound fast with the chains of eternal damnation. [0436B] Whom also they will always see in hell, so that, their torments being beheld, they may always rejoice, and love their deliverer, and give thanks to him: whence the Lord speaks to Job, that the elect receive the devil as an everlasting reward [n: (Job XL)]; and because here he is occupied with them while by tempting he afflicts them, and there while they shall rejoice at the sight of his punishment which they escaped, for all these things there will be thanksgiving, and there will be the voice of praise, because him whom they will love they will praise perpetually. For Jerusalem will praise, that is, *the vision of peace,* that is, all who see that peace will praise the Lord, who is their peace and exultation and eternal beatitude; and in his own peace and concord, and in charity, he so unites and joins his own in the kingdom, that whatever of happiness and of joy one of them shall have, this becomes also, through charity, [0436C] another's, while this one wishes for that one what he himself has, and that one loves this one's good in him as his own in himself. As many, then, as are the elect who will reign with Christ, so many will the rewards be there, and of so many will be the joys of each one who shall be saved. If any man could reckon the number of the citizens above, he would also be able to reckon the joys of each one, because so many are the joys of the elect.



## CHAPTER X. *On the rest above, and how the perfect desire it.* (cont.)

It must now be seen how poured out they are, and how long-lasting. Let us compare the joy of this life with the joy of our homeland. If we double it, if we treble it, however much number can be multiplied by number, we shall not reach the equality of the joy of the blessed; and because here there is a little fire, there we sing [cj: *canimus*; read *caminus*, "a furnace"], as the authority says: The Lord liveth, whose fire is in Sion and his furnace [0436D] in Jerusalem [var: Vulg. *dicit Dominus*, "saith the Lord"]. But neither can we reach its term, because it is infinite, in immensity as in eternity, so far as concerns the comprehension of the present life; so that by no comparison, by no reckoning can we attain to the greatness of this joy or to its long duration. It has been said how manifold this joy is, how great, how lasting; it must now be seen what it is, or in what it consists. Whence it must be known that it is in God and is God: for neither could it be called true and perfect if it were not God, or if it were outside God. And because nothing is true, nothing perfect outside God, nor could anything besides God suffice for the creature made to the likeness of God, or perfectly fill her, this is that full joy which in the Gospel the Lord admonishes us to ask and to seek [0437A] [n: (Joan. XVI)], and which he declares that those who ask shall receive. And this is that which eye hath not seen, nor ear heard, neither hath it entered into the heart of man, which God hath prepared for his own; the small and the great are there, and the servant and the free man from his master [var: Vulg. Job 3:19, *servus liber a domino suo*, "the servant free from his master"]. Those who here served for God's sake have passed from labor to rest, from servitude to liberty, now free from the fear of any punishment or separation, and they possess the fear of reverence and of love. For they enjoy the liberty of the sons of God, with which liberty the Son of God set them free, that they might be truly free. Christ served that servants might be given over to liberty; he took the form of a servant that those might be reformed who had been deformed through sin, and deprived of the likeness of God. He took flesh from us, he gave us his Spirit, and thus, while he took upon himself the servitude of our infirmity [0437B] and conferred the liberty of his own Spirit, he restored us to liberty: for where the Spirit of the Lord is, there is liberty; in this Spirit we call and receive his Father as our Father also; in the soul, then, he has reformed us to his own likeness, in the body he will reform us to the brightness of his own body. This is the liberty of the elect, this their joy; of this liberty they glory, of this glory they rejoice, namely that they enjoy the glory of the sons of God, that they are the sons of God, and therefore heirs, and that they have God as Father and Christ as brother; that they are heirs of God and coheirs of Christ, and that they possess the inheritance of the heavenly Father together with Christ their brother, in which they have him, the firstborn, as partner and fellow. He is also [0437C] the firstborn among many brethren; these Christ has as brethren and coheirs in his own liberty and glory, these he has with him as companions at table. For they shall eat and drink at his table in his kingdom; and the rest of this table, as is read in Job, is full of fatness [n: (Job LVI)]. The rest of this table is the refreshment of that glory which is full of fatness, because it perfectly satisfies, as the Prophet says: *I shall be satisfied when thy glory shall be made manifest [n: (Psal. XVI)]* [var: Vulg. *apparuerit*, "shall appear"]. With that glory we shall be satisfied, and at the same time we shall desire it, and desire will have no failing, nor satiety any loathing, because satiety will accompany desire, and desire satiety: for its immensity will supply the satiety, and the taste of its sweetness the desire. Always, then, will it satisfy, and always will it delight: this is that refreshment, this is [0437D] the rest of that table, full of fatness. For we shall be filled with the fat of the wheat that fell into the earth, that is, with the divinity of our Savior, who took upon himself the failing of our mortality, that he might refresh us with his divinity. Of the torrent, that is, of his transitory and troubled passion, he drank in the way of this world, that he might give us to drink of the torrent of his pleasure in our homeland. He drank the cup of the passion, and prepared for us the fountain of the water of eternal life: these are the delights, that refreshment, that abundance of Jerusalem, that rest and the fatness of that table. That is the torrent of pleasure with which the elect are made drunk, so that they forget, and are without share in all misery, and rejoice without end: this is the beatitude, the rest and the little bed of our peaceable one, where he feeds his own with these delights, and where he lies down at midday. Here the bride has a little bed with [0438A] the Bridegroom in the night, that is, in the blindness of this life; but there at midday, that is, in full charity and knowledge. Here she knows only in part, there she will know as it is and perfectly; there she sleeps and rests in peace without any disturbance, and in the selfsame, that is, in his peace, she is still, who is peace and joy which is the selfsame. And because he is always the same, and is in the same manner, and is never changed, nor is brought to an end, because he has neither change nor shadow of alteration, that fullness and those delights the devout and the valiant, who manfully sweat in the fulfilling of the commandments, circumscribe by thinking and go about by considering. These are designated by the number sixty, which is completed out of ten and six; for by ten the Decalogue, that is, the commandments, is signified; by six the labor which is expended in [0438B] fulfilling those same commandments. These valiant men, then, go about Solomon's little bed with desire; they do not climb into it, or sleep in it: for they can only think something concerning that rest and search it out, but not comprehend it perfectly and as it is. However much they circumscribe of it by thinking, or conjecture concerning its equality and happiness, this is to go around, not to enter into perfect knowledge. Whence, because what they can know of that rest is very little, it is expressed by a diminutive, and it is called not so much a bed as a little bed. They are not able in like manner to go about this bed who still groan for past sins, and who have need to wash with the tears of penitence the bed of sadness and of a sick conscience: for these [0438C] have not a still bed but a troubled one, while conscience troubles them within, and the sad memory of their sins; nor can they so much go about and desire the rewards above as still fear the torments. But when through true penitence they have been cleansed from the filth of their sins, and after a long struggle set free from the passions of the vices, and strengthened through grace, and have passed over from fear and hope to perfect charity, then with enlightened eyes they are able to ascend and to contemplate the things above. Accordingly the valiant, nay rather those who shall be of the most valiant of Israel, that is, the more devout and spiritual, have it to go about Solomon's bed, and are able to accomplish in work whatever they understand in Scripture. But those who fulfill the commandments sluggishly, and live softly and dissolutely, are not [0438D] able to climb to the consideration of this rest, in whom carnal and worldly desires are still strong, namely the appetite of gluttony, the fury of anger, the heat of avarice, the burning of lust, the spite of impatience or of envy. For these are the weaker in God, the less perfectly these things have failed in them. But when these things have been weakened in them by long zeal and labor, and strengthened through spiritual desires, and then, the more they have pressed these down beneath themselves, so much the more are they strong and powerful above, and can go about this little bed. The most valiant of Israel, then, that is, of those who contemplate and seek God with the mind, and whose spiritual desires shall have been the more fervent, go about this little bed. For, kindled with vehement desires, they go round about on every side and seek an entrance, [0439A] that they may both taste this rest and in some measure enter into it. This, then, is their going about, and the course of desire around this bed.



## CHAPTER XI. *On caution in fighting against the enemy, and on the remedy against blindness of heart.*

*All holding swords, and most skilled in wars [n: (Cant. III)]*. The valiant men who go about Solomon's bed now consider the things above and stretch toward them, now below wage war against the adversary of the human race. That they may be able to fight against him, they first take up, according to the Apostle, the armor of God, that is, the virtues; and since they are armed with these and have them all, they take care not to be negligent in any one of them. For they know that if they offend in one, they are made guilty of all, [0439B] and will have none at all, and so will appear to the enemy not armed but naked. For he who lacks any virtue has the vice contrary to it, and so is outside charity, because virtue and vice cannot exist together. And whoever is outside charity will not be able to have strength against the adversary. Then they fortify themselves with justice, and arm themselves as with a breastplate, covered about with which they remain impenetrable against the darts of the enemy. For he who renders to God and to his neighbor what he owes is fortified on every side, and the enemy finds nothing in him to strike. They have also faith in the things that are not seen, as a shield, which is set first against the enemy, although they attack it first; and the enemy strike and assail this the more, the more spiritually they storm them; for while perfect men believe and [0439C] love things future and eternal, they judge and despise things earthly and transitory. Whence, when there is temptation concerning those things which they do not love, they easily conquer the tempting adversary; for in vain is the net cast before the eyes of them that have wings. For they have wings, and are lifted up by two wings, that their manner of life may be in the heavens, and that they may seek those things and rise above earthly ones, namely by meditation and by contemplation. They take up also the hope of salvation as a helmet; for in this they rejoice so much that they not only bear labors willingly, but are delighted in them, and reckon themselves blessed through them, as it is written: *For thou shalt eat the labors of thy hands [n: (Psal. CXXVII)]*, thou art blessed. For truly blessed is he who both believes and loves things to come, and expends works for which he may look [0439D] and hope for them, and is delighted and rejoices in the labors which he performs for their sake. Fortified with these arms, the soldiers of Christ can fight securely and manfully. For while, enlightened through grace, they believe things to come, and perform works for which they may hope for them, they have faith and hope; they have also the virtues and justice. Thus armed, they take up the word of God as a sword, with which both to protect themselves and to strike the enemy: for the word of God is living and effectual, and like a penetrating sword, discerning the thoughts of the evil one. The valiant men of our Solomon, then, all hold swords, because they embrace Holy Scripture and retain it in memory, and cleave to it with the mind, in which they have divers instructions whereby they may know and overcome the ambushes of the crafty enemy. For the reading of Scripture and meditation, [0440A] and the devout chanting of psalmody, strengthen the mind, render the enemy weak, and empty out his strength. And so, while they hold it in memory and fulfill it in work, and through it become most skilled for contending with the enemy, because through the understanding of Scripture they are instructed, and through working they are enlightened, as the Psalmist says: *In the understandings of his hands,* that is, through working, *he brought them back [n: (Psal. LXXVII)]* [var: Vulg. *in intellectu manuum suarum deduxit eos*, "in the understanding of his hands he led them"]. Their first caution is this, that, being about to fight with the enemy, they take up this humility, and do not trust that they can contend by their own strength, but, reckoning themselves weak, commit themselves wholly to the divine protection. For they become strong in this conflict only so far as, for contending with the enemy, they trust not in themselves but in God. For with the enemy of the human race no man [0440B] could contend on equal terms, their strengths being compared the one with the other; whose fortitude is so great that no human strength can be compared to him, because of the many arts of harming which he has, which no man suffices to know.

With divine strength, therefore, must we engage with these, which strength the knowledge of one's own infirmity and humility properly deserves; and humility alone, as is read in the Lives of the Fathers, can escape these snares. So Jonathan, hemmed in by a multitude of enemies, when he knew that he could not resist, fled to divine help and came forth victor [n: (I Reg. XIV)]. So Josaphat, set in a like peril, when he professed himself weak before himself, turned himself wholly to the calling upon God, and prevailed against his enemies [n: (II Par. XVII)]. Each of these triumphed more by humility and prayer than by strength; but with humility [0440C] and prayer the Lord's passion is to be remembered and to be sounded forth with faith. In it the soldiers of Christ receive strength against the enemy; for there the fortitude of God was made weak, that we might be strengthened. There the enemy came forth strong, that he might be made weak; there he prevailed, that he might be cast down. So much, then, are the members of Christ strong in this battle, as they have been able to place their faith in it. In this firmness of faith, on this stone of help, Moses stood, when, his people fighting with the Amalekites, he prayed to the Lord [n: (Exod. XVII)]. And though his hands were heavy before, so great a fortitude thereafter came forth in them that they were not slackened until the enemies were overcome. But his hands in praying were held up by Aaron, who is interpreted *the mountain of fortitude,* [0440D] and by Hur, who is called *fire:* for prayer must be pressed manfully and with fervor, which fervor is understood by this fire. Prayer lifted up by these two, namely by constancy and by devotion, will triumph over the enemy, and will obtain what it asks. Sometimes these soldiers of our peaceable one fight thus with Moses, the throng of good thoughts engaging with the throng of evil or vain ones; sometimes, consolation being withdrawn and they being as it were forsaken, they receive and recover help with Jonathan by praying, and conquer the enemy. For he, forsaken by his companions, with his garments rent, put earth upon his head and prayed; he received back his companions, he slew the enemy. So the soldiers of Christ, in the anguish of temptation, when good thoughts withdraw and are troubled, rend their garments and put earth [0441A] upon their head, while they reckon the virtues in themselves rent and imperfect. And because they are earth and ashes, they turn this over in mind, and by praying and by humbling themselves they recover their spiritual thoughts, those they had before, and overcome the enemy. Of Judas Maccabeus too we read that, having once engaged with the enemy, he was of such constancy and confidence that he crossed over to them before he would permit them to cross over to him [n: (I Machab. III)]. Which, as in him, so in the servants of Christ is a sign of victory: for his enemies held this for a sign, that if Judas should first cross the torrent that lay midway between them, he would come forth victor, and they would not be able to resist him: which was done, and he came forth victor. So it prefigures that the soldiers of Christ will be victors, if, undaunted at obstacles, they are prepared to bear persecutors or adversities [0441B] and constantly pass through them. This Judas, when war pressed on, was strenuous; but in time of peace provident: in war he acted manfully, and when it ceased, or rather was interrupted, he did not grow torpid in idleness, but made arrangement concerning the things to be done; the polluted places (opportunity being taken) he himself cleansed, and renewed the holy things, and rebuilt what had been destroyed. So the soldiers of Christ are taught now to fight bravely with the enemy, and now to cleanse the polluted places of their own mind (which is the house of God), and to build up the virtues, and to renew holy pursuits and pious works. These swords (namely these instructions and examples of Scripture) they hold, and through them they become most skilled for spiritual wars: *And every man's blade upon his thigh, because of fears in the night [n: (Cant. III)]*. In the thigh carnal propagation is noted, [0441C] since by the thigh can be understood carnal motions and the wills of the heart. For from each of these a great propagation is drawn; because as from the flesh divers thoughts are generated, so from the heart many wills proceed.



## CHAPTER XI. *On caution in fighting against the enemy, and on the remedy against blindness of heart.* (cont.)

As much as anyone follows these delights or these wills, so much is he blinded in mind, because the things that are sweet to the flesh are hurtful to the spirit, and the things that give carnal delight make the soul bitter. Earthly delight is that sour grape, and whoever eats it, his teeth are set on edge (that is, spiritual understanding is blunted), and according to Jeremiah his teeth are broken to the number; because according to the number of carnal delights the knowledge of spiritual things is broken and scattered. And as much as a man delights in these things, so much is he blinded [0441D] within, and the night of blindness is begotten in the mind. Which night is the more to be feared, in that even without knowing it we sin in many things, and take delight; for human nature is prone to evil, and offends in many things which it does not know; because, as is read in Job, we dwell in houses of clay, and have an earthly foundation, and are consumed as by a moth, which secretly and without sound consumes a garment [n: (Job IV)]: hence it is that we are the more often dipped in the filth of sins and are made abominable, that is, such garments of ours, that is, our bodies, make us abominable. Again, we often sin, and take delight in what we too little know, and we sin without knowing it; and these delights and sins the Lord knows, although we ourselves are ignorant of them; and therefore he permits us to be blinded. Again, he knows [0442A] the works of individuals, and therefore he brings on night, as is read in Job, that is, he permits blindness to come to pass *(Job XXXIV)* [var: Vulg. Job 34:25 *Novit enim opera eorum, et idcirco inducet noctem*, "For he knoweth their works, and therefore he shall bring night"]. For by sin we are blinded, and being blinded we sin the more, because we do not see in how many things we offend: whence these nights are to be feared, and against these fears of the night the sword of the word of God is to be held upon the thigh of delights and pleasures, not to be laid aside or set far off, so that through it sins may be known and cut away: for the word of God illumines, and gives understanding of them. Which things, if men guard against them too little, will also be the less known. The mind being little by little blinded, they are strengthened even by habit against a man, so that he cannot easily avoid them. There is also another step, effectual and necessary for knowing and repressing these delights, [0442B] namely the sword of the love of God, and that the soul be wounded with charity for him who was wounded through them, and grieve together with him who bore sorrows for her: for he himself was wounded for our offences, and himself bore our sorrows. For these things, O man, let the sword of love wound thee, and let the sword of sorrow pass through thy soul. This sword is twice sharpened: it wounds with love, and thus opens the understanding and illumines the mind. For love begets knowledge and cuts away sins, that thou mayest beware of offending him whom thou lovest. And so upon the thigh of delights, or of carnal pleasures, the soldiers of Christ have the sword of love because of the fears of the night, that is, because of hidden lapses into sin; because if they do not love, then by reason of blindness and ignorance they offend in many things. And again, [0442C] while they are fervent with love, they turn aside the peril of lukewarmness; because, as love illumines, so lukewarmness blinds. For in a slack mind earthly delights grow, and self-wills grow into a wood; because, according to Solomon, briars and thorns fill the field of the slothful man [n: (Prov. XXIV)] [var: Vulg. Prov. 24:31 *urticae*, "nettles"], which, as much as it has borne fruit, so much do they beget a shady grove of these thickets and darken the mind, so that the slack man understands the less in how many things he offends, attends the less to himself, takes the less hold of himself. This night is to be feared, in which a man daily becomes worse, and yet more secure about himself. But those who have had the sword of divine love will shake off this fear, and this fear of thorns and briars will not come upon them; and for the having of it [0442D] the memory of God's benefits avails most, that thou mayest call to mind and think over again that he has made thee and remade thee, that he has done well to thee. Think upon his goodness, his bounty and his liberality: how good he is in himself, how bountiful toward thee, spending himself for thee, how liberal to thee, heaping his gifts upon thee. Thinking on these things thou wilt love him whom thou shalt consider to be such toward thee. And this sword of love will cut off the evils that sprout up, and will render thee secure from this fear of the night.



## CHAPTER XII. *On the virtues and perfection of the soul, on cleanness, desire and discretion.*

*King Solomon made himself a ferculum of the wood of Lebanon [n: (Cant. III)]*. The *ferculum* is the holy soul, who feeds and refreshes. For she refreshes Christ, and refreshes his members [0443A] also. She refreshes Christ, while he takes delight in her good works and zeal and desires, as it is written: My people has sated me with voices, has filled me with sweet odors. For she refreshes his members by example, while by her holy life and manners each is edified and amended. She refreshes by prayer, while she obtains for them from God the grace by which they are consoled and edified. She feeds unto salvation by teaching, while she strengthens them by preaching, and instructs them to savor things eternal and wholesome. She feeds by counsel, while she exhorts to good, and by sweet admonition raises up and restores the sorrowful, and kindles and strengthens the good yet more; she feeds by converse, while she converses of what is good, and, so far as she prevails, takes away occasion for useless speech. She brings forward words of edification into the midst, and furnishes charity [0443B] with matter for good discourse and gives her labor to peace; she lends no ear to a detracting tongue, but scatters it with a sad countenance. Thus the holy soul refreshes both Christ and his members. Whence Christ is said to have made this *ferculum* for himself; and the holy soul is also called a *ferculum*, a *ferculum* from bearing, because she is borne from place to place, wherever the will of him who governs shall have directed her, namely of God who disposes all things, that she may refresh many and instruct them unto salvation. This *ferculum* Christ made of the wood of Lebanon. These are the cedars which grow on Mount Lebanon, and they signify the elect, who grow and bear fruit through Christ and in Christ. For Lebanon, which is called *whitening*, signifies Christ, who came forth of a clean virgin without any stain [0443C] of sin. These woods are fair, tall, incorruptible, sweet-smelling, unharmed by worms, and they signify the saints, who are fair through holy manner of life and through honourable morals; tall, because they stretch upward by desire, and seek the things above; incorruptible, because their charity and holiness so begins here that it is not ended unto eternity, but is perfected in the world to come; sweet-smelling, because they send forth from themselves the odor of good repute, and by holy esteem draw others to good; unharmed by worms they are, because they do not suffer the worms, that is, the demons, to feed upon their inward parts, nor, they themselves being unaware, do they suffer strangers to eat up the strength of their mind, because, taught by many trials, they know their hidden suggestions; of these woods Christ made a *ferculum*, that is, a soul filled with all the fulness of grace, [0443D] so much so that she is abundant in herself, and is able to refresh others and to profit them; he makes, I say, such a soul of the wood of Lebanon, that is, of the example of the saints, when in their life and manner of living he shows her a form which she may imitate; when in the same spirit in which he enlightened them he restores her, and leads her through to the step of perfection; when he builds in her the virtues with which they were adorned; when at last, according to their form and likeness, he shapes and composes her.

The pillars of this soul are the four principal virtues, namely prudence, fortitude, temperance, justice: well is that soul supported which is propped by these, so that she be prudent to know the things that pertain to salvation; strong to accomplish what she has known; temperate, that she may beware of evils, and may do good things [0444A] discreetly, in place and in time. And when she has been such, she will be just, and will hold justice in the fourth place. These pillars are of silver, because they make the soul that is sustained by these virtues splendid both within before God and without before men, and set forth from her a fair appearance. He made the reclining-place of gold. The reclining-place is a pure conscience, in which Christ, the wisdom of God, reclines himself and rests; which is golden, because it is baked out by the fire of tribulation, and purged as gold and proved. For she who is pleasing through innocence of life is made more pleasing and cleaner through the bearing of adversities, and worthy to be God's golden reclining-place. The Jews put wood into his bread, who is the bread of angels and of men; they set wood for him to be racked upon, they did not put wood under him [0444B] to recline upon. This bread was baked out by the passion, and made firm. Suffer with him, holy soul, so that thou mayest be purged and made firm by the fire of tribulation; be not consumed as lead, but be made cleaner as gold. If, scorched by tribulation, thou shalt not have failed, but shalt have profited through patience, thou shalt be gold and God's reclining-place. And he will recline in thee, who had no reclining-place on the cross, and will make a golden one in thee, who himself had none of wood. For this cause he endured the passion, and had no reclining-place: that by his example and love we may suffer, and that through the bearing of adversities we may be so cleansed that he himself may recline and rest in us in this world, and we may rest with him in the world to come. Think upon this charity of his, soul, and bend thyself wholly to his love and good pleasure, that thou mayest spend [0444C] thyself wholly upon him, and bear steadfastly and with a devout mind whatever things shall have been brought upon thee for his sake, that thou mayest in some measure answer to his benefits and become his reclining-place. Therefore, that thou mayest be a golden reclining-place, study to have also the gold of spiritual wisdom; let not thy wisdom be muddy, but golden; not earthly, sensual, devilish, but spiritual and divine. Of this spiritual wisdom the deep of human wisdom says: It is not in me; and the sea, that is, the bitterness of vices, says: It is not with me. This carnal wisdom therefore is to be cast away, and to be exchanged for this spiritual one; and so gold is to be bought, clean, fire-tried, proved, that we may be made rich, and may possess this gold. After spiritual wisdom and the amendment [0444D] of morals, one must come to this virtue of patience, that we may willingly, nay cheerfully, bear whatever adversities, and rejoice in them for Christ's sake. Thus by degrees and little by little Christ advances and cleanses the soul, as much by his own grace as by the example of his gifts, that she may become gold, nay his own golden reclining-place. He makes in her also a purple ascent. He makes an ascent in the soul, when by his grace he makes her desire the things above. But this ascent is purple and red: for such a soul suffers at times revilings and detractions from the wicked, who envy her devotion, for the reason that they themselves neither love the good nor will to do it. And when she hears the revilings of the wicked, she receives from them a certain disturbance of mind and a redness of confusion. Now purple is so red, [0445A] that yet that redness appears scattered and varied: so in the holy and devout soul that redness is varied; because whence she reddens from the hurling of revilings, thence she reddens with charity, wherewith she also burns toward those who revile. Again she reddens from the desire wherewith she is kindled toward things above and eternal: these rednesses are various, and all precious, so that they deservedly adorn the devout soul, and ought to be compared to purple. The middle parts of this soul are paved with charity. These middle parts are works of the class of good things, namely common works, which can be done with both a good and a bad mind, such as are watchings, fastings, prayers and almsgivings, and the rest. These ought to be paved with charity, namely that they be done in charity and with a good intention. And again, that they be so done with brotherly charity that the weak be not [0445B] scandalized or weakened in them, but that these be level (that is, made with such evenness and discretion) that the soul may walk in all of them without complaint, and may know how to practise them opportunely and again to leave them off: well will these middle parts be paved with charity, if they shall have been so ordered. And so Solomon now makes himself a *ferculum* of the wood of Lebanon, and pillars of silver, and a reclining-place of gold, a purple ascent, and paves the middle parts with charity, when he enriches the soul with the fulness of grace and with spiritual banquets, and shapes her from the examples and teaching of the perfect, that she may be able to edify others. When he adorns her with the beauty of virtues and strengthens her with perfection, and when he makes her so clean that he may rest in her and have a reclining-place, and so lofty and precious that through desire of the things above [0445C] and charity toward her neighbours and the bearing of evils she may be compared to purple, and so discreet and companionable in her outward manner of life that she may please the divine gaze, and not offend the sight of her neighbours.



## CHAPTER XIII. *On the manner of making progress, and on the example of Christ, and on the reward.*

*Go forth, and see, daughters of Sion, king Solomon in the diadem wherewith his mother crowned him, in the day of solemnity and of gladness [n: (Cant. III)]* [var: Vulg. Cant. 3:11 closes *desponsationis illius, et laetitiae cordis ejus*, "of his espousals, and of the gladness of his heart"]. It is an exhortation to faithful and devout souls, that they consider and contemplate in mind the beauty and glory of Jesus Christ, which he possesses in heaven [0445D] through the punishment of his martyrdom. These souls are the daughters of Sion, that is, of that city above, who behold God, which is our mother: these have been spiritually born again and regenerated in Christ, to this end, that they may be daughters and heirs of that vision and beatitude: to these it is said that by faith and desire they go forth from earthly things to consider the things above, and to contemplate the diadem of our Solomon, that is, of Christ, who redeemed us and made peace for us. O daughters of Sion, go forth from carnal concupiscence, and from vicious habit, and from blindness of heart, and see, that is, contemplate spiritually, the glory of our king, the felicity of that gladness above, that you may be eager in entering, drawn by that sweetness and delight, so that you may so walk as to go forth from your own selves. For some walk, [0446A] but do not go forth; because now they go forward, now they go backward, now they walk slackly, now crookedly. For sometimes some press on in good works, and also commit evils. For through a certain sloth and blindness of mind they beware of evils too little, while they are zealous to do well, not attending to the fact that good things mingled with evils do not please God. Again, some are too little strong for anything laborious, and from faintheartedness of spirit as well as of body they endure a burden; others think nothing harder to bear than that they can resist their own pleasures, and they take delight in temptations or in concupiscences, resisting their own passions and motions too little; who, as much as they suffer themselves to be overcome in the least things, so much do they become weak against sins and vices or temptations, and insufficient for walking [0446B] and for going forward. Others go forward the less, because they do not walk rightly and discreetly. In certain good works only, and those less useful, they exercise themselves the more, and in others more fitting for their own advancement they neglect themselves; they press on in labors of the body, and neglect the virtues of the soul and godliness, whereas bodily exercise avails to little, and godliness is more necessary, because it avails to all things. Again, because they have not keeping of the heart and circumspection, through this negligence they offend in many things. And again, others are very solicitous about certain small and light matters, and the greater ones they neither beware of nor know, who, swallowing a camel, strain out a gnat. But all these, although they walk, yet remain within themselves; because they do not leave their former manners and their ingrained [0446C] imperfection and torpor, so as to go forth, with all things amended and the eyes of the heart cleansed, to see God, and Solomon crowned with the diadem. First of all, therefore, it is necessary that we so do good that we turn aside from evil; because he does not go forward well who now walks, now falls down. Again, it behooves us to strengthen the weak knees, and to beware of excessive faintheartedness and infirmity; otherwise we shall not go forth from our own selves so as to attain to this vision. Again, we ought to make straight steps for our feet, that we may know the things by which we can most make progress, and may direct thither the steps of the mind; that is, that we press on to correcting ourselves in all things, to cleansing the heart, to increasing charity. If [0446D] we so walk, we shall assuredly go forth from our own selves, and shall be able to see king Solomon, the eyes of the heart being cleansed from former affections and from blindness. For then we shall be able to see him, that is, to contemplate him, through loftiness of mind, shining in the diadem, that is, the aureole, wherewith in this life his mother Judaea crowned him by inflicting martyrdom upon him, through which she fashioned for him such a crown and glory. For because she crowned him with martyrdom in the time of his passion, he himself was crowned with such a diadem in the day of solemnity and of gladness of his glorious Ascension: for indeed a great solemnity it was, when the citizens above ran to meet their king, and the Lord, the Redeemer of men, coming with triumph; a great gladness indeed, both of the Redeemer himself and of those same citizens, for the redeemed: whence [0447A] it is said of him that he caused his citizens to be called together, that they might congratulate him and rejoice together with him over the man found and redeemed, whom he had lost: this is the day of solemnity and of gladness, or of the espousals and of the gladness of his heart. For then he espoused to himself the bride alone, namely the Church of the angels above; then he entered upon those nuptials, and joined himself to her, from which he will return to those nuptials of men at the end of the world, when, namely, he will join this bride to that bride: *In the day of his espousals,* he says, *and of the gladness of his heart.* How great a gladness of heart the Son of God received in that espousal, that is, the joy of his humanity, namely according as he is man, as much as pertains to the glory of the Redeemer, speech does not express, understanding does not grasp. For as many as his redeemed shall be, of so many [0447B] will his gladness be. [sic: *Ex*] As much as each one will rejoice over his own salvation, so much and a greater joy will he have over each one of them. O daughters of Sion, consider this glory and gladness of our king, set this example before you! When you suffer adversities, go forth from your imperfection and impatience, that you may lightly bear the evils brought upon you; see your king, who suffered for you, and through his passion was crowned with glory and honor. And when you suffer anything for godliness' sake, think over again what manner of persecution and contradiction he endured from the ungodly for you. For if God suffered for man, how much more ought man to suffer for himself? Scanty and brief is that which you can suffer, immense that which you await through it. The momentary and light of our tribulation works in us also an eternal [0447C] weight of glory. Think upon the eternal, and you bear the momentary lightly; think upon the weight, that is, the greatness of the glory, and light becomes the tribulation which such a recompense accompanies; while you suffer, gaze upon the crown: for then there is made for you the diadem wherewith you may be crowned in the day of solemnity, which will be concerning you when you go forth out of the body, or of that espousal, when from the nuptials of the Church militant you shall pass over to the nuptials of the Church triumphant. Therefore, O daughters of Sion, prudent virgins, trim your lamps, prepare for yourselves the oil of good works and the light of charity, and so go out to meet the Bridegroom, and the bride! Then you shall be a crown of glory in the hand of the Lord, and a diadem of the kingdom in the hand of your God.



## CHAPTER XIV. *On interior and exterior humility, and on its threefold distinction.*

[0447D]

*Behold thou art fair, my love, behold thou art fair [n: (Cant. IV)]* [var: Vulg. Cant. 4:1 *Quam pulchra es, amica mea, quam pulchra es*, "How fair art thou, my love, how fair art thou"; the wording printed here is that of Cant. 1:15, whose second clause also closes with *es*]. Christ commends in the perfect soul a twofold beauty, namely that she is fair both without through holy manner of life, and within through a simple intention and humility of mind. And so this exterior beauty of the soul is when she lives temperately in all things, when she uses slender food and clothing, when she guards against all things superfluous or blameworthy, and practises all things just and honourable. Again, these things adorn her, when she is composed in work and equally in speech, when she shows herself companionable and lovable toward all, hurting none, circumventing none, having compassion [0448A] on all and succouring all according to her power. And again, when she is prudent, chaste, and mild, and meek, and adorned in all things with the other good qualities, the soul rightly has beauty outwardly when she has honourableness in habit, and gravity in bearing, cheerfulness in countenance, ears that do not itch, eyes not lofty or curious, a tongue instructed, and tempered from light or useless words, and bringing forth things good and wholesome. It also beautifies her when she is not disturbed by anger, or by impatience or rancour, when she does not contend, does not detract, does not judge, nor lends an ear to those who deal in such things, when she converses with all not only without complaint, but even with grace. She is likewise fair, when she is fervent in good work, in God's service, in the occupation of brotherly charity, strenuous, fervent and discreet in all things [0448B] to be done. There is another, interior beauty, when in all things that she does praiseworthily she has a simple eye, that is, intention, neither affecting nor caring to be praised by men, but seeking the praise which is from God alone and desiring only the things above. And so, although she be fair without through works, she is more adorned, and pleases within to the divine gaze, through purity of intention and humility, and through that sense of mind whereby she both does great things and always grows cheap in her own sight. Which cheapness of mind is the more precious, the more it proceeds from the fount of love and the root of fervor. For to one who loves and is devout, what he has never suffices, nor does he reckon great what he has done, because whoever always pants with desire after greater things judges himself needy.

[0448C]There is therefore a humility that is devout, one that is voluntary, one that is constrained. It is constrained when a man, calling back before his eyes his sins, his infirmity and his imperfection, makes his mind, as it were unwilling and holding its own good things rather before its eyes, to fall dumb and to grow cheap in its own sight. In these things, even if it is constrained, yet it is brought to completion, so that the mind attains it in some measure. If, however, through negligence the mastery of constraining should cease, it will at once slip away from it, and will be led back to its former pride without remission. But in some it cannot even be constrained, the mind always stretching upward to high and accustomed things, nor is there anything that can press down and subject the mind through the lowest things of humility. For to the proud humility is high; and if at some time they attempt to climb up to it, they at once slip back. [0448D] To which they therefore do not climb up, because they stretch toward it with a weak desire. For they will and will not, as Solomon says of the slothful man: *The slothful man willeth and willeth not [n: (Prov. XIII)]*. Humility is voluntary when from the leading of reason a man sees how infirm he is and how much a sinner. And that, according to Job, there is no help for him in himself; and the will consents to this reason, and loves this judgment, and chooses to be despised, because it knows that so to know oneself to be is so to be profited. And so willingly he humbles himself, because, being enlightened through grace, he now loves humility as also the other good things. But this humility is not constrained; because, time being now shaken off and the passions of the vices weakened, the soul comes to this liberty of spirit, that the good delights her and is savoury to her.

[0449A]There is likewise another, more sublime humility, which is not constrained, not drawn by the leading of reason, nor only voluntary, but fervent and devout: this the perfect soul has, who is the more humble the more devout she has been, and as much as she grows in spirit or in knowledge, so much does she advance in humility. For true grace does not exalt, but humbles; because it shows a man more fully to himself, and, understanding its sublimity, he sees how scanty is that which he has been able to grasp of it. As much as anyone eats of spiritual banquets, so much also does he hunger: which, because they can never be comprehended to the full, never suffice for one who desires: whence they always humble her, because her delights always abound less than they are desired. And so grace tasted humbles such a soul, and equally the examples of the good [0449B] considered, the consideration of her pilgrimage and of the manifold misery of humanity, the love of the felicity above and the desire of liberty, namely because she will be delivered from the servitude of corruption into the liberty of the glory of the sons of God. Such a soul, purged of earthly delights, knows how to desire only spiritual things, and passes over wholly into the love of them and into the affection of the virtues; the love of Christ humbles her, who was humbled for her; the love of that virtue itself humbles her, which she therefore embraces because Christ especially loved and had it; whence it is also singularly called the virtue of Christ: all humble things please her, because she understands them to be sublime. For she knows that as soon as she is humbled she is exalted within, because she is made like to Christ, who was the least, that is, the most humble, [0449C] in the kingdom of heaven, that is, in the present Church, although he was great and the Son of the Most High. The Spirit of Christ, who kindles in her love of him, instructs her also unto his humility, whereby, as much as she is full, so much does she choose to be abject, and however blessed she is, so poor is she in spirit. For neither can she who has the Spirit of Christ not love what Christ loves and has. And so great is the agreement of the love of Christ and of humility, that whoever has had the one of these is proved necessarily to have the other. And as much as he has had the one, so much may he have the other; so that if thou truly lovest, thou art truly humble; and if thou art less humble, thou art convicted of loving less. The perfect soul therefore, because she loves, is humbled, and on account of this humility, which proceeds from the root of love, she is deservedly called beloved and [0449D] fair. Whose twofold beauty is commended, namely of exterior work and of interior humility.
