And deservedly is he praised who both does great things and thinks humbly of himself; who is not high-minded, but consents to lowly things, and in all things bears a humble mind.



## CHAPTER XV. *How the soul despises earthly things, and knows the things within.*

*Thine eyes are of doves, besides that which lies hid within [n: (Cant. IV)]*. Such a soul has the eyes of doves, because she looks simply upon these earthly things, nor is she snatched toward them by the desire of covetousness, but rather is content with little, does not court what belongs to others, is not carried away by the desire of ambition or of riches, or of any carnal [0450A] delight. The inward sufficiency of the mind makes the outward look pleasing, so that concerning earthly things she has the eyes of doves, just as in those covetous of earthly things the appetite makes the eyes of kites. The care of things does not afflict her, love does not vex her, business does not weary her; she enjoys great stillness and security, while she does not seek these things, does not care for them, does not covet them. For as is read in Job: *Man is born to labor, but the bird to flying [n: (Job V)]* [var: Vulg. Job 5:7 *et avis ad volatum*, "and the bird to fly"]. Because those who are men, that is, human, carnal, that is, taken up with the desire of worldly things, have labor; but the good, while they do not love or desire these things, escape and fly away from their affliction. For the evil this labor is born of covetousness, which is the root of all evils; but the good are born to this flight [0450B] and liberty by the renewing and lifting up of the Holy Spirit. These are they who fly as clouds, and as doves to their windows [n: (Isa. LX)], because they are lifted up to eternal desires, and are carried by no covetousness to the lowest things. Which flight and dove-like look the Holy Spirit works in the soul, who is designated by the dove. This Spirit makes the outward eyes simple, and opens the inward; because he illumines the sense of a man and advances it to understand and comprehend spiritual things and the things above. The eyes therefore of the devout soul are of doves, because her senses have been illumined through the Holy Spirit, and taught, savouring spiritual things. Seeking those things, intent upon them, treading down and despising earthly things. For they savor according to the spirit those things which are wisdom, which is life and peace: this wisdom is life, because it quickens [0450C] the soul; it is peace, because it makes her tranquil, composes, moderates, feeds, gladdens, advances her, makes her blessed. This wisdom is not singular, but also set apart [cj: *sed et segregata*; read *nec segregata*, "nor set apart"], but common; not seeking her own things, but those of others; not crafty, not malevolent, but pure, sweet, lovable, pleasing; not obstinate, but easy to be persuaded; consenting to the good, agreeing with the well-disposed; she lacks the gall of the disturbances and bitternesses which the love of earthly things has in itself. She savors God, while she knows, she is from God, and in God. Whence her eyes, that is, her senses, are called doves', that is, the Holy Spirit, since through him she sees and understands. These doves' eyes, that is, the spiritual senses, this sublimity, this tranquillity of mind is in thee, O soul, besides those things which lie hid [0450D] within. For those things which thou turnest over secretly with Christ in the chamber of the heart, who could set forth outwardly? Who outwardly could understand or express in words that mutual talking together, the boldness of speaking to him, the consolation received from him or the revelation, the unity of minds, the concord of pleasures [cj: *voluptatum*; read *voluntatum*, "of wills"], the mutual love, the zeal for justice and for souls, the desire of the vision and of the enjoyment of God, the weariness of pilgrimage, the longing to be dissolved? Now the sense of such a soul is opened, that she may understand the Scriptures; now she is instructed concerning hidden, or doubtful things, now she is so united to God that she is made one spirit with him; now she is so absorbed by the spirit that she does not recall that she is dwelling in the flesh; now she knows certain secrets of the divine inbreathing which before she did not recognize; but now [0451A] she is so joined to God, and feels God joined to her, and so transfers her whole will into God, that she either understands God's will or bends it to her own. For out of that purity of heart wherewith she agrees with God's will in all things she also understands it, and is worthy to bend it to her own pleasure. Now she so joins God to herself through love and draws him, that she believes she has him as it were alone; now she is so enlarged into him and passes over into him, that in him she embraces every man through charity, and sees all men in the light wherewith she is enlightened, and toward all (because with one and a like charity) she has compassion and condescends. So she directs her intention upon him, that through him she may see and comprehend many as well as few. Such a soul obtains certain things she has asked for on account of the greatness of her faith, certain ones [0451B] on account of her profound humility, certain ones through importunity. Sometimes by devotion, and by the fervor of an immense desire, she constrains God; sometimes she bends him by the composition of words, and by coaxing or careful speech; sometimes, a just cause for mercy having been shown, and by reason, she receives consolation concerning some of her petitions, concerning others a revelation, and now a certain one, now a doubtful one, sometimes she understands that she has been heard, but does not know the manner. So Tobias knew that he had been heard, but concerning the manner it was not revealed to him. For he believed this to be the manner, that he should die, whereas rather it was that he should be enlightened. In certain things she hopes that God will be propitious, in that she feels herself forestalled with consolation in praying, and helped. In certain things to be obtained [0451C] she feels that God must be bent, and beaten upon with long-continued prayers. In certain things she learns by experience that he seeks and desires a man who may set the hope of prayer against his vengeance. But in some things she recognizes that his wrath cannot be resisted, even if it should happen that Moses and Samuel stand in his sight. These and suchlike familiar and secret things of prayer, of meditation and of knowledge lie hid within in the perfect soul in the present. But what things concerning things to come lie hid, they are hidden from the eyes of all living. For it does not yet appear what shall then be; because, when in that life God shall have appeared, she will be like to him, because she will see him as he is. Then she will be perfectly made like to God, to whom now she is made like in part; because, when that which is perfect shall have come, that which is in part shall be done away, and [0451D] then she shall know even as she has been known. Then she shall see how the Father is in the Son, and the Son in the Father, and the Holy Spirit in both: then at last she shall see the holy Trinity in its own glory.



## CHAPTER XVI. *How holy meditations are obtained from Christ through prayer.*

*Thy hair is as flocks of goats, which have come up from mount Galaad [n: (Cant. III)]*. The hair of the bride is holy meditations, which, as hair grows upon the head, so arise in the mind. And this hair is compared to goats on account of certain likenesses. For goats, in searching out pastures, are wont to seek the heights of mountains and of trees, and they also ruminate. So holy [0452A] meditations always stretch toward the heights, and labor to search out and to know spiritual and heavenly things. And so far as they prevail to grasp them, they are eager to ruminate, that is, to go over them again and to think them over afresh, and to call them back up from the belly of the mind to the memory, and to grind and break these small for savor and for edification. Those trees are good men, growing in grace and lofty. While therefore the devout soul attends to these and meditates upon their holiness, and strives to raise herself up to their imitation, she climbs these trees by meditation; and while she loves and considers the vigor of their mind, the vigor of their daily advance and of their spiritual renewal, and passes it over alike into memory and into imitation, she plucks from them, as it were, food whereby she may be nourished. For he who attends to those who do well, and is delighted by their virtues, [0452B] by their devotion and their manner of life, is himself through them stirred up and nourished to the good and the perfect. These goats climb mountains also, while the meditations of the soul look upon the lofty life and manners of the perfect, and desire to ascend to these, and join themselves to good men, and seek out their fellowship, their consolation, their converse, their prayer. So these goats climb trees or mountains, so they always seek the heights, and despising the lowest things they seek and search out the things above, and are delighted by the sight of these, are refreshed by feeding upon them, are renewed by desire, make progress, and stretch upward by their example. They always make progress, because they always attend to good men; they are always fresh, always eager. For a man becomes of such a manner of life [0452C] as those he looks upon, as those he attends to, and as those to whom he joins himself more familiarly. These goats, namely holy meditations, come up from mount Galaad. Mount Galaad signifies Christ: for Galaad is interpreted *a heap of testimony,* and the saints are heaped together in Christ, and are gathered, and have the testimony of Jesus, and of faith in him and of his works, living stones indeed, and belonging to the heavenly building. Mount Galaad is read to have had most abundant pastures, and Christ feeds most abundantly, who not only refreshes by example, but also pours in grace. For none of the saints builds up by example as Christ does, because in none was there such humility, patience and obedience, meekness, charity, and perfection of all the virtues. In him also was all the fullness of grace, and that to such a degree that out of this [0452D] fullness all receive, as many as have grace; this is the mountain of God, a fat mountain, and not only fat in itself, but also making others fat out of itself, nourishing some with the milk of grace, refreshing some through the more abundant sweetness of wisdom more abundant therefore are the pastures on this mountain.

But it must be seen how these goats, namely devout meditations, come up from God. The soul lies down, and is in the depths, when she thinks of earthly and carnal things. But she rises, and ascends on high, when she meditates on spiritual things and turns them over in mind. In carnal thoughts, therefore, we are pressed down and cast down by our very selves; to spiritual things we are lifted up by God, because good meditations are from God and rise in the soul, while carnal and evil ones are from us. For there is not in us, [0453A] that is in our flesh, any good, because the flesh is barren, nor does it bring forth thoughts of this kind, but germinates for us thorns and thistles. Out of the heart also come forth evil thoughts, and suggestions are made besides through evil angels. When therefore the soul is pricked with compunction for past sins, or purposes to correct and to keep herself as to present ones; when she is kindled with delight in some virtue or with zeal for making progress, is moved to compassion for her neighbor, to zeal for justice, to the love or the desire of eternal life; when she is affected with some devotion or spiritual sweetness, when she is illumined, instructed, or roused to some good: such meditations and affections are from Christ, who speaks in the soul a thought of peace, and whose Spirit suggests all these things in secret. When [0453B] therefore things of this kind are felt in the mind, then assuredly the goats come up from mount Galaad, that is, holy meditations arise and rise up from Christ. For he who abides in Christ, and Christ in him, this man bears much fruit of these thoughts; and in whom these meditations are gathered and heaped together, he has testimony that Christ abides in him, and from him he has the assent of these meditations. From Christ therefore they are, and from him they come up, who nourishes them in our heart, and makes them rise up together and ascend. Who now suggests them of himself, and now is to be prayed to that he may bestow them: for Christ sleeps at times, so that, his Spirit not speaking but a contrary wind blowing, the little ship of the mind is covered with the waves of temptations [0453C] or of evil thoughts. Whence he must be roused, that he may command these winds and the sea of these disturbances, and make a calm in the mind. And so, when the soul prays and rouses Christ by the insistence of her prayers, spiritual and longed-for thoughts are received. For they come up from Christ roused, and made favorable through prayer. And thou mayest see in what manner, while the soul is insistent in prayers, one after another rises and comes up; and as many motions of praying as there are, so many meditations rise and come up; and when the soul is insistent and multiplies her prayers, these thoughts are multiplied; and at length they are so thickened in the soul that now she is walled about by an immense host of them, she who before seemed alone, and as it were forsaken. Because therefore these meditations are from Christ, and come up in the mind, let the soul come up into this mountain, [0453D] let her abide in Christ, let her call upon Christ. Let her take up the sublime office of praying. Let her go up into the mountain to pray alone, all occupations, that is, being shut out and put away; let the words of Christ abide in her, let her hear his law with the ear of the heart, and keep his commandments, that whatever she shall have willed to ask may be done for her. For he who hears the law, his prayer shall be acceptable; and he who fulfills the commandments shall be heard, and from the commandments fulfilled in work he shall be instructed, because to him who keeps justice God will give wisdom and the acquaintance with spiritual things. And, as Solomon says [n: (Prov. VI)], and according to the Psalmist, from the commandments of God he understands [n: (Psal. CXVIII)]. So from this fat and abundant mountain, the devout soul praying, these goats come up, that is, holy meditations; but by the malignant spirit useless thoughts are suggested and rise up, [0454A] drying up the fatness of devotion, and extinguishing the meditations of good things.

These are that people of the Amalekites. Amalec is interpreted *a people licking:* for they lick and destroy the sweetness of the ointment, they consume the unction of the Holy Spirit. Against these one must fight by prayer with Moses, and the hands must be lifted up together with the heart, that we may pray, and likewise keep the commandments in work; if we so lift up both to God, and are not wearied, we shall overcome [n: (Exod. XVII)]. If we cease, or act more slothfully, Amalec shall overcome, as we read was done there. Sometimes we possess holy meditations in stillness, and without the assault of Amalec; sometimes, when he engages with us, and now prevails, now is overcome, prayer profits, and the hands in praying [0454B] shall have been lifted up more slackly, so that the soul experiences these vicissitudes, so that now she sees herself the higher, now is affected with spiritual delight, now is occupied and pressed by evil thoughts: yet if she does not cease in praying, nor grow weary, there is no doubt but that Amalec is overcome and put to flight. When through our own negligence and the dissolution of the heart we slip away from good thoughts to vain and hurtful ones, and sometimes when the adversary tempts and suggests, it happens at times that, dried up for a long while and destitute of grace and of spiritual thoughts, she does not experience that sweet sweetness; for which she must pray so much the more humbly and insistently, inasmuch as we are ignorant whether we are in any way the cause of this failure. For a man is deprived of grace on account of some hidden sin, or [0454C] on account of ingratitude, namely by working unworthily through it, or by not rendering thanks to the giver, or because he does not seek it as he ought, or because he admits carnal thoughts. When therefore this dryness has come about, and the soul has been made as earth without water, the hands must be spread out to God in prayer; and with Elias, who after the dryness of three years asked for rain [n: (III Reg. XVIII)], one must sit down to prayer, and ask for the rain of grace. He sent the boy again and again, to prove whether his prayers had been heard, and it was not done before the seventh time; so, when the mind has been dried up for a long while from the grace of devotion, one must pray and press on many times over, nor cease from prayer, until the stillness of the mind, which [0454D] is understood through the seven-fold number, and the grace which is sevenfold, may work. For without great insistence and laborious prayer, a mind dried up from grace does not deserve to receive it. But she is not able to obtain that rest by devout prayer, since she is destitute of devotion and of those sweet affections; but it is necessary that she make recompense by labor and importunity. And so, if not for friendship's sake, yet for importunity's sake she shall receive what she asks. But that her prayers may be heard, it is necessary that she cut off and circumcise what is superfluous of speech, of thought, and also of work, lest she be dissolute in thought, speaking vain, superfluous, or hurtful things. Nor let her be without keeping of the heart, admitting indifferently whatever thoughts, and neither discerning concerning them nor being wary for herself; nor let her even be soft [0455A] and dissolute in work, handling and doing not what is expedient, but what delights. For that mount Carmel, upon which Elias poured forth this prayer and received what he asked, notes this keeping and that circumcision. For Carmel is interpreted *the knowledge of circumcision.* Those who have not known how to circumcise and cut off these things in themselves are not worthy to be heard. They are indeed the mountains of Gelboe, upon which neither dew nor rain of grace descends, because upon it was cast away the shield of the mighty, that is, manly keeping and circumcision. For without keeping neither is any virtue granted, nor is fuller grace acquired. So therefore let the circumspect soul see, and cut off what is superfluous and hurtful; let her press on with prayers again and again, and not cease when at first the little cloud of grace comes up, until at length there break forth and follow [0455B] the immensity of the saving rain, and it fully water and pour into the mind which had long been parched with hurtful dryness and with the failure of grace. Hence therefore let her ask for grace and await it, hence let her prepare herself for it, and consider with what caution she may return to holy meditations: which will come about best in this way, that she run back first of all to those most lately had. For these are received the more easily, the more nearly they have been had; but to the more remote, and to those had further back, the running back is more difficult. In this manner the end of prayer is better than the beginning. So from Christ, who is the beginning and the end, and through Christ, the diligent and solicitous soul will obtain all things, and will make in herself the assent of good meditations.



## CHAPTER XVII. *On the discretion of thoughts, and how one comes to it.*

[0455C]

*Thy teeth are as flocks of the shorn, which have come up from the washing [n: (Cant. IV)]*. The teeth are the examinations of discretion, by which spiritual meditations and the thoughts of the heart are sifted and broken small, and are discerned, whether they be pure and truly good, whether they be from God, or from an evil spirit, or from the human heart. For the evil spirit knows how to suggest certain thoughts which seem spiritual, and the human spirit at times feigns and lies that it is the Holy Spirit, as is read in Job: *Behold, my belly is as new wine [n: (Job XXXII)]*. Which happened also to Elias himself [cj: *Eliae*; read *Eliu*, "Elihu"], who brought forth words through the swelling of his own spirit which he believed himself to speak from the Holy Spirit and from zeal for God. For by the belly is understood the bosom of the heart, by the new wine the fervor of the Holy [0455D] Spirit, by which Eliu in Job supposed himself to be stirred, when yet he was moved by the impulse of his own heart alone. In many such things a man follows the motion of the heart, or even the impulse of a vice, and yet judges himself to be moved by the Holy Spirit. The demons too at times hide evils under the appearance of good, they stir men up to certain good things, and, that they may lead them through to evil, they even announce things to come. For certain things to come they are able to foretell, because at times they know them; certain things they search out through the subtlety of their nature; certain things they learn from the holy angels revealing them; certain things they conjecture from the outcomes of causes, certain from the natural things of man, certain from the disposition of the air. And for a long while they declare some things as they are, that at last they may deceive the credulous [0456A] mind. They deceive also by false devotion, because they produce in the soul certain prayers and meditations, a sweet affection, even tears, that they may lead either the mind into error, or into elation, or the body into weakness. At times they suggest, by a false interpretation, that something of Scripture is to be understood otherwise, or that from it something is to be done otherwise, than the truth holds; they make some men ill solicitous about the salvation of others, whom they stir up and kindle to the conversion or the edification of others set far off, so far as to cast them down from the stillness of the mind and from their own profit. Which business, painting it in the soul as a kind of day, they insinuate as more healthful and more useful than their former occupation.

When therefore something is suggested to us to be done, we ought to weigh whether any indiscretion, [0456B] in some part of itself, mingle itself in, or the guile of the enemy thrust itself forward; and fully to examine it: whether it be done with due fear and caution; whether not so much our own profit as also that of others consist in it; whether human praise or ostentation creep in; whether any vanity or lightness impel; whether it dissent in nothing from Scripture or from the examples of the saints. But whatever is from an evil spirit will be able to be caught out as false in some part, or its peril hangs upon the future and will quickly be laid bare; because the enemy, in uncovering his own fraud, will suggest more openly what he had hidden, whereby he will be known. The Holy Spirit also works in the soul in divers manners, whence his operations must be known and discerned. For at times he converts the mind by fear of punishment, at times he ascends to the love of God, nor yet does he afford much [0456C] knowledge. He moves the mind, and stirs it up to the good and to God, nor yet does he at once make the man perfect. For in these there is not full knowledge, but a beginning and an incitement to the good, or a consolation; because these things one who is weak and imperfect sometimes perceives, and one who attains less to knowledge and to spiritual understanding. But when he shall have made progress in knowledge, and thereafter ascends to the degree of contemplation, if he perceives something of God, and then seems to himself perfectly to comprehend it, he does not understand it as it is in God; whence Elias, who saw the Lord pass by, because he understood him to be incomprehensible, says that the Lord is not present in spirit, and in commotion, and in fire, but in the whistling of a light air; because whatever is able to be perfectly comprehended is not there, but rather there something subtle [0456D] is perceived of God, and that which could not be fully comprehended. To one still beginning and weak grace is given, that being enlightened by it he may know himself, and being strengthened may be able to correct himself, and so may come more fully to acquaintance. For acquaintance of this kind is not attained unless, the manners being bettered, a greater purity of heart be obtained. The more perfectly each man shall have cleansed the eyes of the heart, the more fully will he be able to see God; and the more fully he sees him, the more does he understand him whom he sees to be incomprehensible. Whence he who has made progress in acquaintance perceives perfectly concerning God, and understands whatever he can grasp of him to be a small thing. For the nearer he approaches to wisdom and to acquaintance, the less does that seem to him to be which he can grasp; because, according to Solomon, the more [0457A] a man is made wise, the more does wisdom itself withdraw from him [n: (Eccle. VII)], that is, he understands only this much, how incomprehensible it is. Such knowledge does not press upon the soul, but kindles and humbles her, and shows her own blindness to her more fully. In this each man can know that he savors soundly, and that his eye then sees God, if he himself reproves himself out of humility, and hungers the more for the wisdom he has perceived, and understands that what he has grasped of it, or can grasp, is the least thing. Between the Holy Spirit therefore and his own a man ought carefully to distinguish, and to examine himself, and, when he is moved to some good work, whether some human delight, or a vice, or a temptation impel him to the carrying out of it. For there are in the soul certain impulses from the disposition of the man, or from some cause, [0457B] or occasion, or curiosity, or ambition, or lightness, or vice, or temptation. Those which are from vice or from temptation must be altogether guarded against. Those which are from our humanity and from the rest before named we can tolerate, and use well, namely that we never follow them, but suffer them at our side, for ministry and not for harm, that is to say, that from them we may have a greater eagerness for carrying out that work which we intend to do. For where natural things work together with us without another cause, our spirit is the readier in these, and more eagerly carries out what it intends.

For it must be seen, and altogether guarded against, that no carnal or human delight impel us principally; nor let us follow such an impulse, even if we suffer it, [0457C] but where we know the impulse of the Holy Spirit to be, there let us walk, and let us do what he suggests. Likewise it must be considered that in a soul still weak and imperfect the carnal motions are more prone than the spiritual. Whence when anyone is moved easily to some good, and with a certain lightness of heart, it is to be feared that lightness of this kind comes from carnality, especially if something be annexed there which might be able to delight [sic: *possis*] his humanity. Likewise the eagerness of the heart is suspect, when a man is impelled to something with a certain vehemence and impatience of delay; because the Holy Spirit is modest and tranquil, and his motions are tempered. Lightness of heart, natural kindliness [sic: *benegnitas*], prosperity, or some arisen cause of gladness also at times works together with devotion, and mounts it more fully. [0457D] And likewise a sadder disposition, or some adversity, and a cause of sorrow, presses it down and even hinders it. Whence in prayer some simple man is at times falsely consoled, or distrusts, while according as he feels the eagerness of his spirit he conjectures the signs of being heard, prophesying out of his own heart, and judging that what his own heart speaks the Lord speaks. The enemy also knows how to deceive by false consolations, or by false revelations, him whom he catches credulous in such things. Whence the discretion of these spirits must be known; all which things, while the devout soul judges and examines them, she grinds and breaks small as it were with certain teeth of discretion. These are the teeth of the bride, which are likened to flocks of shorn sheep; the shearings are harmless thoughts, cutting away outward things, [0458A] namely the love of earthly things, and the desire of having the prudence of the world. For the love of earthly things blinds the eyes of the heart, weighs down and presses down the mind, that it may not be able to rise up to the knowledge and discretion of spiritual things. Worldly wisdom also does not grasp God, nor does the animal man perceive the things that are of the Spirit of God. For God has made foolish the wisdom of this world, so that the more a man savors earthly things, the less he savors God; and the more widely the heart is stretched out, the more is it straitened in the love of divine things. For thus the light of outward things and of carnality so beclouds a man that he apprehends the less the purity of discerning spiritual things; and the more he is scattered abroad to many things, the less does he gather himself to the one thing and most necessary. Inwardly also something at times clings [0458B] to the soul whereby the discretion of spiritual things is greatly hindered, namely her own will. The more this seeks its own, the less is it joined to God; and the more it abides in itself, and does not ascend to God, the less does it apprehend the things that are of God. Likewise he who loves his own will is not able to perceive discretion; because, while he follows what delights and not what the truth judges, as he withdraws from the truth, so is he separated from true knowledge, and thus from discretion. This is the fleece which must be cut off, namely at the end of delight, the weight by which, while the soul is warmed and burdened, she is hindered from spiritual things. So the shorn sheep (namely these harmless thoughts) more freely grasp and discern spiritual things, and write wisdom in the emptiness of these. These thoughts come up from the washing, [0458C] that is, from compunction and the tears of penitence. In this washing they are cleansed from the filth alike of sins and of vices, and are unburdened of their weight, that they may be able to come up, and to climb to the acquaintance with spiritual things. For compunction fully consumes sins and vices, and cleanses the soul from all their filth, and at the same time from the dregs of carnality; it opens also the pores of the heart, and pours forth the rains of the virtues, by which she may be able to fly to these things and to be lifted up. The soul, suffused with these, rises as at daybreak to discern her single thoughts, and to see whether any has strayed, or has been impure, or has worked uselessly. So discretion, while after innocence and the casting off of worldly things, and the mortification of her own will, she is cleansed through the grace of compunction, and [0458D] is instructed unto the understanding and examination of spiritual things, is deservedly declared to come up from the washing.



## CHAPTER XVIII. *On the discretion of natural things, and of things of grace, and on the divers motions of the soul.*

*They are all with twin offspring, and there is not a barren one among them [n: (Cant. IV)]*. Great is the virtue of discretion, by which a man searches out in what part he is prone to evil, or disposed to good, and how much the goods of nature and the gifts of grace can support him; he discerns virtues and vices, the motions of the heart also, the causes arising from without or from within, works more perfect or imperfect, the consolation of grace and the knowledge of these things; in short, he is eager to know his inward and outward [0459A] state. For discretion, applied to the word of God, is more piercing than any two-edged sword, reaching unto the division of the soul and the spirit, of the joints also and of the marrow, and a discerner of the thoughts and intentions of the heart: this shows a man how there is a twofold affection in him, namely a carnal and a spiritual; and that, so long as the carnal is not yet tamed, the soul cannot perfectly be delighted in spiritual things; and although she exult in the Lord, yet the flesh cannot exult, according to what the Prophet says: *My heart and my flesh have exulted in the living God [n: (Psal. LXXXIII)]*. So long as a man is so diverse and divided, he does not fully rise up to spiritual joy, nor to the carrying out of any laborious work. For one still imperfect and carnal is without strength for [0459B] any laborious work, for the doing of which he has nothing of his own, that is, in which no will of the heart draws him, nor delight of the flesh, nor any human cause or occasion, with which he very greatly prevails to do spiritual things for which, out of grace alone, he would be less ready. All the works and desires of such a soul are imperfect, because partly she is borne by the impulse of the flesh, partly she is moved spiritually. Of such a one it is to be suspected that in every work in which he is found frequent or ready, or in which he perseveres for a long time, grace alone does not help him, but along with it some human delight. But if the motion of venial sins impelled him more frequently than that of reason or of charity, unless he blotted them out through penitence, [0459C] it would be to be feared that he would quickly fall into a mortal one, and be buried under the sand of such things. Yet so long as a man is not so blinded that he turns aside from charity, what he does will not be fruitless, because whatever is done out of charity does not lose its merit. If finally the enemy stir up one established in charity to some good work, that he may turn him away from a more useful one, or in some way deceive him, even this will be meritorious to the man who does not know it and who intends it well, because it is done out of charity. For so far do the camps of the devil serve God as a demon, with evil intention, stirs a man up to some good. For the enemy does not always assail openly, but at times under the appearance of good joins himself to a man that he may deceive him. The very nature also of man and his disposition is, in some good work, contrary [0459D] and without strength; in another so ready that its eagerness could seem to be devotion: for natural things are exceedingly powerful in man, so that in an imperfect man there proceed from them more frequently the motions of thought, of speech and of work, and likewise of joy or of sadness, of eagerness of heart or of weariness, and of the other affections of the soul. Even in the most devout man, and in one who more perfectly rules all his motions inward and outward, yet the things he does are formed according to his own disposition, and these natural things help or hinder.

For as is commonly said, that which has been in some vessel savors of the vessel; whence it happens that an irascible man, even though he be moved by a good zeal, natural bitterness mingles itself in. If anyone shall have been light or slack more than he ought, the one will pursue rigor strictly, the other [0460A] clemency loosely. Likewise a man sad or rigid from natural disposition shows less kindness; one benevolent and pleasant, less of the rigor of discipline. In these things and the like we must keep ourselves from her who sleeps in our bosom, namely human frailty, so that even the most devout man be wary for himself against those motions by which he feels his nature disposed and prone to evil. All these things discretion knows and judges, and in what manner it must deal with them that they may become meritorious. It understands also in what manner the nature of man, or another cause, helps or hinders the works of the virtues, namely of humility, patience, obedience, meekness, charity, and all the rest. And that a man merits not according as he is ready or slow in any of these, but according [0460B] as he is rooted in the virtues and has an intense charity. For at times, according to the natural things of a man or another cause, one has a greater charity while having less eagerness in some work of virtue, and one having less charity has greater eagerness. For the virtues, since all, are equal, cannot all be exercised equally, because according to natural disposition one man is more prone to exercise this one than that; which yet are all equal, because they proceed from the same root. If therefore anyone be more prone and more disposed to compassion than to patience, yet patience is as perfect in him as compassion, because both virtues proceed from the same charity. For out of as great a charity as he has compassion, out of [0460C] so great is he patient; because from an equal charity, and by both virtues, he merits equally, because an equal charity obtains an equal reward. And although a man exercise this one more laboriously than that, if his motion is as much intended in carrying out this as in that, he merits as much; nay rather, he who strives and labors merits more, because here the battle is greater, and in him charity is the more increased. A like judgment holds concerning devotion; because, if two be in equal charity, and the one have the grace of exercising himself in devotion, while the other labors under temptation, or wages war against some weakness or imperfection, this latter merits as much by the motions and the striving of the battle as the former by devotion and eagerness of mind; nay, he will be able to merit more if he has striven [0460D] lawfully. Paul declares, in these words in which he says, that a man is able to merit most in the labor of temptation, and that in it charity is increased: When I am weak, then am I stronger and powerful [n: (II Cor. XVIII)] [var: Vulg. *cum infirmor, tunc potens sum*, "when I am weak, then am I powerful"]. For vehement weariness and bitterness of heart exercises the virtues, it does not overthrow them. Whether therefore a man labor under sloth, and dryness of heart, and some temptation, or whether he enjoy devotion to the full, he can merit as much in that adverse state as in this prosperous one, if his motion and striving be as much intended in the struggling.

But it happens that a man is moved more in that virtue to which he is better disposed, and so merits more; whence for the weak the state of devotion is for the most part more fruitful, but it belongs to the robust and the constant [0461A] to merit more fully in the adverse state. Yet it happens that the weak man is made equal in the spiritual battle to the strong; because the more without strength he is than he, the more he labors in resisting; whence what the one acquires by virtue, this the other acquires by a greater weariness. But if rather, because both the weak and the strong slip more frequently into venial sins, because he labors alike in resisting, it happens the more that to the punishment owed for venial sins there answers the punishment of resisting, and that punishment blots out punishment. Nevertheless in him who commits fewer venial sins charity often grows the more. Discretion understands all these things, and knows how to use well the adverse state as well as the prosperous. In this manner therefore it makes a twin fruit, while it moderates both states, and profits from it. And likewise, while it both guards against the evil of indiscretion and prudently [0461B] does the good. For if anyone intend well and act less prudently, he offends; because, itself [cj: *se*; read *si*, "if"] thou offer rightly, and divide not rightly, thou hast sinned, says the authority; likewise it makes a twin fruit, while it both rules the thoughts within and prudently disposes the work without. Not only does it make this twofold fruit in itself, it makes it also in others, whom it amends as much by the admonition of prudence as by the example of working rightly. Rightly therefore are these goats (namely discreet examinations) said to have a twin fruit, which both in themselves and in others bear fruit and beget spiritual offspring, and there is not a barren one among them. For no labor of examining these things, no motion of pious intention, nothing finally that is done by charity will be able to be barren, but it is fruitful and meritorious.



## CHAPTER XIX. *On the keeping of speech, and on sweetness.*

[0461C]

*Thy lips are as a scarlet fillet, and thy speech sweet [n: (Cant. IV)]*. The lips of the bride are compared to a fillet, because as a fillet binds together the hair, lest it flow down loosely, so the devout soul binds and restrains her lips, lest they speak evil or useless things, and flow down and be dissolved unto her own ruin or that of others. And this fillet is called scarlet because by scarlet is understood charity, wherewith these lips are bound. For through the love of God and of the neighbor words are contained and repressed, namely that nothing be brought forth which either offends God or harms the neighbor. This *dilectio* may be called *Dei lectio,* a reading of God, or *Dei electio,* a choosing of God. For she reads God in the school [0461D] of the heart; there she learns to know God, to savor God. She also chooses God in love, and God has chosen her, and has chosen her beforehand; whence also before others she reads in him, and learns what she thinks, speaks and does. He who is one Spirit with God, as he does not perceive the things that are foreign to him, so neither does he speak them. He who is allied to goodness is not disjoined from it through hurtful things. He who cleaves to truth is turned away from vanity, because truth and vanity do not dwell together in one dwelling; and whoever has been devout does not flow away through useless words. The lips therefore of the bride, because they compress words lest they flow loosely, are compared to life [cj: *vitae*; read *vittae*, "a fillet"]. But because they are dyed through the love of God, they are likened to scarlet. Fillets are wont, while they bind the hair, also to adorn the head, and to show that very adornment in minds. [0462A] So the keeping and discipline of speaking adorns the mind, which is the head and the chief part of a man, and outwardly shows the soul beautiful. For words loose and ineptly brought forth deform outwardly, and inwardly scatter the rectitude of the mind. For with as many superfluous words as a man uses, in so many ways is he led outside himself, and scattered from his own keeping. But where words shall have been composed and brought forth with discipline, they give testimony of constancy of mind, of prudence and of discretion. The lips therefore of the bride are compared not to any fillet whatever, but to a scarlet one, which is more precious; because so great a discipline of speaking is the more sublime and the more worthy in her, inasmuch as it is not had from human wisdom, or from natural disposition only, but from divine love. But one man is naturally of a good disposition [0462B] and of modest speech, another moderating his words out of natural prudence, according to what Solomon says: *He that moderates his lips is most prudent [n: (Prov. X)]*; but that which is had from grace and charity is more pleasing, and edifies. Whence it is added that the speech of such a soul is sweet. Deservedly is that speech sweet which proceeds from grace and from divine love; whence, because it arises from such a root, it delights outwardly and edifies. What is brought forth out of a dry and empty heart, because it does not pour out of a full one, does not edify or refresh. He who is needy in himself cannot enrich others, nor pour out again that with which he has not been poured in. For he who brings forth words out of a full heart, and fulfills in work what he says, his words have effect, they have savor and are sweet. Deservedly therefore is the speech of a devout soul sweet [0462C] and delights, is pleasing and edifies, because it proceeds from charity and flows forth from the fountain of grace. For she tastes that the Lord is sweet: whence she pours out again the sweetness which she experiences, and belches forth the fullness which she enjoys; for because the heart is filled with spiritual delights, it belches forth outwardly a good word of sweetness, of consolation, of instruction, of admonition; the storehouses of the heart are full of these riches, belching forth from this gift of grace into that, from this spiritual meditation into that, from one fullness into another. Out of the abundance therefore of the heart she has refreshment, out of the taste sweetness. Likewise she has sweet words, because her conscience has been cleansed from the bitterness of vices and from the dregs of carnality. Whence they do not savor of rancor, not [0462D] of indignation, not of anger, not of envy, not of detraction, nor finally of anything that is hurtful, or dishonorable, or of a vice or an imperfection lying hidden in the soul, whence she might bring forth judgment. The words of such a soul have kindness or charity, meekness, patience, humility. In the bitterness of an injury received they keep sweetness, because the soul retains charity. Nor is she only patient, but also kind, so that she grieves more over him who wounds her than over her own wounding, that she may grieve together with him. She is more prone to pardon than to anger, to compassion than to indignation. For as is read in Ecclesiasticus: *In the commandments of God is wasting and death [n: (Eccli. XXVIII)]* [var: Vulg. *tabitudo enim et mors imminent in mandatis ejus*, "for wasting and death threaten in his commandments"], that is, compassion, whereby a man wastes away through compassion over the sins of his neighbors, [0463A] and death, that is, the mortification of vices; because he has mortified these in himself, when provoked he knows not only how to pardon, but even how to suffer together with the other. For while he knows his own misery, and himself seeks mercy from God, that he may be pardoned, he has mercy, that God may have mercy on him: *Remember the testament of the Most High,* says Ecclesiasticus, *and despise the ignorance of thy neighbor [n: (ibid.)]*. The testament of the Most High is, that is, the covenant, that if we forgive, it be forgiven us. He who wills that it be forgiven him is mindful of this testament, and forgives that it may be forgiven him; he also despises the ignorance of his neighbor, that is, he contemns and does not care for the folly of the man who reviles or wounds him. For he is foolish who wounds and does not know what he does, because he harms himself, and renders a service to him whom he wounds. For the fool serves the wise man (namely unto salvation) [0463B] while he afflicts him; and he who does injury holds also the place of a sick man, while he is unable to restrain his own motions; but the patient and long-suffering man bears the manner of one strong and unharmed, while he is not overcome by evil, but overcomes evil in good; he would be overcome if he succumbed through impatience, but he overcomes while he bravely bears; nor does he envy, nor does he answer a banquet [cj: *convivium*; read *convicium*, "a reproach"], nor does he in any way harm the man who has reviled him; therefore he is not overcome by evil, lest he be evil together with him; nor does he answer the fool according to his folly, lest he be made like to him; but by a soft answer he breaks the anger of the man stirred up, and soothes the viper. Such a soul knows how to excuse before herself the man stirred up, and to ascribe what he has said rather to weakness or imperfection than to malice, and to reckon this with herself, that after a little while it will repent him of having so gone beyond bounds in words. [0463C] For she does not attend to the present state, and to the things which he did when stirred up, but rather to his former tranquillity, or even friendship, and that after a little while the man will return to himself. Not only therefore does she set a keeping to her mouth when the sinner stands against her, but at all times she keeps the ways of her words, that she may not offend in her tongue. Because if the vessel of the mouth have not a lid (of silence and of keeping), it will be unclean. And with as many superfluous words as she shall have contracted stains, so much will she dry up of the sweetness of grace. Through the keeping of the mouth therefore she preserves the cleanness of the heart, and out of the cleanness of the heart she forms her words outwardly. Christ therefore commends this keeping in the bride, and compares it to scarlet, because she is informed by charity, and knows how to speak for God's sake and not for human [0463D] praise or seemliness; therefore he proclaims her sweetness, because it does not proceed from flattery, or from a feigned kindness of words, but from grace and purity of heart.



## CHAPTER XX. *On the beauty of the penitent soul, and on humility, and on her hidden merits.*

*Thy cheeks are as a piece of a pomegranate, besides that which lies hidden within [n: (Can. IV)]*. In the cheeks, which he says are as a piece of a pomegranate, shame is designated; because, while we blush, we are red in the cheeks. The pomegranate is red without, and white within, and is full of grains, whence also it is called a granate apple; to which are likened the cheeks of the devout soul, which is red without, that is, blushes at the memory [0464A] and recollection of past sins, at the daily and shamefaced consideration of shameful thoughts or of temptations, at her weakness also and imperfection: these very things she sees unceasingly, that God may not see them, to whom every heart lies open; and she judges all things also, that God may not judge them. For what she shall have had before herself will not be before God, and what she judges, God will not judge, who does not judge twice for the same thing; if only she shall have judged sufficiently her sins and all her reprehensible things, and shall have had before herself the things which displease God and could be objected against her by him, and paints for herself an image of these, and is red for them, that is, blushes; she is displeasing to herself that she may be pleasing to him, she becomes worthless to herself, and appears base in her own eyes; but as much as she blushes for herself, as much as she seems base to herself, so much is she made beautiful before God. For she sets apart [0464B] the useless from the useful, she winnows the chaff and separates it from the grains, and burns these with the fire of confession and of penitence; the grains she hides within by this humility, by this redness of confession and of confusion, and from her sins she is made clean, and comely before God. This redness therefore both consumes sins and adorns the face. This rind of humility guards and preserves good things. For humility is the keeper of the virtues. This redness of the cheeks adorns the soul, kindles God to love of her, provokes him to look upon her. For the heavenly Bridegroom looks upon the shamefacedness and humility of the devout soul; a lowered countenance pleases him, not an upraised one, eyes not lofty but cast down; and so far does he love and gaze upon this redness, that he does not see the redness of sin wherewith she was red like a little worm, but wholly hands it over to oblivion. [0464C] Prudently therefore the bride covers and wipes away this redness with the other, and being made judge of herself for God's sake she exchanges redness for redness, and offers to the divine gaze what is pleasing, and hides what is hurtful. For the lover of men gratefully receives this exchange, who wills not the death of sinners, but their conversion.

Well therefore does the soul divide with God, leaving indeed her own good things to the divine judgment, but herself reproving and judging her evil ones, converting the hurtful redness and the healthful one, and blushing through penitence, she who was red through fault, so that thus she who was defiled may also be beautiful, and there may be comeliness in place of baseness, a sweet odor in place of stench. Whence these cheeks, that is the redness, are compared to a granate apple, because it has redness without, and within [0464D] the whiteness of the grains. But it is not said to be like a whole granate apple, but a piece, which partly hides the grains, partly uncovers them: for although good men hide their virtues, that they may be known only to the Lord, yet sometimes they are in part uncovered, and become known to men. For no devout man can so lie hidden but that sometimes, from some work of virtue, it is caught what he is. Likewise where the granate apple is broken, there the grains are seen, which are not seen where it remains whole; if the rind be opened, even there the whiteness and virtue of the others lying hidden is found. So in a perfect soul the virtues lie hidden, but while she is beaten with adversities, it is uncovered. She is known to have patience, while she bears an injury, and does not envy, nor is overcome by anger; humility, while she bears contempt with an even mind, [0465A] and is not caught striving after exaltation; obedience, by the fulfilling of it in adversities; charity, by the love of enemies; love of God, by the observance of the commandments; compassion for the neighbor, by succor in his need. So by works is perfection proved. And according to Job the bones which had been covered are laid bare [n: (Job XXXIII)], that is, the virtues which had lain hidden are made manifest. The soul has also the redness of the pomegranate in her cheeks, when for Christ's sake she does not flee the blushing of reproach, of poverty, of contumelies, of afflictions and of labors. For in these she is pleased with herself for Christ's sake, and is willingly suffused with this redness for love of him, who in his passion was made red with his own precious blood poured out for her. So after the likeness of the pomegranate good men are red without, but within are white with divers [0465B] gifts of graces. But their affliction is compared not to a whole apple, because they have not borne things like to the passion of Christ, nor were they full of grace as he was, but out of his fullness all have received. For Christ in his suffering can be likened to a pomegranate which is full and whole, because he was full of grace, which was not given to him by measure. And he was wholly red in his punishment, because he bore for us not small things, nor a part of sorrows or of labors, but things so great and so grievous that never was there any sorrow like his sorrow. It was more bitter, and exceeded the sorrows of all men which they were to suffer from the beginning of the world unto its end. But it behooved his sorrow to be the more bitter, that he might bear the sorrows of all, [0465C] because he was a victim sufficient for all, but not effectual. For he did not make all safe, which yet rests upon those very men who are not saved. For from the hour of his birth he labored under want, flight, persecutions, hunger, thirst, many labors and adversities. In his heart also he suffered, because he was incomparably afflicted by this, that he knew the bitterness of his passion before he undergoes it, and knew most certainly that he was to suffer it. In his passion also he was tormented not in a part of the body, but in the whole body. For not only were his limbs transfixed with nails, but the body itself also bore stretchings, punishment and scourges; and the head, besides the blows and the veiling of the eyes, bore also the prickings and wounds of thorns. Because therefore Christ suffered so grievously and in so manifold a way, [0465D] therefore every affliction of the elect, which they have borne for Christ or after Christ's example, can be compared only to a piece; because in respect of those things which he bore, the things which they were able to suffer will be small, and only a slight part of them. Whence while they consider and know these things, they esteem lightly all that they can suffer; and while they think upon his loftiness, and the greatness of the things he bore, and that he suffered this, as blessed Job speaks, without the iniquity of his own hand [n: (Job XVI)], and that he was crushed for our wickednesses, and by his bruise we are healed [n: (Isai. LIII)], they always make little of what they themselves can do. It delights them to bear adversities for him; they are kindled to greater things out of love of him, and always remain humble in themselves; without they are red with labors and afflictions, [0466A] within they are white with virtues; they suffer evils, they do good, and they become worthless to themselves. To a piece of a pomegranate are the cheeks of the devout soul compared. And besides these she has within divers merits and gifts of the Spirit, the perfection of the love of God, and the desire of dying for him.

Which if it is not granted in the body, she makes for herself a martyrdom in the mind. She mortifies carnal appetites, her own will, the vices of the heart, depraved manners; she wrestles with her own flesh, and with the enemy unceasingly assailing it. The martyrdom of abstinence also she has invisibly in the mind, while she represses the earthly delights of the heart, and the motions of sensuality, while she refuses all consolations which delight carnally. She suffers spiritually, while she is fried in the frying-pan of the mind with zeal for souls, while she is tormented over [0466B] the contempt of justice, over the prevailing iniquity of evil men. The martyrdom of passion also she obtains, while she is afflicted for those who sin, while she suffers together with the sick, while she condescends to those laboring under any necessity whatever. Likewise it lies hidden, nor does it appear without, how all her glory is from within, and that she is the daughter of the heavenly King; in what manner she serves him, not with penal fear, but with filial love; that she does the justifications of God, not only for the sake of retribution, but willingly sacrifices to God and confesses, that is, praises him because he is good, loving the good for the good's sake, and is delighted with the good itself and with the good pleasure of God. Finally she glories in God only, not in the world, nor in any thing, without outward or earthly advantage. Likewise she has the testimony of a good conscience: nothing that gnaws at her conscience, [0466C] nothing that she believes, in doing, to be against God, nothing intending otherwise than according to God. In manifold ways also she merits secretly in the heart; because, with God alone as witness, she intends or does many things inwardly. For she merits by the motions of desires, by the contemplation of things above, by compunction of heart, by delight in the love of God, by compassion for the neighbor and by prayer, by the will of accomplishing something which she is not able to do; because before God she nevertheless does as much as she intends; rightly out of as great a charity as she intends, so much does she merit, because she is judged to have done so much. She has also hidden merits in the heart, which although they lie hidden are known by God; because, with him alone as witness and privy to them, many things which are merits are done in secret. [0466D] How many are the ways in which she secretly humbles herself for God's sake in heart or in work, or, being humbled by others, willingly bears it; and many times also, being injured, keeps silence, when she could reasonably answer, lest she desert the virtue of patience? At times also, when the injury shall have been greater and the cause of excuse better, she does not defend or excuse herself, knowing that then virtue the more profits in her, and merit grows. Since her friends are wordy, her eye drops toward God. For these who do injury are friends, because out of them she makes gain, and they do not harm but render service: for because they make a din with words, with the eye of her intention she drops toward God, whom she knows to be the witness of conscience and the rewarder of hidden things. She knows, according to place and time, now to be silent, now to speak, now to set herself against certain [0467A] causes, now to bear, now to do something, now to leave off; wherein she is not led by negligence, but uses discretion and a pure intention. These are the things which lie hidden within, and commend the bride in the eyes of the inward judge, who sees hidden things.



## CHAPTER XXI. *On progress, and on the strength of the good, and on the teachings of Scripture.*

*As the tower of David is thy neck, which is built with bulwarks [n: (Cant. IV)]*. [var: Vulg. *aedificata est*, "has been built"] The neck joins the head and the members. Whence by the neck are signified those who by preaching or by admonition convert others, and join them to Christ the head. These are as the tower of David, strong in grace and lofty before God: for nothing so pleases God as zeal and the gain of souls. [0467B] This tower is a spiritual building, namely the construction and heaping up of good works, the increase of grace, and perfect love [sic: *dilectis*] of God and of neighbor. In these, by the grace of Christ, both preachers and other devout and perfect men are made solid, according to their measure and their capacity, urgently intent upon the conversion of others. This is the tower of David, who is interpreted *strong of hand,* and signifies Christ, who strengthens us, that in Christ we may be able to do all things, and in whom every building, being framed together, grows and advances. This tower is said to be not so much theirs as Christ's, from the fact that it is completed by the power of Christ rather than by their own. Not I, says Paul, but the grace of God with me [n: (I Cor. XV)]. And Job: Behold, there is no help for me in myself [n: (Job VI)]; [0467C] whence elsewhere too he makes plain that he had no confidence in himself, saying: I have despaired [n: (Job VII)]. Who, by this that he despaired of himself, grew strong the better in divine hope. As much as the faithful distrust themselves, so much do they trust in the help of divine hope; and as much as they are made weak in themselves, so much are they strong and mighty in God. For as that which is sown unless it first die, so he who wishes to make progress is not strengthened unless he be first made weak in himself, and be humbled thereby; nor does he advance in God if he does not first fail in himself, and know his own failing. It is impossible therefore that anyone be able to triumph over himself, until he has learned by experience that he cannot obtain the triumph by his own power. And so to those who know their weakness, and who humble themselves thereby, and who do not trust in themselves, Christ affords [0467D] strength, that they may cease to be strong carnally and may grow strong spiritually so, while they despair of themselves and hope in Christ, they change their strength, and take wings as eagles and fly. For he who had been strong in seeking earthly things, let them become strong in desiring the things above and the heavenly: *My soul hath chosen hanging,* says blessed Job, *and my bones death [n: (Job VII)]*. Because while by his intention he hung himself toward the things above, he mortified carnal strength in himself. But this carnal mortification is not obtained at once, nor without labor. For no one becomes highest suddenly, nor is the top of a ladder climbed by flying, but by ascending step by step. It is necessary therefore that everyone making progress strive to conquer himself, and to break his own wills, and to beware not only of greater sins but also of lesser delights. [0468A] For as much as he is conquered by his own will, or by delight, or by carnal consolation, even in the least things, so much does he fall away from strength, and become weak in mind. For by whom a man is overcome, he is made his servant also; and as much as he is overcome, so much is he led into this servitude. Again, as much as he succumbs to the small suggestions of the enemy, so much does he make him strong against himself, as Job says in the person of such a one: He rushed upon me like a giant [n: (Job XVI)], so that now he is not able to resist him to whom, by frequently yielding, he was giving strength against himself. He therefore who by living softly and dissolutely has afforded the enemy strength against himself, let him, conversely, that he may be strengthened against him and resist him, begin from the least things, and often try to prevail in such things, so that by the practice of conquering he may be strengthened, and from the victory over lesser things [0468B] may little by little grow strong for conquering greater. He ought also to accustom himself to decline the delights and comforts of the body, and to break the wills of the heart. As much as he shall have prevailed in these, and shall have broken himself, so much will he be unbroken and strong spiritually. As often therefore as he resists either the carnal motions of the heart or the suggestions of the enemy, so often is strength adjudged to him against vices or temptations. He ought also to shun faint-heartedness and womanish softness, and to accustom himself to fitting labors and afflictions of the body. For if he is conquered by faint-heartedness, he will little by little fail, and will be unnerved from the strength of the spirit.

By these aforesaid means the soul which desires to build in herself the tower of the [0468C] spiritual building ought to grow strong out of weakness, and to pass over into manly strength. And although this tower is called the strong hand of Christ, because it is completed by his help and power, it is nevertheless needful that all a man's industry and labor be applied as well. The perfect have conquered all these things, because they have both compelled the body to serve the spirit, and have subjected the unlawful motions of the heart to reason. To a tower therefore are compared preachers, or other devout men who are eager to attend to the conversion of others, because they who are eager to raise others to the things above and the heavenly ought themselves to be firm and lofty by merit. Now this tower has bulwarks (namely the diverse teachings of Scripture) whereby it may defend itself against enemies, and from which it may assail and strike them. For in Holy Scripture it sees the snares and cunning devices of the enemies by which they deceive men, now [0468D] by open evils, now by hidden ones, now by other and other ways. Whence the Lord in Job declares that the enemy now fights against the soldier of Christ out of a quiver by ambushes, now brandishes a spear before his face [n: (Job XXX)], because some vices he covers over under the appearance of virtues, and others, as they are open, he casts before his eyes; and if he does not deceive a man by open battle, he sets a shield against him, so that if by striking he does not pierce the breast of the one resisting, he may at least block the way of the one making progress by certain objections, and hinder him with alien thoughts or occupations. For he suggests something that may delight a man, or may seem to have usefulness, that he may turn him away from the more useful thing that he was intending to do. But the Lord established the sacred word, in which he uncovers the arguments of the demons, and sets against them [0469A] the remedies by which he may be met. For as many as can be the arguments of his temptations, so many teachings are found in Scripture against them. The Lord himself also, when tempted, answered the enemy out of Scripture. For when he urged him to change stones into loaves, he confounded him out of Scripture [n: (Matth. IV)]. And so when he suggests to us the eating of bread (that is, of earthly delight), let us consider that we are more blessed if we eat the labors of our hands, that is, if out of the tribulation of our labors we are refreshed by the consolation of hope and by joys. For he who understands out of Scripture what reward he awaits for his labor, and that for single labors a reckoning of a thousand years in eternal happiness, and more, corresponds, is rightly refreshed better by his labors than by earthly delight, and eats this the more joyfully. [0469B] Let us therefore call to mind that saying of blessed Job: *His bread in the belly shall be turned into the gall of asps within [n: (Job XX)]*. Because earthly delight, bitter at the end of retribution, is known to have been the persuasion of demons, while those who have fed upon it are led to the bitterness of eternal torment. These two testimonies of Scripture are as it were two bulwarks, by which we may be defended from the enemy, and may conquer him. Again, we are taught out of Scripture that the enemy urges us to cast ourselves down, when he suggests the affecting of miracles. For we read that weakness of conscience is better than the vanity of signs. Paul also teaches us to leave the things that are behind, and to be stretched forth to the things before [n: (Philip. III)], namely to progress toward virtue, not to the glory of miracles. [0469C] For signs are given for the sake of unbelievers, that through them they may be converted to the faith, but the virtues of the soul are required of the faithful [n: (I Cor. XIV)]: for without these there is no salvation. But signs have sometimes been done by unbelievers, or even by the reprobate, as for instance by Alexander of Macedon and Judas the traitor, and by many others, who did these things unto damnation. By some they were done for the faith or the profit of the bystanders. Sometimes too signs were done through demons, as it is written, that there are certain spirits of demons working signs. And so among the faithful, and especially among spiritual men, the signs of this time are deservedly suspect, especially those which have ostentation but no profit, namely those which are said to happen to these women, and which are shown to be done rather by demons [0469D] than by God. For there are in these days some, according to the Apostle, who, giving heed to certain spirits of error, turn away their hearing indeed from the truth, but are turned to fables and to the rumors of new things, because they wonder at and give heed rather to certain miracles, whether done by demons or feigned by certain persons to whom they seem to happen, than to perfection of manners and to virtues. For blessed Gregory says that perfection is not indeed to work miracles, but for each one to love others as himself, to know true things concerning God, and to think better of others than of ourselves.

Again he says that each one is perfect in so far as he perfectly feels the sorrows of others. And again, that he is truly perfect who is not [0470A] impatient toward the imperfection of his neighbor. Charity therefore, humility, patience and the other virtues, and perfect manners, make a man perfect, not miracles. And so salvation is in the virtues; upon these the soul is strengthened and stands firm in miracles she is not made solid or grounded, but is lifted up and raised to no purpose and in vain. Those therefore cast themselves down who affect miracles without cause of profit, and they are suspended to no purpose, they do not stand in the solid and the safe, so that they go backward, not forward, that they might go from virtue to virtue. But on the contrary Scripture teaches us not to tempt God in the showing of miracles, but rather to be eager for the perfection of virtues, and to give labor to the fulfilling of the commandments. The Lord also teaches us by his answer to meet the third temptation, when [0470B] he says: *Thou shalt adore the Lord thy God [n: (Matth. IV)]*. For where God is said to be adored and he despises the kingdoms of the world, he calls forth to the worship of God, and teaches us to seek true riches and to despise the false. For if we worship God and serve him we shall possess unending riches, because for single good works a greater reward will be given us than the kingdom of the world is. Finally, of every single one who shall be saved we shall have a singular joy, and by the abundance of each we shall be more enriched than if we ruled over the whole world. These testimonies and examples of Scripture are bulwarks, by which we may defend ourselves from the enemy, and instruct and kindle others, and cast darts against the enemy; whence the enemy himself strives to hinder us in the understanding [0470C] of Scripture, that we may not advance in the learning of it, staining and darkening the purity of spiritual understanding by earthly thoughts or by carnal knowledge. But when our Redeemer defends us from their temptations, then the mind lays hold of this knowledge, as is read in Job: *The Almighty shall be against thy enemies, and silver shall be heaped up for thee [n: (Job XXII)]*, that is, the charity and purity of the divine word. God therefore, while he instructs his own with the understanding of Holy Scripture and refreshes them with its food, makes them at once wary and strong against temptations, as is rightly read in Job: *In famine he shall deliver thee from death, and in war from the hand of the sword [n: (Job V)]*, so that while they are refreshed by the word of God, they may not be overcome in the war of temptation. As often therefore as we resist the temptations of the enemy, and when [0470D] we confute them, being both taught out of the Scriptures and helped by God, so often indeed do we strike and wound him, and the penalty is turned back upon him which would be inflicted on us if we were conquered by him: these teachings and testimonies of Scripture are the bulwarks with which the soldier of Christ fortifies and protects himself.



## CHAPTER XXII. *On true perfection, and on the examples of the perfect.*

*A thousand shields hang from it [n: (Cant. IV)]*. The shields are the virtues by which we are defended against vices or temptations. But by the number of a thousand the perfection of these same is signified. For if the virtues have been founded in the soul, [d: she perfectly despises herself, and is truly humble, is less hurt or burdened,] the vices or temptations have no strength against her, and he who perfectly [0471A] despises himself, and is truly humble, is less hurt or burdened, if contempt be shown him. For he is not pierced by the dart of pride who, being founded in humility, understands the humility of exaltation, and glories in contempt or abjection, as it is written: *Let the humble glory in his exaltation [n: (Jac. I)]*. For he who loves his enemies has blocked the entrance of envy; because envy does not penetrate a breast which it has found fortified by the perfection of charity, nor will he be moved in any degree by injury who, founded in the virtue of patience, rejoices even in reproaches. Again, he cannot seek his own who through charity desires his own things to be common to others. In the other virtues the soldier of Christ is fortified, that he may be able to meet the contrary vices, and on whatever side they assail him, on that side they are repelled by the virtues, and [0471B] being repelled they leap back. And so a thousand shields hang on this tower, when a man shall have obtained all the virtues and perfectly attained them, so that on no side can a place lie open to the vices and to the tempter. For as much as the vices are untamed in anyone, as the carnal appetites, so much the more powerful are they against him, and the vices have strength to assail him. But when they have been overcome and mortified, and illumined by the contrary virtues, then if perchance they tempt or assail, they at once leap back, and cannot prevail. But he who is weak in the virtues, and has not yet subdued the vices, carries in himself that whereby he may be tempted and be weak. Hence it is that the apostle said that everyone is tempted by his own concupiscence [n: (Jac. I)], namely one still implanted in him, and not fully overcome. For as long as virtue and vice contend in a man, [0471C] and virtue does not obtain a quiet place, it is necessary that the man be shaken and wearied by many temptations, and be now the upper, now the lower. But when he shall have possessed the virtues perfectly, then he perfectly presses down carnality, and overcomes and repels its assaults. And so these are the signs of perfection, if he has so subdued the vices that, when tempted whether by the concupiscence of the flesh, or by gluttony, or by his own will, but [cj: *sed*; read *sive*, "or"] by affection for kinsfolk, or by some carnal motion, he feels himself strong against these, and easily presses them down beneath him. Again, the perfect man is ruled by reason, the imperfect man is often led by sensuality, and is weighed down by it. For the perfect man ought to be head to the members, that is, of sensuality, and to have it beneath him and press it down by reason. [0471D] But that he may rule and press it down, it therefore behooves that it be illumined and strengthened by Christ, that he may be able to know the carnal motions and to bridle them when known. For if Christ be the head of the man, and rule the reason, the man too, that is the reason, will consequently be able to rule his own lower part, namely sensuality, and as much as he shall have received the Spirit of Christ, so much strength will he have for pressing it down. He therefore is perfect who by the strength of the spirit powerfully presses down both the wills of the flesh and the will of the heart. Again, these, namely the signs of perfection, if they proceed from a good conscience, not from a false righteousness or from presumption of faith, make a man have death in desire and life in patience, for the sake of God and of his brother's profit. Because therefore such is the soldier of Christ who has attained the aforesaid virtues [0472A] and perfection, he is compared to a tower which is fortified with a thousand shields. In which tower all the armor of the strong is added to be, the armor of the strong is the lofty working of good men, and the examples of their virtues, with this armor the soldier of Christ is eager to fortify himself, who is eager to reform his own life by their example. In these each one sees what labors of heart and afflictions, and what temptations they bore, how manfully they fought it out, and broke their wills, and labored in fulfilling the commandments. How great was in them the cleanness of life, the purity of heart, and every perfection of virtues. How many there were in the deserts or in monasteries who were never filled with bread, never fully refreshed with sleep, never warmed with garments, never delighted with the sweetness of a bed, [0472B] because, while in these things they were needy and afflicted, they were rich in the perfection of virtues and in the fullness of grace, commanding demons, healing the sick, and dwelling in mind in heaven.
