The examples of all these and the rest are the arms of the strong, which the soldier of Christ has with him, which he uses, with which he is armed and fortified against the enemies of the soul, against the world, against himself. He who shall have these examples with him, who always gives heed to such men, cannot grow lukewarm in idleness, nor perish unarmed and dissolute before the enemy. The evil too have their own arms, namely their members, which they yield as arms of iniquity unto sin. For with their eyes they see to covet evil, they use the tongue for detracting, for reviling, or for speaking vain or useless things; they have itching ears for hearing [0472C] new or hurtful things, their feet run to evil, and their hands work iniquity, whose right hand is a right hand of iniquity. Finally with heart and body alike they war for sin, and through this for the devil; their arms do not fortify, but slay, and the more familiarly one joins himself to such men, the more grievously is he wounded by these arms. For he who associates with the evil and gives heed to them is separated from the good.

For in two ways they do harm, because they corrupt by example, and seduce by false praise. For because they do not know what is good, they proclaim him best who is not, and him good who has been exceedingly evil; and while anyone has been afflicted by their praises, he is little by little deprived of the knowledge of himself, and believes himself to be such as [0472D] he hears himself to be. For he thinks of himself not according to the testimony of his own heart, but according to the judgment of those who suppose this or that of him. Whence very many are moved by a false opinion about themselves. For to the things which are before the eyes the mind is easily inclined, so that it gives heed to them, or thinks according to them. Whence it is necessary that a man rather be eager to look with the eyes of the heart upon the strong and the perfect, and to set their virtues or deeds before himself and give heed to them. If he gives heed to the evil and entrusts himself to their judgment, he will fall and be seduced, and will be rolled down to worse things; if he thinks upon the good, he will be humbled and advanced to good. From the evil therefore, or the dissolute, we ought to be estranged in mind, and to ignore them (although they be placed near us), to turn our gaze away from considering them, but with the eyes of the heart to look upon the good, and to consider their manner of life [0473A] and to be eager for their virtues. Among these we ought to dwell in mind, whose manner of life is in heaven, and so, according to that which is written, to walk with the good. If we are assiduous with such good men, and know them as acquaintances, and join ourselves to them in desire and love, we shall assuredly be made like them, so that with the holy we may be holy, with the robust, perfect. In this way therefore we ought to compare ourselves with them, and to transfer their examples to ourselves, and to place them with us as arms with which we may war spiritually. Thus the soldier of Christ dwells with these, and, as is read in Job, he abides among the stones [n: (Job VIII)], stones assuredly living and belonging to the heavenly structure, firm and squared, their vices and carnality cut and pruned away. With these he walks in heart, [0473B] among these he abides, not among earthly stones, namely carnal men who are of the earth and speak of the earth, and act and think after an earthly manner. Among these he abides who is delighted by their favors or their manners. But the good man abides among the stones with which he has a covenant, as is said to Job: *Thy covenant with the stones of the regions [n: (Job V)]*. Whoever belongs to the building of the city above has a covenant with the stones of the regions, namely that he may imitate the firm and the strong who are or have been throughout the region of the world, and may be made like them. In this tower therefore, namely in the perfect man, is all the armor of the strong, that is, the examples and works of those who manfully contend in God. Their constancy in labors, their patience in adversities, temperance in prosperity, [0473C] charity toward enemies, obedience in hard things, endurance in injuries, devotion of mind, loftiness of contemplation, he places in his memory; these he thinks upon, in these he exercises himself, these he is eager to imitate, and by their example he shows himself devout and strenuous in the spiritual warfare: for the examples of good men build up, instruct and kindle. This strong armor therefore he has with him, and this he uses, that he may be made equal to their merits, and be endowed with their rewards.



## CHAPTER XXIII. *On the tenderness of the good, and especially of the blessed Virgin.*

*Thy two breasts are as two fawns of a roe, twins, which feed among the lilies, until the day breathe, and the [0473D] shadows decline [n: (Cant. IV)]*. These are the breasts whom above he had called the neck, teachers and also perfect men, converting others to good by admonition. These breasts are filled with the milk of charity, with which they refresh the younger and more tender, and as mothers they suckle and nourish them. The soul as yet carnal does not have these breasts, until she grow in grace, and turn the blood of carnality into the milk of devotion. But when she shall have grown in grace, and conceived tenderness, then she becomes a mother, she suckles the tender, she consoles the desolate; struck with insults or afflicted with injuries she pours out the milk of patience and of compassion, not the blood of carnal agitation. For because she is filled with the milk of tenderness, she shows forth the sweetness which she received, she has compassion on the miseries of the one hurting her more than she is moved by the injury. In him who does the injury there is wickedness [0474A] of heart, and there is misery of soul. He also who endures has in himself that whereby he may be moved either to anger or to clemency. If he is indignant rather than compassionate, he is carnal, and himself also liable to the misery of sin; because as the one sins through wickedness, so does this one through anger and impatience. Blood touches blood, that is, sinner touches sinner. The touch proves what each one is. If the one touched, that is, struck by injury, had breasts, he would pour out milk. But when he suffers injury and does not suffer with it; he grieves and does not grieve with another, because he does not pour forth the milk of tenderness, he proves that he does not have breasts, nor is a mother, but a sister still little and not having breasts. Because therefore the perfect soul receives from above the grace which she pours back upon others, and thus she herself also is suckled, she is deservedly compared to fawns, which run to the milk [0474B] and seek the heights. For she rises from that whereby she herself may grow and may refresh others. The two fawns are the active and the contemplative, who assuredly do not cease to run to the milk of grace, this they desire, and they always tend toward the perfect, and seek the things above. They always reckon themselves new, always beginners and insufficient, because they stretch themselves to the things before, until they meet in the perfect man, until indeed they arrive at perfection; never do they judge that they have laid hold, but with themselves they say that they have only begun, with themselves they are always little ones, that is, humble, and therefore they seek the milk of grace, to this they tend, this they suck: whence, because they run unceasingly to this milk, they receive it, and by it are refreshed, and are so filled with its sweetness that its abundance suffices to supply the want of others. These are [0474C] the fawns of the roe, that is, of the Church, which is accustomed to seek the heights, and they are twins, because both are refreshed by the same doctrine, as twins are nourished by the same milk. For Holy Scripture instructs the contemplative just as it does the active, and therefore both find that whereby they may be spiritually nourished. But these feed among the lilies. By the lilies, which shine and have fragrance [sic: *odotem*], we understand the cleanness of those who live well and the fragrance of the virtues. Among which these twins feed, because they are delighted in the cleanness of the good and in the good fragrance of those who so live. In their salvation, and equally in the fragrance of good example, they rejoice, and in what they afford to others they are glad, among these things they are delightfully refreshed. For at the destruction of the evil they are tormented, but at the progress and prosperity of the good they rejoice together, as long as they dwell in the [0474D] exile and blindness of this world. And this is what follows, until the day breathe, and the shadows decline. Until the ignorance and misery of the present life fail, and the day of eternity and of divine knowledge shine. But these breasts can be transferred to the blessed Virgin. For she has two breasts pouring out the milk of a twofold love, because she obtains pardon for the guilty and grace for the just. Which breasts are as two fawns of a roe; these fawns we can understand to be the angels and the souls of men already translated to the kingdom. Who are well called fawns, because they have climbed the mountain of the heavenly habitation, and are refreshed with the sweetness of divine contemplation, as fawns are accustomed both to seek the heights and to be nourished with milk. These twin fawns are of the roe, that is, of Christ, from whom alike they take and suck the milk of a twofold [0475A] sweetness, namely of divinity and of humanity. For the roe is Christ, because he leaped upon the mountains and sprang over the hills. And thus he gave a leap into the womb of the blessed Virgin. And from this into the world, and from the world into heaven, where he feeds those fawns with heavenly milk. And so, since both the angels and the holy souls are solicitous for sinners, and come to their help both by merits and by intercession, it is to be believed that the blessed Virgin can do as much in this as both these creatures; nay, she is judged mightier than both, because both are repaired through her, and the ruin of the angels has been restored through her, and human nature reconciled. But what wonder if the blessed Virgin pours out the milk both of reconciliation for sinners and of grace for the pious, she who reconciled the world, and bore grace itself, nay [0475B] the very fountain of grace, which is spread abroad through the world and stretched out even to the world's end? For the blessed Virgin is the paradise out of which that fountain goes forth, which is then divided into four heads, because from the body of Christ which proceeded from her there flowed forth a fountain of blood which sacramentally flowed thence into the four parts of the world, alike to wash away sins and to pour in grace upon his own. Deservedly therefore is her mercy compared to the running of fawns, it runs through the world, it waters the world and pours itself in. It is compared to the swiftness of fawns, because her tenderness runs to meet more swiftly than she is invoked, and anticipates the causes of the wretched; but she is compared to fawns, because the sweetness of goodness which she bestows she herself sucks from above. But these are thy breasts, O blessed one, that is, thy tenderness, with which [0475C] thou sucklest the wretched, while thou obtainest mercy for them; thou sucklest the wretched and art thyself suckled by that same mercy, from her thou receivest what thou pourest back. Thou hast merited the prerogative of a singular grace, because thou art privileged above women, so that thou hast nourished, not with the milk of women, but with virginal milk, and not a mere man, but God. Whence how singular is this, because she was seen to have neither a first like her nor one to follow. Deservedly therefore art thou judged able to do as much as the holy angels and the souls of the saints, and art compared to both. To thee therefore, mother of mercy, mother of the wretched, the exiled sons of Eve cry out, the very miseries cry out. For misery has a cry, and this valley has tears: for it is a valley of tears, so much so that, if the wretched themselves cry not out, these things sound not in [0475D] thine ears [cj: *non insonent*; read *insonent*, "they do sound"]. These things cannot be silent before thee, nor lie hidden from thy hearing, in that thou hast ears for hearing miseries, and for thee to know them is to hear them. For as wherever the body shall be, there the eagles also are gathered, so wherever misery shall be, thy mercy both runs and runs to help. By God thy breasts are filled with tenderness, so that, touched by the knowledge of any misery, they may pour out the milk of mercy, nor canst thou know miseries and not come to their help. And what wonder if thou dost abound in mercy, thou who didst bear mercy itself? Christ sucked carnal breasts in thee, that through thee spiritual ones might flow for us. For when thou didst suckle mercy, from that same mercy thou didst receive breasts. And just as he took our flesh in thee, he also gave us his Holy Spirit. When God sucked material milk [0476A] from thee, spiritual milk likewise began to flow through thee to the sons of God. From that time thou wast made mother of the wretched, and didst begin to nourish the wretched with the milk of mercy. And although the mercy of God is from eternity, it began to be more ample from a point of time. And since its beginning began from thee, its bounty also took increase through thee. In thee therefore the milk of divine mercy grew together, and from thee it flowed forth to us. Thou thyself wast first filled, and from thee this abundance came down to us. Whence, because thou art made drunk with this milk, and pourest out that milk for us, and thy tenderness runs through the world, and runs to meet the wretched and comes to their help, deservedly art thou compared to fawns, and art made like to suckling kids, and to those that run alike. For they suck that milk in the divine fruition, and run to meet us; because they are both satisfied with the contemplation of God they run to meet us in charity, and come to our help [0476B] by merits and by intercession. But since both the angels and the holy souls run about with solicitude concerning us, and succor us, thou art compared to both, because thou alone canst do and dost accomplish as much as both, and above both. These fawns too feed among the lilies, because they desire the salvation of those still dwelling in exile, and are refreshed by a clean life and by the fragrance and repute of a good manner of life, which is to feed among the lilies. Which assuredly they do until the day of eternal beatitude dawn, and the shadows be ended, namely the miseries of the present life.



## CHAPTER XXIV. *On mortification, and on prayer, and on the perfect beauty of the soul.*

*I go to the mountain of myrrh, and the hill of frankincense [n: (Cant. IV)]*. [var: Vulg. *vadam*, "I will go"] The mountain of myrrh is the soul which mortifies [0476C] her members that are upon the earth, fornication, uncleanness, lust, evil concupiscence, and avarice, etc. She is lofty, and deservedly to be compared to a mountain, because she treads upon and passes over earthly and lowest things, and raises herself to desire and to know the things above, seeking the things that are above, where Christ is, and relishing these, not the things that are upon the earth. She judges earthly things, and condemns all that are in this mortal life, knowing that nothing in this earthly life of dying men subsists or is lasting. She has myrrh while she mortifies in herself carnal affections, and her own will, or whatever lower thing can delight. Mountains are wont to be high and dry, but lower places moist. The devout therefore are mountains, [0476D] because they are high through the love of the things above, and dry while they extinguish in themselves and dry up the moisture of carnal desires. These mountains the Lord visits, and he does not come to moist places, because God does not remain in damp places, but the devil. Whence every devout man strives in all ways to dry up this moisture, and to be flooded from above with spiritual water. For this he spreads out his hands to God, and his soul as earth without water, namely of carnal moisture, the soul thirsting for this grace as earth which is without moisture is wont to desire water; whence he deserves to be heard swiftly, because his spirit fails, that is, his will and appetite in earthly and carnal things. Dry therefore is the land of the good, and not having the moisture of earthly love. It is not as the land [0477A] of Egypt, that is, of the world, where, the seed being cast, waters of irrigation are led in the manner of gardens. The land of Egypt is the heart, human and darksome, where he casts his seed who sows in the flesh, and plants his root in worldly things, where he easily leads the waters of carnal delight through the garden of the mind. This irrigation is ready at hand, but as much as it is at hand, so quickly does it fail and dry up. But the mountain is the devout mind, dry from the lowest delights, nay that mountainous land which the Lord God gives to his own, because it raises the good from earthly things to desire the things above, as often as it ascends to a high heart. This land awaits from above the rain of grace, and God is wont to send it from the beginning of the year even to the end thereof, from the beginning, that is, from the time of birth even to the end of life, and to pour it in with a heavenly [0477B] irrigation. For which waters the hearts of the good pant, because, as is read in Job, their root is opened beside the waters [n: (Job XXIX)] [var: Vulg. *radix mea*, "my root"], that is, their hidden thought and the intention of the mind is expended to receive the streams of grace and the gifts of the Spirit. These waters they thirst for, and that they may be made worthy to receive them, they dry up from the water of carnal delight, and are empty. For the obtaining of which they ascend also, by prayer and contemplation, to the waters that are above the heavens, namely to all the heavenly spirits, who praise God and are made drunk with the torrent of his pleasure. But how should the soul not draw this fountain, and receive the waters of grace, she who is opened to all the heavenly spirits, and spreads herself out in desire, and knocks upon them all with prayers? Who if at length she so enlarge and spread herself out, that as she asks grace for herself from all [sic: *ad omnibus*], [0477C] so also she desire and demand it for every man. Shall she not deserve to be flooded with it the more largely, the more amply she is enlarged in charity and toward many? Again, when she is stretched out to all those spirits, does she not embrace each one to whom the guardianship of a man has been committed? And so when she spreads out the bosom of her heart to all the spirits that she may take spiritual gifts and draw grace, and is stretched out in turn by desire to each one, and whatever she shall have been able to obtain or to desire is granted to those same and is poured back for the salvation of those committed to them, he is deservedly heard who thus helps his own intercessors and advocates, even as he begs their help and patronage. For they themselves wish mercy for those committed to them, [0477D] and rejoice with those who labor together with them concerning their salvation. Deservedly does such a soul have her advocates favorable in prayer, she who desires as much to bestow as to receive. Deservedly is that soul blessed and pleasing to God which so sows upon all waters, and casts the seed of prayer, that she does not withdraw this seed even from the land of her enemies, but intercedes for them also out of charity. Much is her root opened, which is spread out to all these spirits, and is alike enlarged to all men, which ascends to the upper waters of the spirits, and below comprehends the necessities of all men: much is it opened, when an equal intention is directed to all the spirits for all men, and she strives to obtain grace for all men equally as for herself. When therefore the root [0478A] of the soul is thus opened, and is so manifoldly spread out, namely to the waters of the Holy Spirit, to the waters of the holy spirits, to the waters of the peoples (for the waters are peoples), deservedly does she please God much, much is she flooded with the water of grace, much is she enlarged in charity. She is a lofty mountain by desiring the things above, having myrrh and frankincense alike, while she mortifies in herself all worldly and vicious affections, and devoutly prays for all. Whence Christ deservedly turns aside to her, to her he comes, as being beautiful in his eyes, and pleasing and worthy of his coming. Whence he commends her, saying! *Thou art all beautiful, my love, and there is no spot in thee.* A woman is less beautiful if indeed she is graced with beauty of body, but is pale in countenance. But when she shall have both elegance of body [0478B] and ruddiness of cheeks, then she is judged more perfectly comely. So the soul, when through worthy penance she has been cleansed from sins and has destroyed the body of sin, is beautiful as it were in body. And when also through daily confession she has been cleansed from daily sins, and suffers shame for them, she is at the same time adorned as it were with the ruddiness of cheeks, so that she is deservedly called all beautiful: And there is no spot in thee of mortal sin, nor does venial sin persevere either, because she does not cease daily to wash and cleanse herself by penance and contrition. Nay, she is cleansed from venial sins the more fully, the more she is affected with great charity toward her neighbors, and strives by praying to obtain salvation for them. Again, she is all beautiful who is adorned by good works and virtues, and by humility and by the [0478C] confession of her sins (with which she had been stained) is beautified. For by judging her sins, and humbling herself for them, she is not only cleansed from sins, but also acquires the adornment of humility whereby she may also be beautified. And there is no spot in her, namely of elation. For from this she is free, while through desire she is always kindled to do greater things, and reckons small whatever she has done, while, weighing the benefits and labors of the Savior, she knows that she cannot worthily answer to them. She is therefore all beautiful and without spot, while the memory of her evils even adorns [sic: *decora*] her, and no elation over the good things she has done stains her.



## CHAPTER XXV. *On the crown of the blessed, and how pollution and certain other things are mortal.*

[0478D]

*Come from Lebanon, my bride; come from Lebanon, come [n: (Cant. IV)]*. Lebanon is a mountain, on which myrrh and frankincense grow. Now Lebanon is called *whitening* or *a making white.* Christ therefore calls the bride from Lebanon, when he invites her, cleansed and made white through the mortification of sins and of carnality and through the devotion of prayer, to the reward above. But that he exhorts her to come not only with a doubled voice, but even with a tripled one, insinuates the immensity of the desire and love which he has toward her, and that the threefold repetition may be an attestation of immensity and of firmness, for a threefold cord is hardly broken. Again, the multiplied repetition of the call indicates also the rejoicing with which he rejoices together at her deliverance from present misery. [0479A] He hints also with how great desire he wishes this to be done the more swiftly. For a call repeated again and again expresses the affection alike of one hastening and of one rejoicing together. Therefore also he calls her so many times, that by the repeated call he may insinuate the greatness of the happiness to which she is called. And that the threefold call may indicate the enjoyment of the Trinity, which she is to receive after labor, which enjoyment is eternal beatitude. And when he calls her, he also foretells to her from what things she is to be rewarded, saying: *Thou shalt be crowned from the head of Amana, from the top of Sanir and Hermon.* Amana is interpreted *restless* or *turbulent,* and signifies the lovers of the world, who are restless and turbulent alike. There is restlessness of labor and weariness in seeking worldly delights, and when a man attains them, he does not find full [0479B] satiety in them. Not had, these things vex; had, they do not satisfy. For while a man desires to satisfy his desire in these things, he kindles it the more. Whence it is said that the remedies of sins are sicknesses. Because whence a man by sinning cures his desire for a time, thence he afterward sickens with a greater desire of sinning. Because therefore it has no satiety, it does not afford stillness, nor does the Spirit of God remain in a mind that seeks earthly things, who rests upon none but the humble and the still. For he is not received by a mind which has been restless through earthly desires, and he does not illumine with spiritual light her who is filled with carnal delight. For where the vessels of the heart have not been empty of carnal things, the oil of the spirit has no place to stand there. For neither does the Holy Spirit come or [0479C] remain in a man when he is flesh, that is, carnal. When therefore the soul is made restless by these desires, she likewise also becomes turbulent, that is, dark and confused and estranged from the knowledge of truth. For earthly desires confound and darken the eyes of the heart, so that a man knows neither himself, nor his own blindness and misery. But the mind of the good refuses to be consoled in these delights, and therefore deserves to be delighted, and to be illumined with grace. For blessed Job condemns and curses the day of carnal delight [n: (Job III)], and turns it by his own judgment into darkness, or night, while he weighs to how great a blindness and misery it will lead. For through the night considered, which he considers to follow this day, he destroys the day itself, and makes it perish in himself, and so [0479D] arrives at the day of grace. Thus he turns night into day, and after the darkness of night which he condemns, he deservedly hopes for light, and receives the illumination of grace. Those therefore whom earthly delight overcomes are Amana, that is, the restless and the turbulent, and their head is the devil, who through these things holds princedom in them. While the devout soul resists his persuasions, and does not look back upon them, she triumphs over this head. But not only from the head of Amana, but also from the top of Sanir and Hermon, does Christ promise that she is to be crowned. Sanir and Hermon are mountains of Judea, in which lions and leopards dwell. These mountains signify those who stink through uncleanness, and who are separated by fault from God and from the fellowship of the good. For Sanir is interpreted *stench,* and Hermon *anathematizing.* But of those who [0480A] are Sanir, some are polluted by act, others by thought, and others, consenting to some temptation or frailty, or resisting less lawfully, do not escape the fault of uncleanness. And the uncleanness of acts indeed is plain enough, nor is it needful that much be said about these. But in thought one sins mortally either by the will of sinning, or by consenting to the delight of the thought, and by delaying in the delight. For if thou consent to the delight of a thought concerning a mortal sin, even though thou be unwilling to commit the act, thou sinnest mortally, especially if thou delay long in such delight after reason has detected it, and it please thee. For if the delight itself has lasted while reason does not consent, or does not notice it, it is not called mortal. Thus sometimes a longer delay is venial, and [0480B] a shorter one mortal, when it is noticed, and is not resisted, but is consented to. But if some pollution happen in the body to one awake, and a flux of seed, it is mortal, unless it happen from some natural infirmity. If therefore it happen from delight, it is mortal, even though it be not voluntary when it happens, if a mortal fault has gone before, of familiarity, of thought, of the glance and the like, from which the lingering delight through which it came about had its occasion. For because these were voluntary, and through them the lingering delight was had, the sin itself is not excused, even though it happen against the will. But if anyone, after a lingering delight, has been contrite for it, if afterward pollution happen to him, and the delight returns again, [0480C] it is mortal, because in the very hour of its happening he in some degree consents to the delight, so that it happens to him. For if he does not consent, it is a venial sin, because no mortal sin happens unless it be voluntary. Either it happens from infirmity, and then it is venial, or it is no sin at all, if there has been no delight in it. For it can happen from infirmity, and yet there is some delight in it which nevertheless is venial. If also it happen to one sleeping from some fault which is mortal, it will be mortal. But if contrition intervene, it is venial, although it has its occasion thence, because it has been abolished by contrition.

It is similar concerning the madman, and the ignorant, and the one who omits. For when a madman, set in his madness, kills or fornicates or does something mortal, although [0480D] he does not then have the sense of discerning what he does, yet he sins mortally, if he fell into that very madness by his own fault, and was in the will of sinning mortally, and thus is made guilty from the preceding fault; so for one sleeping, from preceding causes, pollution is mortal. So it is concerning the ignorant; because, although in the hour of knowing what he ought he is unwilling to be ignorant, yet he is guilty, because he earlier despised to know when he was able to know. And concerning the one who omits, when he omits what he ought, because he could have shown himself [sic: *exhibui se*] solicitous for the doing, and so, at the time of doing, not have omitted it. Sanir therefore are those who stink, whom the devil pollutes, either in work or in thought, who is read to creep upon breast and belly. But Hermon, which is interpreted *anathematizing,* signifies those who are anathematized, that is, separated from God by their own [0481A] iniquity, and others who for manifest sins are excluded from the society of the Church by the sentence of excommunication. And let us speak of the first, who are disjoined from God through mortal sins. Every mortal sin is committed by acting against the precepts, or against conscience. For whatever conscience should dictate to be mortal, even if it were venial, he who should do this against such a conscience would sin mortally, the Apostle saying: *All that is not done of faith,* that is, against conscience, *is sin [n: (Rom. XIV)]* [var: Vulg. *non est ex fide*, "is not of faith"], and what is done against conscience builds unto hell. The other mortal things that are done are done against the precepts.



## CHAPTER XXV. *On the crown of the blessed, and how pollution and certain other things are mortal.* (cont.)

But there are certain sins which are always mortal, such as hatred, idolatry, poisoning, sorcery, fornication and the like. But anger, impatience, [0481B] sadness, avarice, gluttony, pride, vain glory, detraction, and many such things are not always mortal, unless, namely, one sin in these through grave contempt, or do something against a precept, that is, against the love of God and of the neighbor. For anger is not mortal when one is moved concerning his neighbor with the will and consent [cj: *voluntate et consensu*; read *sine voluntate et consensu*, "without the will and consent"] of harming him in person, or in property, or in repute, because he incurs only the first motions of wrath. And therefore the sun does not go down upon his wrath [n: (Ephes. IV)] [var: Vulg. *sol non occidat super iracundiam vestram*, "let not the sun go down upon your wrath"], that is, Christ. It is the same with all the other motions short of the will and the consent to act against the love of God and of the neighbor. Through impatience one sins mortally when one so exceeds through it that the virtue of patience is utterly abandoned. Through sadness, when one is so overcome [0481C] that he in no wise consoles himself in God, but consents altogether. Through avarice, when the tenacity is so great that he does not succor his neighbor in straitened need, and holds back what ought to be given. Through gluttony, when by habit or frequently one slips into surfeit or drunkenness, and, having had experience of his own weakness in this, does not beware for himself, nor moderate himself; or when he is overcome, like Esau, through the ardor of concupiscence for food or drink. And again, sometimes in these ways: over-hastily, sumptuously, too ardently, with studied care. Through pride, or vain glory, when he sets himself before others, and puts human favor before the love of God. By detracting also, or by publishing another's sin out of envy, one then sins mortally; or [0481D] when other things are said by which he intends to harm the person, or the repute; or when one strives to corrupt another's good standing. A venial sin also becomes mortal through contempt, as Augustine says, no sin is so far a venial sin that it does not become mortal while it pleases, if, namely, it so please that a man would commit it even if he knew it to be mortal; or a venial sin becomes mortal by occasion, because through a multitude of venial sins one comes to the committing of a mortal one. Whenever therefore one sins mortally, then it is done either against conscience, or through contempt, or against something which is commanded or forbidden. Until, therefore, the first motions go so far forward that something is done against one of these, there is no mortal sin. But when one so consents to them [0482A] that he offends in one of them, then for the first time he sins mortally. But in whatever aforesaid way the perverse sin, they are anathematized, and separated from God through mortal sin. But those who have been excommunicated by name are excommunicated with the greater excommunication, and are to be shunned in prayer, at table, in greeting. For no one can pray in church while such are present, neither secretly nor openly. Innocent, however, says the contrary. He can, however, outside the church, they being present or hearing. For such also one can pray secretly, not openly, or with anyone hearing. In these things, however, we can communicate with them which pertain to their absolution and salvation. And if they should owe anything to anyone, it can be demanded back from them, and they can be sued, not sue. [0482B] The perverse, therefore, are Amana, that is, the restless through love of earthly things; or Sanir, that is, those who stink through unclean life; or Hermon, that is, those anathematized through sin, or through the sentence of excommunication. But their head or top is the devil, who by tempting leads them to this, and through this bears princedom in them; and while each devout man resists his persuasion, lest he be entangled in sins, he is crowned from this head, because he triumphs. The lions also and the leopards are the demons, because now, with open iniquity, raging, they deceive some, now with flattering counsels and various frauds they seduce the upright, just as lions are fierce, and leopards are sprinkled with varied color. The dwelling of these lions or leopards are the wicked, whose perverse manners and sins, while the good [0482C] do not imitate but flee them, and sometimes convert such men to good by example and by teaching. From these mountains and dens of lions and leopards are they crowned, and are endowed with eternal rewards.



## CHAPTER XXVI. *On the whole and perfect beauty of the blessed Virgin Mary.*

*Thou art all fair, my love, and there is no spot in thee [n: (Cant. IV)]*. The blessed Virgin Mary was all fair, because she was sanctified in the womb. And once gone forth from the womb she never committed either a mortal or a venial sin. And before the conception of the Son of God, indeed, she was first kept from sins by grace; but after it she was so confirmed by the power of the Most High, overshadowed [0482D] and strengthened, that she could not commit sin at all. From the time she was made the temple of God, she was so privileged that she could in no wise be disfigured by any spot. She was likewise all fair, because full of grace, so that there was not in her any venial sin, not even the least, to prevent grace from being able to have place in her. For when one commits some venial sin, then he does not merit, nor is grace increased in him. All fair therefore was she whom grace possessed wholly, because sin had no place in her. She had also the virtues without disturbance or the passions of the vices, and she possessed them, delighted with love of them and helped by grace. Therefore in her the virtues were perfect, and poured over into continual use, so that she savored, willed, or felt nothing other than good. Such [0483A] was she in the virtues; so great also was her charity that it was extended not only to her own nation (namely the Jews), but these unknown and known, to all men at last: to all these her charity was widened, for these she was solicitous, for these she prayed unceasingly, for these she was heard in praying, the angel Gabriel saying to her: *Thou hast found grace with God [n: (Luc. I)]*, namely, that which thou wast seeking. She desired, sought and obtained the salvation of all; nay, the salvation of all was made through her, whence she is also called the salvation of the world. All fair therefore was she, because cleansed in the womb, and thereafter confirmed in holiness and cleanness, the tinder of sin being first lulled asleep in her, so that she did not sin, and at last extinguished, so that she could not sin thereafter; she attained at last angelic purity on earth, [0483B] and the likeness of God in the virtues and in holiness and in perfection of manners: just, tender, prudent, modest, merciful, holy in mind and body. Rightly therefore is she proclaimed fair, who attained on earth both the divine and the angelic likeness. And because humility is the place of God's grace, so much so that one is capable of grace in the measure that he has been humble, as she possessed this virtue perfectly and wholly, so grace filled her wholly, and adorned her wholly. This then is the inward beauty of the mind. The outward beauty also was the comeliness of chastity, the whiteness of virginity. She was also wholly bright in work, having nothing dark or sinister, but all bright and perfect, so that she lay open to the gaze of others and shone by example, clothed in the gilded raiment of the virtues and surrounded with the variety of merits. [0483C] Such a one did the heavenly King desire; into one so comely, so clothed, the sun of justice poured himself, and clothed himself in flesh in her. Nor is it a wonder if she was bright whom the splendor of glory filled, if she was fair who received into herself the splendor of light. Nor is it to be doubted that the fire of love and the inward whiteness shone in her outwardly also, so that she who had angelic purity had an angelic countenance also. Rightly therefore is she called all fair, because she was fair in face, fair in mind and body. She was terrible even to the princes of darkness themselves, so that they did not presume to approach her, or to tempt her. For the flame of charity frightened them off; her prayers and the fervor of her devotion burned them; they stood amazed at her, free from sins. For no one [0483D] is so holy as not to have had a spot and a defect, except her. For the moon, which shines by night, and the stars are not clean in comparison with her; that is, those who shine by holiness of life as the moon or the stars are darkened, in the night of this life, in comparison with her. For the stars, that is the saints, are darkened by the mist of human fault; but the blessed Virgin was all fair, whom the sun of justice wholly enlightened and flooded, so that she had neither spot nor the darkness of fault.

Above these things she was adorned with martyrdom. For a sword pierced through her own soul, not a material one, but one of sorrow. By which martyrdom she suffered more grievously than by iron. For as incomparably as she loved, so much the more vehemently did she grieve. Whence, as there was no love like her love, so neither was there sorrow like her sorrow. In the martyrs [0484A] the greatness of love softened the sorrow of the passion; but the blessed Virgin, the more she loved, the more she grieved, and by so much was her martyrdom the more grievous. Whence, because she loved more than all, and the force of her sorrow was according to the greatness of her love, she suffered more grievously in mind than the martyrs in flesh. Fair therefore was the blessed Virgin in the comeliness of virginity, fair in the praise of confession, and fairer than all the rest who have lived holily, fair in the palm of martyrdom, and without any spot. Because therefore she is so fair, and in so manifold a way comely, rightly is she called from Lebanon, that is, from a whitening. She is also said to be about to be crowned from the head of Amana, Sanir and Hermon, that is, from the prince of this world, who bears princedom over the lovers of the world, and over those separated from God through the stench of cleanness [cj: *munditiae*; read *immunditiae*, "of uncleanness"], or through other sins. This prince of the world [0484B] and head of the wicked the blessed Virgin overcame, trod down, and crushed his strength, while she kept perfect cleanness in body and humility in heart, so that he could have no place of creeping either in her breast or in her mind: this strong woman, whose price is far off and from the uttermost ends, bearing him who should overcome the strong armed man that possessed his own court (that is, the world) in peace. Which blessed one, while by her merits and by her intercession she freed from their hearts those whom he had either cast down by openly raging, or, covered over, had deceived by secret and various fraud, is declared to be crowned from the dens or mountains of lions and leopards.



## CHAPTER XXVII. *On spiritual sight, and how the care of one's own salvation, and of another's, is pleasing to God.*

[0484C]

*Thou hast wounded my heart, my sister, my bride; thou hast wounded my heart with one of thine eyes, and with one hair of thy neck [n: (Cant. IV)]*. There are two sights or eyes: one by which we contemplate the things above, another by which the transitory and earthly things. That one by which the things above are seen the Savior came to open, as he himself says: *Into this world I came, that they who see not may see, and they who see may become blind [n: (Joan. IX)]* [var: Vulg. *In judicium ego in hunc mundum veni*, "For judgment I am come into this world"]. For the enlightening of this he came to us; he endured the opening of wounds and death, that he might open this light to us, and quicken us unto the knowledge of salvation. Whence he expresses in these words how much he loves this eye, namely the contemplation and the desire of our salvation, saying: thou hast wounded [0484D] my heart. As if he should say: O soul, thou who hast deserved to be called my sister, from the time when, by taking flesh, I made myself thy brother, and whom I chose as bride when I joined thee to myself in love and in unity of will: in that thou seest and seekest the eternal rather than the earthly with the eyes of the heart, thou dost so gladden me and become pleasing to me, that thy love wounds my heart and pierces it by its own excess. My love toward thee was strong as death. Whence I willed to die for thee, and to take upon me the hard wounds of the body for thee, that I might receive from thee the sweet things of love. That thought therefore, and fixing the eye of the heart upon the opening of them, thou openest this one alike to love and to knowledge. For love opens acquaintance, so that while thou lovest thy Savior, thou mayest understand also the salvation which he purchased with such charity and at the price of his very self, to be immense, and worthily [0485A] to be sought after. Loving me therefore because I bore these things for thee, thou beginnest also to love the joy which I loved for thee and prepared. And thus through the visible things of my humanity and of my labor thou comest to the seeing and comprehending of the invisible things of things eternal; from me thou transferest thy contemplation to the seeing of me; from me having no beauty or comeliness, to my glory and majesty in the heights, where I am such that I am delightful to look upon even to the angels. Thy spiritual sight therefore, and thy salvation, is the contemplation of my love and of the enjoyment of me. For I am thy Savior, and thy salvation; and I who was thy price shall be thy reward, and the twofold vision of me is eternal beatitude. This spiritual sight, and the desire of thy salvation, I love vehemently in thee; with this one eye alone, that is, with the one and single [0485B] contemplation of thy salvation and of my love and enjoyment, thou woundest my heart, and with one hair of thy neck. The neck joins the head and the members. Thou art therefore a neck to me, while thou art zealous to convert souls and to join them to me. One hair of the neck is the single thought and the chief intention with which thou laborest about this. In this twofold solicitude and care, both of thy own salvation and of another's, thou claimest my love specially for thyself, and piercest to the inmost parts of my heart. There pleases me also, and singularly touches me, the simple eye of intention with which thou seekest nothing against me, nothing besides me. For through this thou art bright in thy whole body, that is, by good and sincere work, so that thou art worthily praised, and hast learned to wound my heart with thy love.



## CHAPTER XXVIII. *On the praise of the twofold compassion, and on sweet teaching.*

[0485C]

*How fair are thy paps, my sister, my bride; thy breasts are fairer than wine, and the odor of thy ointments above all aromatical spices [n: (Cant. IV)]*. The two paps are the twofold compassion, bodily and spiritual. These the soul has when she has compassion on those laboring under bodily need or under any adversity, or bestows consolation and prayer upon those set in some sin or temptation. These breasts she has when she knows how to rejoice with them that rejoice, and to weep with them that weep, to be weak with the weak, to burn with them that are scandalized. Fair are the paps of that soul which has compassion on all out of affection, and cannot pass by any man's sorrows without sorrow of heart. And well does she see spiritually, through one [0485D] of her eyes, that whereby she is also moved spiritually toward her neighbors. For he who loves God embraces his neighbors also out of charity. Because he has drawn near to goodness, he has received the bowels of tenderness, so that he is moved toward others, and in charity, even as he has learned to love God. Likewise, as he has a single regard to his own salvation and to God, so likewise toward his neighbors he has one motion, namely of tenderness, not of commotion or of rancor. Rightly is the soul that has these breasts fair, and pleases the gaze of Christ, having charity which covers her own sins, and bestowing mercy upon the sins of others, that she herself may obtain mercy; of which it is read in Job: Visiting thy beauty, thou shalt not sin. Our beauty is our neighbor, in whom we look upon what we are. [0486A] When we so visit him through compassion, we do not sin, that is, we are free from sin, because through this tenderness we are cleansed from sins. The soul also becomes fully clean from sins which not only draws no sin from her neighbor through some rancor, or rash judgment and suspicion, but moreover, by having compassion, receives pardon for what she has committed. Rightly therefore is she commended who through tenderness is both cleansed from sin and at the same time adorned with merits. Whose breasts are said to be fairer than wine. In the wine we can take the rigor of justice, which pleases God greatly and adorns the soul; yet the bowels of mercy commend and adorn her more. For as oil floats upon the other liquids, and puts forward its own brightness, so mercy exalts itself above judgment, and is [0486B] preferred to it, and is the stronger; so much so that justice ought not to exercise any right, or any judgment, until mercy has more fully fulfilled her own, nor can justice work anything except in what mercy leaves over. Rightly is mercy so far pre-eminent and worthier, that she can exercise her office, if discretion be present, without fault; but justice cannot exercise her own, save with mercy mediating. Rightly therefore are the breasts in the bride proclaimed fair and commended, because by this she advances toward the likeness of God, whose property it is to have mercy and to spare, in the measure that he himself knows how to have compassion perfectly and to succor. And the odor of thy ointments above all aromatical spices. The fragrance of this sweetness, and of the spiritual unction whereby she is so kindly affected toward all, has compassion, groans, prays and [0486C] desires that succor be given, is in the nostrils of the Bridegroom above; sweet with all the virtues, she is fragrant in her works, and is known to please. For charity is the mother of all the virtues; whence not undeservedly does this virtue, which proceeds from her root, deserve to excel judgment, and to set before it the fame of a good odor. Such is the bride in heart as she is also in mouth and in work.

There follows: *A honeycomb dropping are thy lips, bride; honey and milk under thy tongue, and the odor of thy garments as the odor of frankincense.* A honeycomb dropping are the lips of the devout soul, because they flow with the liquor of spiritual sweetness, and pour it out to others for edification. This honeycomb the soul gathers from the various flowers of Scripture; these she searches out, upon these she sits, out of these she draws and elicits the sweetness [0486D] of spiritual delight. The knowledge that puffs up she sets apart and despises; edification she rather seeks out; she chooses sweet-smelling sentences, not pompous eloquence. The examples also and the spiritual sayings of the saints she investigates and surveys with her mind, who were assuredly flowers, and flourished like the palm. As their life was holy, so sweet and pleasant were the things which they brought forth, or taught, to flowers of this kind she flies; from these she gathers spiritual honey, but chiefly from that one singular flower, that is, the flower which came forth from the rod of Jesse, the flower of the field and not of the garden. The odor of this flower is as the odor of a full field, which the Lord hath blessed. Full is this field, in which dwells the fulness of the Godhead, in which are all the treasures of wisdom and of knowledge, in which the just flourish, and [0487A] in which they bear fruit both in good life and in sacred knowledge. This is the flowering field, nay, full of flowers, in which there are as many flowers as there are just men bearing fruit in good, and outside which there is no flourishing, but withering. To this flowering and full field the bride flies like a prudent bee; in the odor of this flower she runs, to him she goes forth with desire, to him she clings with love and with faith, from him by the importunity of prayer she sucks the honey of grace, of his fulness she receives grace, through his spirit charity is poured abroad in her heart, and she receives the breasts of tenderness, and this grace is poured abroad in her lips, so that she may breathe out the savor of the sweetness above and give others to drink of it. Honey also and milk she has under her tongue, that is, the refreshment of the strong and the consolation of little ones, the solid food of the perfect and [0487B] the milk of tender teaching for the weak. For she has fore-tasted the sweetness of the life to come, where souls shall be refreshed by the enjoyment of Christ's divinity and by the milk of his humanity. She has tasted the foretaste of that beatitude, because she is refreshed by the streamlets of the river of honey and butter, of which it is read in Job that the hypocrite and the feigned man shall not see them [n: (Job XX)], that is, shall not understand or taste them. This twofold sweetness, like the stream of a river, gladdens the city of God above; but it visits with its streamlets the good who are still pilgrims and exiles, and gently pours in and refreshes them. Out of this abundance the soul has breasts by which she may bring forth the sweetness she has received, which savor and edify the more, in the measure that it has been given her to learn these things in experience, and to teach them from her own judgment. And the odor of her garments [0487C] as the odor of frankincense. The garments of the soul are the works which are, or are done, in charity, clothed with which, and not naked, the elect are found before God. Which garments are as the odor of frankincense, because good working sends forth from itself a good odor even as devout prayer does. For if anyone break it off for the sake of a useful occupation, that occupation itself will be reckoned in place of prayer, so that, according to the Apostle, he prays without ceasing who always works well.



## CHAPTER XXIX. *On the twofold enclosing of the soul, and on hidden martyrdom.*

*A garden enclosed, my sister, my bride, a garden enclosed, a fountain sealed; thy sendings-forth are a paradise of pomegranates with the fruits of apples* [0487D] [n: (Cant. IV)]. The garden is the soul, in which are cultivated the seedlings of the virtues, and the twin seedlings of spiritual studies. This garden is then digged, while in her the vices are rooted utterly out, and manners are converted. And it is then digged the more deeply, when a man is zealous to know the nature and the origins of the vices. For never will a lukewarm worker, ignorant of the nature of the vices, be able to mortify the vices well. It must be digged, therefore, with the memory of the divine passion; and those nails with which the wicked digged the hands and the feet of the Lord pierce it through, while we hold the memory of these things, and grieve together with this sorrow, and thereupon grieve for our own sins, we who recall ourselves ungrateful for so great benefits. Such sorrow therefore drives out and represses the vices, so that they cannot prevail in us or harm us, while, these things [0488A] being considered, we grieve vehemently. Which sorrow, when the love of God accompanies it, from this twofold striking compunction is generated, which, while it softens the heart, also makes the virtues to advance. This garden is enclosed with the bar of silence. For through silence she cultivates in advancement and in justice, because the cultivation of justice requires silence. In which she restrains herself not only from harmful things, but also from idle and superfluous ones, because through the least things one comes to the greater, and through idle things the mind is deceived, and is led outside itself, as Gregory says. By as many superfluous words as the mind is scattered from the strictness of its own silence, by so many streams, as it were, is it led outside itself. That therefore it may remain within itself and see itself, know itself, correct itself, it restrains and tempers itself from idle and superfluous words. Hearing also and sight she encloses, lest they draw inward [0488B] what may disquiet or stain the mind. And upon these windows she sets bars, because through them death is wont to enter in. For she takes care lest she be overcome by curiosity of seeing or of hearing, because through the delight of these the spirit is unnerved from its strength, and one becomes the servant of sin in the measure that he is overcome by sin. For death does not always rush in through these windows violently or suddenly, but even little by little, and creeps in through a small negligence in the mind. For neglected bilge-water sinks a ship as surely as an inrushing wave. For these things, by use, heap dust upon the mind, whereby it is defiled and darkened, and little by little it is overcome by them, so that it fails from its own vigor, and at last willingly endures what it has negligently endured. So he that neglects small things falls away little by little [n: (Eccli. XIX)]. The care also of outward things [0488C] burns the heart, and chokes the shoots of the virtues so that they do not come forth; and the multitude of earthly thoughts, if it has broken into this garden, tramples it down like a beast.

These things therefore the devout soul shuts out, and against such things she encloses her own garden. But because this garden is called enclosed a second time, we ought to consider another enclosing of it also. For not only does she fortify and shut it from outward perils, but within she hides her own good things, and conceals them from men, knowing that when good works become known and are praised, they loosen and unnerve the mind. For it is difficult for one to be praised and venerated, and not in some measure to be delighted with his own praise, or even to glory within. It is difficult for one to reckon himself an unprofitable servant [0488D] while by the testimony of good men he is proclaimed good; nay, it is wont to happen that, deceived by praise, he believes report rather than conscience. Who, deprived of knowledge of himself, is sometimes led, blinded, to this pride, that he believes himself to have what he has not. So while a man is called blessed, he is seduced, and the way of his steps is scattered, so that henceforth he does not strive toward what is perfect, but believes himself to have finished. But the devout soul hides her good things and shuts herself against praises, that she may not hear them, deaf to them like the asp that stops her ears lest she hear the voice of the charmers. For the soul is charmed by praises, so that she does not see her own evils, and deceitfully exalts herself for her justice. Thus, strong, she shuts and fortifies herself; but within she makes ready for grace, and opens her heart to it, as it is read in Job: *My root is opened above the waters* [0489A] [n: (Job XXIX)] [var: Vulg. *secus aquas*, "beside the waters"], that is, the intention of the heart lies open, through desire, to receive the grace of the Holy Spirit. Which she draws the more swiftly and the more fully, the more she lies open to nothing else. For her love is directed to God the more freely, the less it is hindered by the delight or the hindrance of praise. One thing she asks, and this she seeks after, that she may dwell in the house of the Lord not made by hand, but eternal; that she may see the will [var: Vulg. *voluptatem*, "the delight"] of the Lord, by the fulfilling of which she may come to it, and may visit that temple in which all its citizens declare his glory. This temple she visits by meditation and by desire. Its happiness and joy she contemplates, and by the contemplation of it she easily spurns the joy of praise, and shuts it out from the entrance of the heart. This garden then, thus enclosed, a sealed fountain waters; understand therefore the fountain sealed [0489B] to be the spiritual understanding, stamped with the seal of the Holy Spirit, and fortified with the teachings of Holy Scripture and with the examples of the Fathers. For what such a soul savors and feels is neither corrupted by an evil spirit, nor discordant in anything from sound doctrine, nor is it found contrary to the examples of the saints, nor is it vitiated by harmful novelty or by presumption. With this fountain the seedlings of this garden are watered, because by this knowledge the virtues and good pursuits are formed, and advance, and are made discreet. By a sober draught of this fountain a man is quickened in soul, and obtains peace of heart. For to savor according to the spirit is life and peace. Whose sendings-forth are a paradise of pomegranates with the fruits of apples. Red pomegranates signify the martyrdom which takes place in the peace [0489C] of the Church, namely, whereby the vices and the sins that are bloody and red as a little worm are mortified. It is martyrdom when we mortify our members which are upon the earth, fornication, uncleanness, evil concupiscence, avarice and the other evils. Now the pomegranate contains within many and white grains. So too the soul has in herself many works of martyrdom which make her clean and white. For they are works of martyrdom, that one afflicts himself through compassion for his neighbor, that he pardons him who hurts him, that he loves his enemy, that he chastises the flesh with abstinence, with watchings or with other labors, that he labors against various temptations, that he struggles against his own will, that he strives against the pleasures of the flesh. In the affliction [0489D] and labor of this he drinks the chalice of the passion, and daily takes up his cross. These are the grains of the pomegranate, which assuredly have whiteness, because they not only adorn the soul with merits, but cleanse her from the punishment owed for the sins of which she is penitent. For because they are laborious and penal, therefore they are said to blot out punishment rather than other works that are not penal. In another way also they adorn the soul, because through them charity is believed to be the more kindled, and so merit to be increased. The fruits of apples which this garden puts forth at the same time are the other works, fruitful and meritorious of eternal life, and fragrant with the odor of good example and of good repute. For it sends forth cypress, with spikenard and saffron. The seed of the cypress is said to be white as the seed of coriander, as is read of the manna. Whence rightly, through the whiteness which the manna has, [0490A] it signifies the refreshment of heavenly grace. But the spikenard, with which the Lord's body was anointed, signifies the unction of the love of God in the heart, because we are so anointed that we are not overcome by the fire of tribulation. When grace refreshes and fills us, it makes us strong against adversities. Saffron, which brings coolness to the burning limbs of the sick, signifies this virtue of charity, which tempers the mind from the heat of carnal pleasures, and inflames it to desire the joys above. Her sendings-forth also are the sweet cane and cinnamon with all the trees of Lebanon, myrrh and aloes with all the chief ointments: the sweet cane, which is also called cassia, is of a stout bark, and is of a purple color, and is said to avail for curing the troubles of the bowels. And rightly, by reason of its shortness, it signifies humility: [0490B] for the humble, as though clothed in purple color, are always mindful of the Lord's passion, always ready to suffer for the Lord. This virtue is especially wont to repress the inward motions of sin, and wantonness, as it were the pains of the bowels, when, considering again that the Lord suffered for us, we acknowledge that we suffer less than we deserve. Cinnamon holds the figure of the same virtue; whence by this, that he joined cinnamon to the sweet cane, it can be understood that we ought again and again to press on to the advancement of humility, and to search out the ways of this virtue and the modes of humbling ourselves. And because through it grace is chiefly obtained, so through it perfection is chiefly attained. Now this tree is of a blackish [0490C] or ashen color, signifying this, that the humble know themselves to be sinners, dust and ashes, and judge that it behooves them to do penance in embers and ashes: *The sweet cane,* he says, *and cinnamon with all the trees of Lebanon.* As the sweet cane and cinnamon show the humble gatherings of the just, so the trees of Lebanon show their lofty working; these grow in the garden of the good, while they both do great things and are always humble and little in their own eyes. Myrrh and aloes express the continence of the flesh; and the nature of these aromatical spices is such that the bodies of the dead anointed with them do not putrefy at all. But the chief ointments are those of which Paul says: *Be zealous for the better gifts [n: (I Cor. XII)]*. Fairly also are myrrh and aloes joined with all the chief ointments, because [0490D] while we restrain the flesh from wantonness, it follows that we receive the greater gifts of the Spirit. This paradise of trees she brings forth and sends forth from the root of the heart of the devout soul. A garden well tilled, namely the virtues and the shoots of spiritual studies. This is the paradise in which the soul dwells, with whose delights and pleasantness she is delighted. This paradise she possesses on earth, being about to pass from it to the heavenly one. The first parents had an earthly paradise, being about to have the heavenly one after it if they had kept the commandment. But instead of the food of the trees of paradise they earned thorns and thistles through disobedience. She, the thorns of the vices being plucked out, receives the fruits of the Spirit, and a paradise of virtues: this paradise is justice, peace and joy, delight in the Holy Spirit, a taste of the virtues and an experience [0491A] of the divine goodness, a foretaste of the beatitude to come, the hope of eternal life. This paradise the garden of the devout soul sends forth, and out of this garden she is sent forth to the enclosed garden of the heavenly city Jerusalem. Whose bars of the gates have been strengthened, all misery shut out, and all happiness and everlasting beatitude shut in.



## CHAPTER XXX. *On the fountain of the soul and the living waters of grace which flow from Christ.*

*Fountain of gardens, well of living waters, which flow with a rush from Lebanon [n: (Cant. IV)]*. The fountain is the devout soul which wells up and flows, and always rises new, because she is always renewed in God. For she meditates, or works good, or is borne by desire toward the things ahead. This fountain does not cease to boil up, and to break forth into the [0491B] love of God, to grow up toward its own, and to widen out into the love of the neighbor. For she does not grow old through sluggishness, but always grows most perfectly and to the full, and out of the fullness in which she abounds she visits and waters those lower and weaker than herself. These are gardens, and they are enclosed so long as they keep themselves from sins, and cultivate and exercise themselves in virtues and good works. Toward these, set as it were nearby and joined by spiritual kinship, she flows out; and those who go astray, these she pours herself into, these she corrects, these she makes fat, these she consoles. And those who for the most part tremble with faint-heartedness while they perceive less of the advance of grace, while they are less raised up by the help of preaching or of consolation: such the devout and perfect soul visits, because she lifts them now by consolation, now by the help of prayer, now by teaching [0491C] or admonition. For she is a well of living waters, having attained, that is, the deep grace and knowledge of the Holy Spirit, because she both abounds in herself so that she can pour out again upon others. Not so that well from which the Samaritan woman drew water, of which he who drinks thirsts again. The water of that well is the delight of sin, which, when a man drinks it, does not so much satisfy him as provoke in him a thirst for sinning. In short, while he desires to settle his thirst, another thirst grows. But this well holds living water, which may quench the thirst of sin and leap up into life everlasting, because this satisfies spiritually and leads through to an eternal satiety. That well is deep, because things desired are obtained with difficulty; but the depth of this one makes no difficulty of drawing, but [0491D] ease. For the more deeply and the more fully she receives grace, the more she has it at hand. For depth is a founding, and a perfection by which she has the good even in her affection in such a way that she loves it for the sake of the good itself, and takes delight in the good itself, and does good not only for the sake of reward, but confesses to God because he is good. This is Isaac's third well, which he called Breadth, because the Lord had spread him wide upon the earth; for a man is spread wide upon the earth when he has come to the perfection of grace and, servile and initial fear being passed, has climbed up to filial love. A man digs the first well when he casts earth (that is, earthly sins) out of himself through fear of punishment. The second, when not only by fear but also by love of the good he guards against sins. The third, [0492A] when out of love alone he both forsakes evil and does good. And in the digging of the two wells he endures the slanders and the enmities of the shepherds of Gerar, that is, of the demons who see our sojourning. For Gerar is interpreted *incolatus visio,* the seeing of a sojourning. These shepherds feed and nourish the carnal motions in us, seeing us to be sojourners and newcomers upon the earth, and striving to deprive us of our fatherland. But when we have come to perfect charity and spiritual fervor, and are led to every good out of love, then strife ceases in us, and the assault of the demons, because they have no strength to contend with us, but [sic: *ipse*] they are weakened in the fight and unnerved by spiritual fervor and by love of the good. Then the soul is so spread wide in charity and grace, and is so enriched with its fullness, that out of herself [0492B] she pours into others also. And as it is written: *Out of his belly shall flow living waters [n: (Joan. VII)]*. This grace of the Holy Spirit is compared to living waters, because, while it fills, it here quickens the soul and leads it through to the life to come. But these waters flow with a rush from Lebanon. Lebanon, which is interpreted *whitening,* signifies Christ, who is white, because he is without all sin, and who shines in the heavens with such whiteness and comeliness that the contemplation of him is the joy of the angels as much as of the saints. For he is the brightness of eternal light and the unspotted mirror of the majesty of God. He is white also, and is the light that enlightens every man coming into this world. And so copiously is that light poured abroad that it rises even upon the ungrateful, and shines in darknesses which by no means comprehend it. It flows around the evil, however, [0492C] but does not flow into them, because it does not reach through to their hearts. For they have shut themselves up and have condemned their own entrance with the bars of sins, lest the light of the divine gifts penetrate to them; but the good it illumines and flows into, because, fortifying the lodging of the heart against the defilements of sins and being zealous for cleanness, they prepare themselves to receive grace. In these it dwells, these it fills, refreshes and makes drunk; these holy ones, the dwelling-place and city of God, the rush of this river gladdens. Again, there are waters which flow with a rush from Lebanon, that is, from Christ, into the devout and the humble. Lebanon is a mountain from which flow the waters of Jordan, which is interpreted *descensus eorum,* their descent. That mountain is Christ, from whom grace descends to the hearts of those who are valleys, that [0492D] is, the humble, who love abjection and who take and seek grace with a single and simple desire. Such men lie open in no way to carnal or worldly desires, nor do they strain after human favors: therefore they receive these streams of grace as copiously as they zealously shut themselves up against all outward allurements. And so with a rush the waters of grace flow from Christ to cleanness of heart, to burning desire, to the valley of humility. From Lebanon, that is from Christ, living waters flowed down when the Holy Spirit flowed into the disciples like a torrent and made them drunk with the sweet abundance of his gifts. And again, when the same Spirit filled the round world. Whence these waters are deservedly said to flow from Christ, because from him the time of grace began; from Lebanon these waters flowed with a rush [0493A] when, Christ at the supper blessing the bread, the sacrament of the Eucharist began to be celebrated, and, his one passible presence being taken away, he remained with us in many places until the consummation of the age. Offered also once upon the cross, he is offered for us many times upon the altar. And of him who now dies no more, and over whom death shall no further have dominion, the mystery of his death and its effect unto our salvation is daily repeated; for there the death of the Lord is proclaimed. These living waters flow with a great rush. For this grace runs through the borders of Christendom, and waters the faithful and flows into them. For the faithful receive it not only when present, but they take it in remote places also, if they draw near with desire and touch with faith. By faith the woman touched him, she who came up behind, and [0493B] did not press upon him bodily. But to those who touch the form of the sacrament by faith this fruit comes through. This power goes out from him, namely, ampler grace, remission of sins, strength against sins, defense against temptations. So the faithful, in whatever places they may be, are cleansed as much as they draw near by faith, and receive this grace. These are the living waters which are drawn from the fountain of the Savior, so that they are deservedly said to flow with a rush, which fill so many and in so many ways, and are known to flow so far and so widely. Be spread wide, then, my soul, toward this fountain that flows so widely; go about all its places, that thou mayest draw much water of grace from this fountain. Nowhere dost thou approach this fountain idly, never dost thou return from [0493C] it without fruit. So much wilt thou draw from this fountain as thy desire and thy faith shall have been. He required a spiritual touch of Mary and forbade the bodily one. For to touch and to see spiritually is more healthful and more meritorious, the Lord saying: *Blessed are they that have not seen, and have believed [n: (Joan. XX)]*. Grant me therefore, Lord Jesus, fountain of goodness, who flowest with so great a rush and art spread wide in so many places, that I may run to thee by faith so far and wide, and may touch thee, so that I may fully draw thee and deeply possess thee; and thus may become a well of living waters, and may be able to pour out again upon others thee who flowest into me with a rush.



## CHAPTER XXXI. *On the consolation of temptation, and that an ampler grace follows it.*

[0493D] *Arise, north wind, and come, south wind; blow through my garden, and its spices shall flow [n: (Cant. IV)]*. [var: Vulg. Cant. 4:16 *et fluant aromata illius*, "and let its spices flow"; jussive subjunctive, where Migne prints the future indicative *fluent*] Though the virtues are in a man, they are sometimes pressed down by some temptation or adversity, so that they do not appear outwardly though they are held within. When, therefore, someone labors under the adversity of temptation, in his garden the north wind blows, a wind, namely, icy and dry. This wind binds tight the waters of grace and fetters the exercise of the virtues; whence the north wind is called *aquas ligans,* the binder of waters. Yet although anyone in such a state, if he bears himself well, merits no less, he is nevertheless less able to exercise himself freely or gladly in grace or in the virtues. For the waters of grace are hindered by the cold of this wind, so that they cannot run freely. For the Lord, lest a man be lifted up if he should continually enjoy this consolation, by dispensation [0494A] permits him now to be troubled, now kindly consoles the same man, and works all these things for his profit. He turns this captivity as a torrent is turned in the south wind, and melts the ice of the mind with the clear weather of his grace. He bids the north wind arise, that is, withdraw, and the south wind blow, that is, that the adversity cease and the consolation of his Spirit come. He commands this wind and the sea, that is, he makes the disturbance cease, and makes a tranquillity not middling but great. For he makes the south wind, that is, the Holy Spirit, not only blow in the garden of the soul devoted to him, but also blow through this garden, that is, fully take possession of it. Then, the north wind withdrawing and the ice of the body being scattered and the Holy Spirit blowing, the waters of grace flow through the garden of the soul. The south wind was indeed blowing in this garden, [0494B] because the soul had charity, which is poured in not otherwise than through the Holy Spirit; and although these spices flowed less, they were not held less. For no one is less in the virtues when he is possessed by temptation. For although the virtues are pressed down, yet they are not extinguished, and as often as anyone resists, so often is he crowned. And it comes about that the soul which has labored more harshly under temptation is more fully rewarded with perfection, and the adversity of the fight becomes the occasion of an ampler grace. In himself Christ permitted the enemy to rage for a time, that he might destroy him; and he likewise allows his own to be tempted, that they may overcome him, and that on that account he may reward them with a greater grace. For when the enemy is conquered, his strength is unnerved, and the victors are spiritually strengthened and endowed [0494C] with grace. Then the south wind, that is, the Holy Spirit, blows through the garden of the soul, because he blows perfectly in it and endows the soul with perfection. Then the spices of the virtues flow with such abundance indeed that a man reckons any adversities to be prosperity, and turns outward losses into the gain of the soul. Then patience becomes glad, charity kind, humility devout, obedience ready. The enemy is loved not so much by the leading of reason as by the affection of the mind. The carnal motions of the heart are weakened, because the spiritual ones are strengthened. The soul has taste according to the spirit and is moved; she is affected with spiritual sweetness, she is kindled with zeal and with love of every good and with charity for the salvation of the brethren. In short, she has the virtues not so much in desire as in effect. Then the spices of the virtues so flow that she embraces all men with the bowels of tenderness, [0494D] and both corrects others by example and scatters abroad from herself the odor of a good report.
