The south wind so blew through the garden of a certain monk that these spices flowed from it, whose manners and manner of life I have thought should be inserted here. For he was truly meek and humble in heart, so reckoning himself within, by his own judgment, vile and nothing, as outwardly he humbled himself for God's sake. For he judged himself a man ever of no usefulness to be fed in the house of God. And although he flourished in the other virtues, he nevertheless obtained the grace of gentleness in a special way. Therefore he showed himself lovable and affable to all. So circumcised was his mouth that scarcely ever was there heard from his mouth the sound of detraction. a word of scurrility, speech of vainglory, the voice of envy; he was not caught judging others, or consenting [0495A] to those who judged. Not only would he have guarded against speaking empty things; nay rather, whoever spoke such things feared him. Set among those who laughed, lest he become burdensome, he showed himself cheerful, but he did not admit an entire laugh. If ever he was called to counsel for the sake of some business, he persevered sad and clouded, until he was restored to the fellowship of the brethren. Very rarely was he found to have been absent from the solemn vigils, very rarely from the hours of those chanting in choir. In the refectory using none but the common foods, if anything were by chance set before him beyond the usual he did not take it; scarcely ever, save when obedience compelled, did he enter the infirmary, and scarcely, when he had entered, could he be kept there. If anyone was struck by the multitude or the greatness of temptations, he heard from his mouth both the root of the temptation and the remedy of its healing. [0495B] For he so ran through all the corners of the conscience of the one who was sick that he who was confessing could believe that he had seen all things, had been present at all. He was of such charity, and had so put on the bowels of tenderness, that he excused all, interceded for all, they for whom he spoke not knowing of it, and was no accepter of persons but of necessities. In short, the foot which he had set on the paths of the Lord Jesus he did not draw back until he had finished the course of the journey. Whence the south wind, that is, the Holy Spirit, had blown through such a garden and had taken possession of the whole of it, so that no place in it lay open to vice or to carnality, but its spices flowed and ran back to others also.



## CHAPTER XXXII *On the coming of Christ into the soul, and that through mortification one comes through to perfection.*

[0495C]

*Let my beloved come into his garden, that he may eat the fruits of his apples [n: (Cant. V)]*. The bride, advancing in the virtues and her conscience more fully made calm, yet glorying for this not in herself but in the Lord, prays that the Bridegroom come, although he rejoices over the fruit of the virtues acquired by her. For she knows that he rejoices together at our advance, and that the fruit of salvation delightfully refreshes him. He remains, however, in the devout soul: for without him she could not be devout, nor bear fruit unless he remained in her; but he is said to come into the soul when he increases grace and rejoices with her at her advance. For daily he comes into the devout, because he renews them and heaps up grace in them. And so she says: *Let [0495D] the beloved come,* etc. As if she said: Not by the boldness of presumption, but trusting in a good conscience and in faith not feigned, I have applied myself to cleansing my heart and to preparing it more fully for thy coming. Gladden me with thy visitation, refresh me after labor, take delight and be fed with the fruit of my labor. Whence she calls the beloved her own in a singular way, and the more confidently indeed the more perfectly, adorned with virtues, she has prepared for him a more pleasing dwelling. Whom also she asks to come into his own garden, and to receive his own fruit. For the fruit is his, the garden is his; he is the gardener, hedging it about with his keeping and giving it increase; because neither he that plants nor he that waters is anything, but God who gives the increase. Therefore she attributes not to herself but to God the fact that she advances, not to her own strength or industry, but to the divine tenderness: [0496A] for she knows that without him she can do nothing, and that unless he keeps this garden, watch would be kept over its keeping in vain. For if the divine keeping ceased, the wall would be destroyed and it would be given over to plundering, nor would the plantings advance. And so she invites him to the eating of his apples. The apples are the works of the virtues, which have refreshment and odor. For they refresh the mind, and send forth from themselves to others the odor of good example. The beloved eats these apples when he is fed with those works, and is delighted as much with their advance as with ours. Drawn therefore by the bride's desire, and delighted by the beauty of her virtues and the cleanness of her heart, the Bridegroom shows forth his presence, saying: *Come into my garden, my sister, my bride.* And that same cleanness [0496B] and perfection which he found, nay rather which he himself worked in her, he enumerates, saying: *I have reaped my myrrh with my spices. I have eaten my honeycomb with my honey, I have drunk my wine with my milk [n: (Cant. V)]*. O my sister, because thou art the bride and joined to me in love, I have found in thee my garden enclosed and well tilled, that I may eat in thee and refresh thee after thy labor. Thou hast borne the affliction of penance, the fight, temptation, the labor of the correction of manners, and thou hast come to the harvest of the perfection of the virtues, from a laborious life to the more tranquil state of contemplation. And so I have reaped in thee myrrh with spices, because together with the bitterness of labors the spices of the virtues [0496C] were compounded and perfected in thee, and with the commandments kept wisdom advanced. For when carnality and one's own will have been mortified, then the spices of the virtues flow, and through the consummation and the triumph of this labor one comes to the cultivation of perfection. But Christ reaps the myrrh when he perfects in the soul the labors set forth above, by which she reaches through to the virtues and to perfection, and gently strengthens her so that she does with delight what before she shuddered at, as it is read in Job: *The things which before my soul would not touch, now, for anguish, are my food [n: (Job VI)]*. So the apostles grew strong out of weakness; so Paul could do all things in him who strengthened him [n: (Philip. IV)].

There follows: *I have eaten the honeycomb with my honey, I have drunk my wine with my milk.* The honeycomb is the sweetness of good [0496D] merits which the devout soul has within; the honey is the work which she shows forth outwardly, which is deservedly compared to honey, because sweet is that which is repaid for it in the time to come. Christ eats the honeycomb when he is fed with the sweetness of good manners, when for him whose place is made in peace a place is prepared by manners amended and made peaceful. He is refreshed with honey when someone does good works, when to him whose delights it is to be with the sons of men, who stands at the door and knocks, it is opened. For it is opened to him when through works charity advances, grace is increased and preserved. Therefore by working more we open to him more, that he may enter to us and sup with us. Happy the banquet, and blessed the soul with which and in which Christ feasts. Christ also drinks wine and milk in the soul when at her twofold [0497A] contemplation, namely of his divinity and of his glorified humanity (which are designated by the wine and the milk), he rejoices and takes delight. Hence we can also fit the food and the drink to the soul, and expound these things from her own person. For she herself eats the honeycomb when, adorned with good manners, she is delightfully refreshed by their sweetness. For those who are sweet in manners have great sweetness and tranquillity of mind, as on the contrary there is no peace for the wicked and the insolent. Again, while they so keep themselves from sins that conscience bites them in nothing, they always feast, they always enjoy security, as it is written: *A secure mind is like a continual banquet [n: (Prov. XV)]*. Then the soul eats honey when she is delighted with good work, when she eats the labors of her own hands, [0497B] when from that which she works outwardly she is fed within by the hope of reward, when what she does for the sake of the delight above becomes sweet to her, and the days of labor seem few to her before the greatness of that happiness. For she sighs to see God, whose sweetness, unless she had tasted it in part, she would by no means desire. Whence there follows:

*I have drunk my wine with my milk.* Then the soul drinks wine when she perceives the savor of his divinity, and is so made drunk with this cup that she gives over all earthly things to forgetfulness and cleaves through love to the things above. She receives milk when she thinks upon the humanity of Christ glorified in heaven. By this contemplation she is refreshed, and in this hope and remembrance she grows cheerful and is consoled. This is the twofold happiness, of angels as much as of men, in that life: the enjoyment, namely, of the divinity and the contemplation [0497C] of the humanity. Which happiness the devout soul experiences in part in this life, but will comprehend perfectly in the life to come, when she shall see God as he is, and shall contemplate him face to face. But first the honeycomb and the honey are eaten, and afterward the wine and the milk are drunk, because it is necessary that amended manners and worthy work go before, sooner than the grace of contemplation be granted. Leah is put under first, before one comes to the embraces of Rachel. And so one must labor, and the eyes of the heart must be cleansed that God may be seen. For God is promised to be seen to cleanness of heart, as it is written *Blessed are the clean of heart, for they shall see God [n: (Matth. V)]*. That thou mayest therefore see God, cleanse the eye of thy heart; otherwise thou mayest see the world for thyself in place of [0497D] God or of an angel of light, and receive an angel of darkness, and mayest not drink the wine of God and sweet honey, but the gall of dragons be thy wine, and the incurable venom of asps. For the sight of one who is going blind is often deceived, so that the worse he sees, the more he seems to himself to see; and when one thing appears to his eyes, he believes rather that he sees two. So certain men, when they see certain vain things and divine, lies, they see deceitful visions, because they believe every spirit, and have less cleansed the eyes of the heart for seeing spiritual things, and for discerning.



## CHAPTER XXXIII. *On spiritual refreshment and the use of grace.*

*Eat, friends, and be made drunk, dearest ones [n: (Cant. V)]*. [var: Vulg. Cant. 5:1 *Comedite, amici, et bibite, et inebriamini, carissimi*, "Eat, friends, and drink, and be made drunk"; Migne's setting lacks *et bibite*, "and drink"] The good eat while they receive spiritual consolation, [0498A] while they are fed with the love of Christ, while they are enlightened in faith, while they are consoled in hope, while they are kindled with charity, while they are delighted with justice, truth, chastity and the other virtues. For every virtue feeds the mind while it refreshes it with its own delight. The food of the good is also Scripture, while it is rubbed, while it is chewed, because there lie hidden in it spiritual senses which instruct the mind and feed it. But in this Scripture the evil receive not food but disease, nor do they take from it edification but inflation. For they weigh out the argument [var: Vulg. Isai. 55:2 *argentum*, "silver"] of it not in bread, and their labor not in fullness [n: (Isai. LV)]. But the good have in it as many *fercula*, as many refreshments, as they have spiritual meanings. The good hunger for it; through it they escape dangers, as it is written: *In famine [0498B] he shall deliver thee from death, and in war from the hand of the sword [n: (Job. V)]*. For while they hunger for the word of God and refresh themselves with it, they are delivered from the death of sin and are fortified in the war of temptation, so that, strengthened and instructed by its food, they are neither cast down nor led astray. But the good are wont now to be cherished with consolations, now to be filled with bitternesses, now to abound in grace, now to labor under listlessness. But they have been taught from Scripture that temptation is a testing, and they are wont to ascribe the very burden of listlessness to temptation, when, namely, the flesh struggles against the spirit and wishes to be made equal to the heart, or even to be preferred. They know too that sadness itself is sometimes of more profit to thee than for thee to be without it: *For the Lord is near to those who are troubled in heart,* says the Psalmist: *And the humble of spirit he will save [n: (Psal. XXXIII)]*. The tribulation [0498C] which they suffer makes the Lord a neighbor to the good, and the very failing through which they are the more humbled. Sometimes the Lord so forsakes his own that he as it were slays them, and they grieve that all the things they seemed before to have are extinguished; but while they are thus slain they hope, as it is read in Job: *Although he should slay me, I will hope in him [n: (Job. XIII)]*. They hope while they are thus slain, and they live, because the little fire of charity preserves them, by which, thus destroyed and laid low, they keep their intention of living rightly for God and of in no way sinning mortally. And so while the good tell tales, the Lord is near to them; while they are slain, they hope. But if he should laugh toward them, they do not believe him, as Job likewise speaks concerning God: *If ever I laughed toward them, they did not believe me [n: (Job XX)]*. [var: Vulg. Job 29:24 *non credebant*, "they did not believe"; Migne's setting adds the object *mihi*, "me"] And so [0498D] the good do not quickly or rashly believe God when he laughs, because God knows both how to furnish the consolation of grace, and yet is wont to condemn those who are lifted up over their own good, and are ungrateful, and neglect the commandments. The good know indeed how to be consoled in adversity, they know how to be humbled in prosperity, they know how to be filled and to hunger, to abound and to suffer want. He who has been just will sometimes not lift up his head, filled with affliction and misery. The wicked, to whom in the time to come there shall be woe, are here sometimes refreshed even with inmost gifts: *The enemies of the Lord,* he says, *have lied to him, and he fed them with the fat of wheat, and filled them with honey out of the rock [n: (Psal. L)]*. They are therefore called enemies, and yet they are filled not so much from the wheat, whereby [cj: *qua*; read *quam*, "as"] from the fat of the wheat, receiving even mystical and inward things. And indeed God does what belongs to love, what belongs to friendship, [0499A] even to the unworthy. But he who receives grace from him and does not in turn show himself a friend by answering to the grace and by working worthily from it, makes himself an enemy, and turns it to the heaping up of his own damnation. And so grace refreshes the good; it refreshes the reprobate also. A man has food also from nature herself, because a more cheerful man feeds with the food of sweetness. He has it also from accidental causes: for prosperous things refresh while they turn out well. That food is not the spiritual food with which Christ refreshes. And yet for the most part it is the food of the imperfect, and the herb of the weak. And often this herb usefully feeds the weak, as Paul says: *He that is weak, let him eat herbs [n: (Rom. XIV)]*. For it lightens and in part cures the disease of listlessness which the mind suffers from the want of grace. O how frequently the imperfect and those ignorant of grace [0499B] are moved by carnal joy or by natural briskness, and think themselves moved by spiritual consolation! For often a man feels something from the enemy or from his own heart, and believes this to be from the Holy Spirit. And what wonder if the enemy mingle himself in devotion and speak false things, who spoke even in the prophets themselves. Who also sometimes said from their own spirit what they believed themselves to say from the Holy Spirit. A man ought not therefore to follow the suggestion or the experience of his own heart, since he knows that in matters of this kind certain prophets were sometimes deceived. Nor is it only in the state of sin that these dangers must be guarded against, but also in grace itself it must be prudently attended to what is to be done. *Drink wine with wisdom,* says Solomon, [0499C] signifying grace by the wine [n: (Eccli. IX)]. And again it is written: *That the Holy Spirit will withdraw himself from thoughts that are without understanding [n: (Sap. I)]*. God therefore grants grace, but the use of it lies under man's charge, because one man works through it more usefully and more healthfully than another. He ought therefore to consider the manner of the grace by which he is affected.

For the Holy Spirit is called single and manifold, because, though he is one, he nevertheless visits and affects minds in diverse ways. The soul therefore ought to hear what and how the Lord God speaks in her. And when he speaks, in the midst of the hearing he must be asked to lead her as she hears, and to teach how the meditations are to be directed. For then spiritual meditations offer themselves, so that the mind sits as it were a king, with an army of them standing round about it. And so it must rule these [0499D] and discern which it should chiefly admit, in which it should exercise itself more, how long it should cleave to each one, when it should pass over from that one to this. For every meditation is a *ferculum*, because it feeds the mind; but that devotion itself may be kindled and that thou mayest feast the longer, when thou hast been refreshed with this *ferculum* and its savor has begun to cease, thou usefully takest another. Turn thyself therefore to another meditation which shall have offered itself more nearly, and which thou shalt know to be like the preceding one and not different from it. For although all spiritual meditations are useful, such nevertheless is their manner that they build up in a diverse, nay sometimes even in a contrary way, and now those, now these suit better and have savor for the mind. A mind affected thus these build up, and a mind affected thus others build up. But the consolation of the heart and spiritual [0500A] refreshment is indeed the gift of God, yet thou must cooperate with this gift and be prudently exercised in it. For God is the author and bestower of this refreshment; but man is the servant and the cook, so that he can prepare it for himself either well or badly. But when anyone has fixed his contemplation upon God himself, he will have less need of this alternation of thoughts, but will cleave to the highest good as long as it is given. For then Wisdom herself is the servant and the cook, and makes a rich banquet, since she offers herself for food. For there is no need of human labor or of preparation when the divinity brings in the *fercula* and herself serves the refreshment. God therefore feeds his friends with food, as has been said. But the dearest ones he more fully refreshes and gives to drink, nay with a more abundant grace he even makes them drunk; concerning which food some things [0500B] have been said. But of that drunkenness I am afraid to speak, because it has not been given me to speak of it out of my own judgment. For the soul which at the marriage of the Lamb is a wife, or a daughter, knows how to be made drunk with spiritual wine, she who at this banquet sits beside the sides of Christ, or about the table, as it is written: *Thy wife as a fruitful vine on the sides of thy house, thy children as young olive plants round about thy table [n: (Psal. CXXVII)]*. But I, after the manner of a poor man, am set apart not only from the table but even from the banquet; yet would that before the doors I might receive of that pleasant wine in my little cup. For I know that I am not fully cleansed from the filth of sin, and for this I shall not be admitted to the table of the sons and to their drink, nor draw near. I leave therefore the discoursing about this wine and this drunkenness [0500C] to those who have experience of it. But knowing my own folly and weakness, and attending also to the sores of many, I wish to lie (that is, to humble myself) before the door of the rich Christ, and to beg not only drink but food also from the table of my lords, or of my ladies, that is, of devout souls. For although the wife and the sons or the daughters feast splendidly, yet those who are lower and poorer shall eat and be refreshed from the leftovers of the table. I am unwilling therefore to blush to beg the nourishment of life from such, lest after this life I beg in vain with the foolish virgins. There those who beg suffer a repulse; but here, he that asks receives. And because it is given more willingly and more abundantly to acquaintances and to friends, I wish to show myself a friend of the good and to love their good. He who loves in another a good which [0500D] he himself does not have, Scripture says, it is imputed to him as though he had it. Whatever good therefore there shall be in any, I wish to love it, and to pray for the good that they may advance in the good, that they may be kept from evil and come through to life eternal. So do I wish to associate and to place myself with the good, that I may make myself a partner and a sharer in their merits also. I contemplate also that banquet of fat things full of marrow and of wine purified from the lees, that is, of souls carried over to the things above and purged from all the dregs of sin, and feasting at the table of Christ in his kingdom. There the elect are filled with the fat of wheat, that is, with the divinity of Christ; from this table I desire that crumbs be let fall to me by which I may deserve to be refreshed. And I find a reason why I should receive mercy from that table. For I rejoice together at the happiness of the blessed, [0501A] and give thanks that from the miseries of the world they have come through to the enjoyment of God. When therefore I intercede for those who fight and exult for those who triumph, I trust to be helped by the prayers of both as much as to be enriched by their merits. In short I think upon, and gather into one, all the good things of my Lord, namely the merits of Christ and of his members, whether of those reigning with him or of those still pilgrims on the earth; then the sacrament of the Eucharist, and also the other sacraments of the Church, various indulgences and many relaxations; and finally the mercies of the Lord bestowed in manifold ways in diverse places. When by thinking and by loving I turn all these things over, and implore them as much for myself as for many, I trust that in so general a comprehension and desire I am for a great part lifted out of my want, nay even enriched. [0501B] Whence now I desire to impart of the goods acquired to others who are in need. And so I consider the many who sin, and who by sinning die, some passing from the death of the flesh to eternal death. And since the heap of their misery is great, I consider on the contrary the great heap of the aforesaid goods. Then I stretch toward this by desire and by prayer, that I may set these goods against those miseries, and may implore for the miseries. To this manner of praying or of meditating I strive, and I am sweetly still, when I yet reach through to it. And so to the friends who eat, and to the dearest ones who are made drunk, I wish to be a friend, and their daily beggar.



## CHAPTER XXXIII. *On spiritual refreshment and the use of grace.* (cont.)

*I sleep, and my heart waketh.* The natural motions of the heart are various and innumerable. Whence in manifold ways a man is made sad by his own nature, or [0501C] made glad, is troubled, or is tranquil. He ought not therefore to believe these motions or to yield to them, because according to the measure of human mutability, or of outward causes, the heart is varied, so that it be sorrowful or cheerful, and be affected thus or thus. When therefore some faint-hearted man trembles so greatly that he all but despairs, he ought to believe that this dejection is of faint-heartedness and of a troubled heart, not of despair. For he ought to be confident about his own state, provided only that he be contrite, and have the intention of turning aside from evil and of doing good [n: (Psal. XXXVII)], and of in no way committing a mortal sin. According to this purpose anyone can hope that he is called holy (that is, that he has charity), by whatever burden or temptation he be pressed down. A man ought therefore, [0501D] in an adverse state, to be confident even in the prayers which he is then wont to pour out, and to make for himself the assurance of hope. For confidence hinders the sadness of adversity; nay rather, he is then in a better state than he was before, provided only that he has not fallen from charity through mortal sin, because charity advances as much through adversity as through prosperity. In an adverse state, therefore, and in dejection of mind, let him learn to be confident, and in prosperity not to be led astray by a false hope, but to exult in the Lord with trembling; because, even if consolation is at hand, a man does not know whether he is worthy of love or of hatred. Nor let him attend to the motions of his own heart, or to his experience, so as to measure his state by these, lest he be deceived. Let reason hold rule over sensuality, let it pardon the motions, let it govern the thoughts, let it keep watch, nay let it anticipate the watches, because by forestalling useless [0502A] thoughts it will the more easily repress them. Reason therefore ought to keep watch, and to keep the night watches over the flock of its own thoughts; and when that is neglected, the mind runs into blindness, so that a man knows himself less, sees less what he is, or what he ought to be, how much he is deceived, how much he offends. If therefore it keeps watch anxiously over the flock of good thoughts, so as to nourish these and to repress the bad, the divine grace will flash more largely over him; and while it shines and blazes round about it, he will receive strength against bad thoughts, so as to lull them and make them sleep. When reason thus holds rule and keeps watch, and sleeps, the motions of sensuality and the thoughts, a man can say: I sleep, and my heart waketh. For in that part in which sensuality holds dominion, a man wakes, and he is man, because the things which he then does are of humanity. [0502B] But while he practices spiritual things, he wakes in heart unto salvation, because he returns to his heart, and in heart bears care about inward and invisible things. Then sensuality sleeps, but reason wakes; bad thoughts sleep, and good ones wake; carnal desires and temptations are lulled; a spiritual gladness affects the mind, its understanding is opened, and a greater knowledge is bestowed upon it. With the eyes of the heart it contemplates that supernal joy, namely the praises and the song of the blessed spirits. This is the concert of heaven which God reveals to his own, but makes the reprobate to sleep, that is, hides it from them. And so the Bridegroom does not sleep to the concerts of heaven, because, every disturbance being lulled, he perceives it before the eyes of the heart, keeps watch for it, desires to be present at it, implores it. He prays therefore to the citizens of heaven. The citizens of heaven pray [0502C] for her; she prays to God, and she is roused by him to pray; she speaks to God, and hears God speaking, for he speaks in her peace unto his people, because he moves her and admonishes her to salvation and to the care of her neighbors. And this is what follows:

*The voice of my beloved knocking: Open to me, my sister,* etc. This is the voice of the beloved, that is, the inspiration which he instils in her heart, that, because he is the beloved, for love of him she may love her neighbors also and bear care of them, and study to come to their aid unto salvation. He wills, therefore, that by preaching, or by taking up governance, by admonishing or by consoling, or by praying, she prepare a way for him in others; whence, that he may kindle her the more to this, he calls her sister, that is, coheir; his love, that is, privy to his secrets; [0502D] his dove, that is, illumined by the Holy Spirit; immaculate, that is, cleansed from the filth of sins by herself. As if he said: Because I have bestowed so much upon thee, do thou also answer to my benefits, and about my members exercise the grace with which thou hast been more specially endowed. Because my head is full of dew, and my locks of the drops of the nights. As if he said: From this, that I am God, I abound in mercy and in the dew of grace; and these who cleave to me by faith are filled with the drops of sins and with blindness. For the head of Christ is God, and he himself, according as he is man, is the head of the Church, and received grace not by measure. He bewails therefore our misery, so that, because we are stained in manifold ways with sins, he may show that he mercifully wills to have mercy. For he is Hermon, that is, light exalted, from which descends [0503A] the dew of grace upon mount Sion, that is, upon holy Church, so that where the offense abounds, his grace may superabound. And so he admonishes his beloved to open to him, insinuating also that he wills both to have mercy and to illumine that blindness itself, right.



## CHAPTER XXXIV. *How grace consoles, and kindles, and on perfection and mortification.*

The bride, having heard the voice of the beloved knocking and admonishing her to the salvation and care of her neighbors, although she loves, and is ready to obey the will of the Bridegroom, nevertheless fears to forsake the consolation of contemplation, and to be entangled again in the acts of the world. Whence she adds: *I have put off my tunic, how shall I be clothed with it? I have washed my feet, how shall I defile them? [n: (Cant. V.)]* The tunic is an earthly [0503B] garment, whence by the tunic earthly action can be signified. She fears therefore to be wrapped in earthly acts which she recalls that she has cast off, and to be torn away from heavenly contemplation, to descend from the highest and spiritual things and to be taken up with the lowest. Recalling too that she has washed her feet, that is, her affections, she is afraid to stain them again; these feet she had set in the courts of Jerusalem, and had made them to stand there. These she washes with living waters, that is, with compunction of heart, and afterward makes them stand by contemplation in things supernal and heavenly; and she fears not without cause to defile them, if she should draw them away from these things and send them into outward care. Nor is it a wonder if she who had fixed her feet in heavenly things fears to practice earthly ones. While therefore she trembles, while she wavers, the beloved consoles her, and visits her with ampler [0503C] grace, penetrating her heart, and illuminating it with the ampler light of grace. And this is what follows:

*My beloved put his hand through the opening, and my belly swelled at his touch.* [var: Vulg. *intremuit*, "trembled"] Which visitation of grace she calls the sending of the hand through the opening. For he pours in grace as through a chink, when he does not flood the whole soul, but visits with a certain measure of grace; and her whom he thus visits in part, he does not fully illuminate. The hand of the beloved can be taken as this operation and gift of grace. But the belly is the mind itself, in which thoughts are cooked, as food in the belly. The beloved also sends his hand through the opening, when through the Holy Spirit he suggests to the soul the benefits which he has bestowed on her: that he redeemed her by his own death, that he conferred on her the gifts of his grace, that he preserved those same gifts by [0503D] keeping them. For he granted her life and mercy, and his visitation kept her spirit. He granted her life by dying for her, his mercy by pouring it in; his visitation keeps her by preserving her from the fall of sin. By bringing these things back to the memory of his beloved, God makes an opening and an entrance to her heart, whereby she may be touched and kindled to love, and may tremble, and fear, if she does not answer to so great benefits according to her power. So therefore, touched and made to tremble, she pardons the more [cj: *ignoscit*; read *ignescit*, "catches fire"] unto the love of God and of neighbor. And this is what follows:

*I arose that I might open to my beloved; my hands dripped down myrrh, my fingers are full of the most approved myrrh.* He rises to open to the beloved who, grace being received, is the more kindled to lay hold of it, [0504A] to love more fervently, to press more studiously upon the care and salvation of his neighbors. For she who had before opened to the beloved is the more kindled toward him, and presses upon the works of charity. She then exercises herself in the love of God or in contemplation, and expends works of mercy for the profit of her neighbors, as their necessity demands and the time requires. For some she prays, others she consoles, to others she gives counsel of salvation, and she prepares to undertake rule or to exercise the office of preaching, if the time should require, while she is thus visited by grace, and strives to answer grace and to conform herself to it. She is kindled also to mortify whatever carnal thing she detects in herself. But although she has mortified sins and vices, yet from the more abundant grace received she pardons the more [cj: *ignoscit*; read *ignescit*, "catches fire"] unto this, and finds the more that she repairs. [0504B] The more illuminated, the more she knows her own defects, and, being made better, she grows the more cheap in her own eyes. She then condemns the small defects which before she tolerated, and understands that she has committed many sins both in words and in thoughts, which before she reckoned as lesser evils. While she presses upon the labor and work of this mortification, her hands drip down myrrh, because this operation is understood by the hands, and by the myrrh this mortification is signified: *And her fingers are full of the most approved myrrh,* because she does all things both discreetly and with a pure intention. For by the fingers this discretion is signified, by which she rightly divides what she rightly offers, that is, she makes discrete what she well intends. But by the most approved myrrh is taken the pure intention in mortifying, [0504C] by which she so seeks God that she mortifies vain glory, and despises outward praises, and her eye, that is, her intention, looks always to God, namely, and to the future and true love. She is said to be full of this mortification also, because she exercises it both in mind and in body. For she chastises the body with abstinence, with vigils, and with the rest of labors, and cuts off delights and earthly pleasures, knowing that wisdom, or the grace of God, is not found in the land of those who live sweetly. But by as much as one is delighted in this sweetness, by so much the less is he lifted up in God. She restrains also sight and hearing from curiosity, which two Solomon declares insatiable, and by this represses the other senses, and holds them back from pleasure. She is not fed with sweet odors; she guards against unlawful touch. For she guards against familiarity [0504D] with women, and even freedom of speaking, because from such freedom one comes to the servitude of sin. For that common saying sometimes comes to pass, which says that through bad keeping the wolf has carried off the wool. Therefore she bridles the vice of gluttony, withdrawing from herself what delight craves, and granting what necessity requires. In want she keeps patience, in abundance temperance, knowing with the Apostle how to abound and to suffer want. There, therefore, she acquires merit where the evil heap up sin for themselves. Who in Job are said to be squalid with calamity and misery [n: (Job XXX)], because in clothing themselves pride is served, and in eating, gluttony and concupiscence. The devout soul therefore, while she takes food temperately, and withdraws from herself something that appetite requires, and pours a libation to God, merits even in eating, and offers myrrh pleasing [0505A] to God; thus she shows forth mortification in the body, and fulfills this also in the mind. She resists also carnal delight, so that if she feel it, she never consents to it. She struggles also against the motions of anger and of envy, and of the other vices. When she endures reproaches, she does not answer; she is not overcome by evil, but overcomes evil in good, loving her enemies and interceding for them; she burns with so great a love of Christ that she desires to die for Christ, or for the salvation of her neighbors. And if this is not granted, she fulfills only a certain substitute for martyrdom: namely, that when something delights her more according to human nature, she the more cuts it off from herself, and severs it, and slays the appetite for it. This is deservedly judged the most approved myrrh, because with such great perfection she immolates herself to God. [0505B] But the fingers of the Bridegroom are full of the most approved myrrh, when, keeping her heart with all watchfulness, she discusses innumerable thoughts and motions of the heart, and, repelling the harmful, receives the useful. Because therefore she approves the pure and reproves the impure, and with the sword of contrition cuts off and mortifies sins, while she distinguishes and accomplishes these things with perfect discretion, she is declared to have fingers full of the most approved myrrh.



## CHAPTER XXXV. *How God is desired, and withdraws himself, and how he is present unto salvation, and hears.*

*I opened the bolt of my door to my beloved. But he had turned aside, and had passed by [n: (Cant. V)]*. The bride relates again how, that she might enjoy the beloved, she guarded against the least negligences, which could provoke his offense. [0505C] Namely in useless and superfluous speech, or thought, in bodily comfort, or in wandering freedom, which things can be understood by the bolt, that is, the bar. For the bar, though it be small and narrow, yet closes the breadth of the door, so that unless it be unbarred the door cannot stand open. So these least things, unless they be cut away, the beloved does not deign so to come to the soul as to enter perfectly into her, and to fill her with perfect grace. Small negligences darken the soul, and make an impediment to ampler grace. Knowing this, the bride keeps the eyes of her heart from the dust of these things, that she may be able to see the beloved through contemplation, and to receive him more fully. And so she presses the more upon the cleansing of her heart, that the beloved may enter into her more perfectly, and as through the [0505D] door, by whom she had known herself before to have been visited less fully, and touched as through an opening. But he turns aside, and passes by, not withdrawing from her, but going apart to one side. For he is at her right hand, lest she be moved from the good begun, but not appearing to her in the face. For he is near to all that call upon him in truth [n: (Psal. CXLIV)]. She desires indeed to contemplate him, and to speak with him face to face, as a man is wont with his friend. But he hides his face, remaining with her and in her, although he is felt to be less present. In this way, although he turns aside to her, he turns away from her and passes by, lest she be lifted up, and ascribe it to her own merits if she enjoys him according to her wish. When therefore she has shown herself more worthy of receiving him, he does not offer himself to her as she desires, but turns aside from her, that he may preserve her [0506A] in humility, and may stir up her desire the more and fulfill it more perfectly. For when from his turning aside she has been humbled, and made more fervent, from this her merit also grows, and she becomes worthy to receive him more fully. But he is said to turn aside then, when he does not grant that his presence be felt, though he is present to her. The soul then does laboriously what before she did delightfully. In which labor she is spiritually strengthened, and while she acts manfully her heart is comforted, that she may bear strongly and patiently wait for the Lord, when he either afflicts by adversities or withdraws consolation. For the exercise of labor strengthens the mind by use, that from it she may take strength; and it shows rather that she does good things by the strength of labor than by the sweetness of consolation. And so in the time of contemplation [0506B] fervor toward God is stirred up, and when this is past the work is proved and virtue exercised; or she complains that the beloved turns aside from her, because, comprehended in part, he is understood to be the more incomprehensible, as the Psalmist says: *Man shall ascend to a deep heart, and God shall be exalted [n: (Psal. LX)]*. [var: Vulg. *Accedet homo*, "man shall come"] And Solomon: *I sought wisdom, and it withdrew farther from me [n: (Eccle. VII)]*. [var: Vulg. *Dixi: Sapiens efficiar*, "I said, I shall be made wise"] For by as much as a man ascends to God, by so much does he find him lofty and incomprehensible. Whence Job: *He shall not be searched out when his voice shall have been heard (Job XXXVII)*. She pleads too that he has turned aside, because the mind hungers for and desires what it has tasted, and by as much as it perceives his sweetness, by so much the less does what it grasps of him suffice it. The knowledge of his goodness shows that what she received is scanty, stirs up complaint, kindles her [0506C] to love more, and humbles her the more after she is kindled. And because she does not have him according to her wish, she pleads that he has turned aside and passed by. For she melts wholly in the love of him whose inspiration she has received. And this is what follows:

*My soul melted, when the beloved spoke.* When the beloved speaks in the mind, hard things are softened [sic: *emoliescunt*], and the things which before seemed hard because of earthly pleasures, when the soul is melted and so warmed, grow soft and become delightful, according to that which is written: *The things which before my soul would not touch, now, for anguish, are my food [n: (Job VI)]*; bitter things then become sweet, because she is so melted by love that she little feels whatever tortures the body. She is dissolved into tears [0506D] for the greatness of love, and her tears become her bread day and night, so long as it is not granted her to contemplate her God. Whence again she complains, saying: *I sought, and I found him not; I called, and he did not answer me.* She does not find him whom she sought according to her wish, nor does he answer her as to all the things which she asks or desires, so as to hear as he is asked, or to reveal to her that he has heard; but he does not hear him according to his will, yet sometimes he hears better, unto profit. And when he is thought not to have heard, he hears better, because he grants what he knows to be more expedient. Paul, asking that the sting of his flesh be taken from him, God heard better when he did not take it away [n: (I Cor. XII)], because if he had taken it away, he would not have been saved. And so, when he is thought not to answer, he answers better; nay rather, [0507A] he himself forms in the soul the words and desires to which he may answer. For he himself asks, that is, he makes her ask, and he answers, while he grants the things that are expedient. Sometimes she desires that it be answered her how many iniquities and sins she has, that her crimes and offenses be shown to her. In which he answers her well, when to her contrite he remits all the things which she neither recalls nor ever specially set forth. For hidden dangers also, or necessities, she passes over into him by faith and love, and asks him who sees all things that he himself may see these things for her, and come to her aid. For she knows that her necessities are many, which she herself does not see nor know, and that many dangers lie hid from her, which she fears the less the less she knows them; and therefore it comes about that she is more specially committed to him, that by coming to her aid he may answer her where he himself [0507B] knows her necessities. Which answer she merits, bountiful according to the measure of her faith and love. In this also he answers her, that no voice of her prayer or desire is without merit, because it is made out of charity. If also she should ask something that is not expedient, in this he answers her, that he does not grant it, and that the pious intention itself of the petition will not lack its reward. And so, when she finds that the beloved does not answer her according to her wish, she seeks consolation and instruction in Scripture, and finds it. Whence follows:

*The watchmen who went about the city found me.* [var: Vulg. *custodes qui circumeunt*, "the keepers who go about"] While the bride is thus made anxious by the love of the beloved and by the intent of seeking, the keepers found her, who went about the city, that is, the preachers, who run about preaching to this end, that they may fortify and defend the Church from false doctrine [0507C] and from sins. While she seeks their doctrine, and questions them concerning her salvation and advancement, they meet her, and find her, and instruct and kindle her to love God more. And this is what follows:

*They struck me, and wounded me.* For they strike her who loves with the sharp arrows of the word of God, and wound her with a greater love. For these are the arrows of the Lord, which he wrought for those who burn, that is, made effectual for those who love. For they pierce those who love, but rebound from the sluggish. Which preachers are also called keepers of the walls, because they busy themselves to instruct those also who govern the Church. These also take away the cloak of the bride, because whatever they detect to be still in her from her old manner of life, [0507D] they withdraw through the office of preaching, and by their instruction they uncover the danger or sin which lay hid from her: *Ask thy father,* says Scripture, *and he will declare to thee thy elders, and they will tell thee [n: (Deut. XXXII)]*. Which, because some neglect to do, they wander in the danger of mortal sin, clothed with whose cloak they will not have the nuptial garment of charity.



## CHAPTER XXXVI. *On the love of the beloved, and its quality.*

*I adjure you, daughters of Jerusalem, if you find my beloved, that you announce to him that I languish with love [n: (Cant. V)]*. The bride sought the beloved by herself, and received also the instruction of the preachers; now she seeks the prayers of devout souls. For these are the daughters [0508A] of Jerusalem, born of God, and belonging to the vision of the future peace. These she adjures, that when they have found the beloved, that is, have had him present through the grace of devotion, they may announce to him her love and desire, and offer prayers for her. The heavenly spirits indeed, and the souls of the saints reigning with Christ, she has often entreated, and through the angels in whose presence she was accustomed to pray and to sing psalms she has announced her prayers to them. For these also are daughters of Jerusalem, who unceasingly contemplate that peace. But now she asks these daughters of Jerusalem (that is, devout souls dwelling on earth). For this is the manner of the good and of those advancing, that first they seek God by themselves, and press upon the cleansing of the heart. Then that concerning their own state and hidden dangers they consult the skilled, and [0508B] that they seek the prayers of the good, and love their familiarity. For when they commit themselves to the prayers of the good, and join themselves familiarly to them in charity and humility, it is wont to come about that from the desire which they detect in them they too are kindled. For those whom they understand to seek God so ardently, they trust to know him already in great part. Whence, seeking out of affection, they say: *What manner of one is thy beloved of the beloved, O most beautiful of women, what manner of one is thy beloved of the beloved, that thou hast so adjured us.* The beloved of the beloved is the Son of God of the Father, and he who loves the Son loves the Father also, as he who sees the Son sees the Father also. But out of admiration of him and desire, the devout souls, thus inflamed, repeat and name the beloved, desiring also themselves to be taught [0508C] by her, and to be refreshed by that sweetness of the beloved received from her. Whom they also call most beautiful, because they most surely judge that she has the beauty of the mind and is adorned with virtues. This humility too is wont to be among the good, that they think better of others than of themselves, and hear more delightfully the things which are said out of knowledge (that is, are brought forth from inward experience). Those who love, too, gladly speak of him whom they desire. For the devout, when they come together to speak of God, are wont both to be the more kindled toward God by mutual conference, and, out of the confidence which they have, to bring forth to one another what they feel or understand concerning God. God too is wont to be in the midst of them, and to heap up grace in those who for his sake are heaped together, are gathered, [0508D] and speak of him. Whence the bride, the more inflamed at the question concerning the beloved, begins to express his beauty and to tell the benefits conferred on her by him, saying: *My beloved is white and ruddy, chosen out of thousands.* He is white, because he is the brightness of eternal light, and Light of light. White, because he is free from all sin, because he did no sin, neither was guile found in his mouth. And ruddy, because in his passion he was drenched with his own blood, in which there was neither beauty nor comeliness, that he might make me comely, and that his blood might adorn the cheeks of my soul, and might reform me who was deformed. Therefore is he my beloved to me, because he suffers these things for me, and does these things also for me. Who is also chosen out of thousands, because out of the whole human race none is without [0509A] sin, except him. For none is like him among the lawgivers, nor among the thousands of thousands, and the ten times a hundred thousand who minister to the Lord of majesty, or stand by him. For gold or glass shall not be made equal to him, that is, the society of the supernal citizens, whose hearts both shine with brightness and are translucent with purity to one another. And this, because he is not only man, but God. Whence follows:

*His head is the best gold.* His head is his divinity, because the head of Christ is God. Which head is the best gold, because the goodness of the divinity excels all things that were made by it; this is the gold of the land of the living, and the gold of that land is the best. For shining, so that it cannot be seen by human eyes, and by those living in the flesh. For man shall not see God, [0509B] and live. Therefore then is he my beloved, because this is his head, this his chief part, this, the sublime which cannot be seen. I shall see when that life shall have appeared, because then I shall be like him, and I shall see him as he is. Who will grant me this twin happiness, because he will configure the body of my lowliness to the body of his brightness, and will conform my soul to the likeness of his divinity.



## CHAPTER XXXVII. *On the quality of the members of the beloved.*

*His locks are as the upraised boughs of palms, black as a raven [n: (Cant. V)]*. The upraised boughs of palms are the *spatulae*, that is, the longer branches, ever tending to lofty things. The bride has expressed what the beloved is in himself; now she begins to set forth what he is in his members. Whose [0509C] locks she calls those who cleave to him in faithful service, and these she declares to be like the palm and the ravens alike. The palm below is rough, and wrapped about with tighter bark; above it is beautiful and ample, both in sight and in fruit. So the members of Christ below do hard things, and suffer adversities, and above, in the eyes of God, are beautiful and fruitful. For through the hard things of the body they purge themselves, and advance to the beauty of the soul and to spiritual sublimity. For whence they consider the supernal beauty, thence they gladly labor below. For their eyes are in their head, that is, in Christ, and in his golden head, that is, his divinity; thither they desire to reach, thither to tend, to be refreshed by his comeliness and to be irradiated by his splendor. For they sigh after that vision, after that beginning once seen, and [0509D] the days seem to them few for the greatness of love, and the tribulation light in comparison with the weight of glory. For the sufferings of this time are not worthy of the future glory of that vision. The consideration of glory and of the reward stirs up desire in them, and desire makes labors light, and carries upward. Whence therefore they consider and covet the supernal things, thence they are strengthened below; and whence they are strengthened below, thence they triumph over labors, and grow into the heights of the virtues. For the palm advances greatly by ascending on high, and holds forth victory by its own signification. The locks of the beloved are as the upraised boughs of palms, because the elect, while they ever lift themselves up to the heights of the virtues, at length through God's grace bring themselves through to victory. [0510A] In the lowest things their life is wrapped about as with many barks, while it is straitened by innumerable tribulation. But in the highest it is spread out, as with leaves of beautiful greenness, by the amplitude of the reward. Who are also called black as a raven: the blackness of the good is that they reckon themselves sinners, and bear the shame of their sins in their countenance. For they choose to be looked down upon, they bear contempt with an even mind, and mean works and a sordid habit they not only do not shun, but even seek, knowing that in this humility a precious treasure lies hid. This is the blackness of the good, in which they make themselves like to ravens, that they may be made like to Christ also. For humility is the virtue of Christ, and whoever has it will have also the likeness of Christ; and by as much as he is the blacker through it, by so much will he be the more like [0510B] to Christ, and the more humble in his own eyes. For the good are black, and despised in their own eyes, but beautiful and precious before God. And so, the locks of the beloved being described, she begins to describe his eyes, saying:

*His eyes are as doves upon the streams of waters, which are washed with milk, and sit beside the fullest floods.* The eyes of the beloved are the contemplatives, who contemplate supernal and spiritual things with the eyes of the heart. Who are as doves, because they live simply, and have a simple eye, that is, intention, so that their whole body is lightsome, that is, whatever they do or intend, they do for God's sake. They are doves also at the windows of their eyes, because they are borne toward earthly things neither by rapacity nor by love, having a dull sight in these, but in spiritual and salutary things [0510C] a sharp and clear one. This is the right eye, which, lest it scandalize us and lead us into error by harmful subtlety, cleaves continually to the documents of Scripture. And this is what is declared, that they are upon the streams of waters. Doves are wont to sit upon the streams of waters, that they may see the shadow of the hawk in the waters, and so, casting themselves upon the water, escape his talons. So the good see the shadows of the demons in Scripture, while they find there their every kind of deception, by which they are wont to deceive not only through open evils, but even through hidden ones. In which they see also that they mock now through the appearance of good, now through a false understanding, things which by the simple and by those less exercised in spiritual matters are believed sound and perfect. And so against every error or impurity [0510D] that the enemy might suggest, or that some erring and seduced man might affirm, the divine words must be looked into, and the things which are suggested or heard must be compared with these, that they may be received or confuted. For these are chaste words, silver tried by fire, proved from earthly things, and purged by the sevenfold Spirit. For whatever is consonant and agrees with the sacred Scripture which the Holy Spirit founded must be believed sound and whole, and whatever disagrees with it must be reckoned harmful. For that cannot be sound which in any way differs from the Holy Spirit. Streams too are wont to render the eyes of those who look into them clearer. So Scripture, looked into, makes the eyes of the heart more perspicacious, because the declaration of the words of God enlightens, and gives understanding [0511A] to little ones: for he who elucidates the words of God is enlightened, and is disposed to the understanding of the words of God: for the mystical senses, while they are searched out, instruct the mind, and open the sense to understanding. These doves too are washed with milk, that is, by the sweetness of their mother, grace, they are alike cleansed from sins and kept. For through spiritual consolation delight grows bitter and cheap, and earthly and carnal cleanness [cj: *munditia*; read *immunditia*, "uncleanness"] too; and through this one is pricked, so that he repents of what he has committed, and commits no others. Again, these washed doves sit beside the fullest floods. These fullest floods are the perfect joys of that life, beside which they sit, because they remove themselves from earthly delight or consolation, and study to conform themselves to the knowledge and contemplation of the supernal things, and to make them [0511B] near. For they lie down between the borders, that they may see that rest, because it is good, and the land, because it is best. They choose the utmost things of this world, because they take the supports of life for sustenance, not for pleasure, so that, dwelling thus in its borders, they may in some measure touch the borders of that life also, and lay hold of something of those joys. They lie down in the borders, because although they are here in body, there they dwell in mind, as the Apostle says: *Our conversation is in heaven [n: (Philip. III)]*. But because they use this world as though they used it not, and desire the future rest, and contemplate it in mind, they are declared to sit beside these floods. Which rest they would by no means see through contemplation, if they had not stillness of mind. Whence this sitting beside the floods [0511C] signifies their stillness, for to sit belongs to one who is still. And so, set upon the streams of waters and washed with milk, they sit beside the fullest floods, because, enlightened through Scripture, and cleansed from sins and vicious affections by the operation and sweetness of grace, they have attained stillness of mind and purity of heart. Through which two they draw a sweet draught from that living fountain, and thirst for the fountain itself, saying: *my soul hath thirsted after God, the living fountain; when shall I come, and appear before the face of God? [n: (Psal. XLI.)]* [var: Vulg. *fortem*, "the strong living God"] But those who are still imperfect and weak are not washed with this milk; because, although they have received the sweetness of consolation from God, yet not so full a cleansing alike from sins and from vices. Whence they sit [cj: *resident*; read *non resident*, "they do not sit"] beside these floods, [0511D] because they do not yet have this stillness of mind. Their sweet affection or joy is not perfect or pure, because it proceeds from a heart less cleansed. For there works together in them either natural lightness, or some prosperity, or a hidden temptation of sin. Such men through sweet affection are sometimes rolled down to the accustomed joy of carnality. For the fire of carnal concupiscence is still vehement in them through temptation, whence, cheerfulness being received, it is easily kindled, as though wood were applied. When their affection is directed toward God, it sometimes comes about that, from the occasion of places, the state, having been made lighter, is changed into a carnal one, so that, having begun with the spirit, they consummate in the flesh. Whence some in such a state suffer a motion of the flesh, or even a greater frailty. In these the sweet affection [0512A] is turned into sin. Whence, when they feel a motion of the flesh, they ought at once to draw themselves back from this sweetness, and turn to some good. For such men bitter things are more expedient than sweet, hard than soft: namely the bitterness and grief of penance, and the labor of the fight against the pleasures of the flesh, or of their own will. For harmful delight is better cured by its contrary; and sweet things, as they delight sensuality, so also make its strength. And so through hard things and labors false delight is extinguished, and stillness of mind is acquired, that there may be a sitting beside these floods, and that the supernal things may in some measure be contemplated.



## CHAPTER XXXVIII. *On the members of the beloved.*

*His cheeks are as garden-beds of spices planted by the perfumers* [0512B] [n: (Cant. V)]. The cheeks are the martyrs, who, pouring out their blood for the faith of Christ, blush as cheeks in the face of the Bridegroom. Deservedly beautiful, because they adorned the whole body of the Church, whose members they were. A great fire of love burned in them, which so inflamed them unto torments. Great was the sweetness within, which sweetened the most bitter death. This is charity, the love, namely, of God, the love of the highest good, strong as death, nay stronger than death. This had mortified in the martyrs the desires of the body, and therefore they prevailed to expose the body itself to death. Such men lived spiritually, not carnally. Now they themselves lived no longer, but Christ lived in them. Whence they could die for Christ, who lived in them, and whose spirit they had. These are the cheeks of the beloved, which blushed in the face of the militant [0512C] Church through the penalty of martyrdom, and in the triumphant Church blush with a special comeliness through the garden-bed. Which are as garden-beds of spices, and filled with virtues, which virtues after the manner of spices have alike a sweet refreshment and an odor. For the mind which they fill they refresh delightfully, and they send forth to others the odor of good repute and of example. These garden-beds are planted by the perfumers. The perfumers are the preachers, spiritual physicians, healing the languors of souls by their instruction. These have compounded a precious pigment for the faithful. a sweet draught, namely spiritual sentences with which they have steeped us. By these perfumers are these garden-beds planted, which virtues have been described by them, and written down in books, concerning which it is added:

[0512D] *His lips are lilies dropping the first myrrh.* The lips of the beloved are the preachers, because they speak his words, and announce his will. Which lips are as lilies, because they have the whiteness of innocence, and the odor of good fame. Their doctrine is beautiful, and is fragrant, because a clean life adorns and commends it. For the example of good work strengthens their preaching, and they easily persuade who accomplish in work as they teach in word. Because therefore they do what they teach, they are white and fragrant. These are the skilled physicians, first healing themselves, and taught from the remedy of their own disease to know and to heal the diseases of others. But they drop the first myrrh, when first of all they mortify themselves, and so [0513A] preach mortification to others, beginning to do, and so to teach. The first myrrh is also the penalty of martyrdom, which they drop upon the faithful, when they preach that the body is rather to be exposed to death than that one should depart from God, or commit a mortal sin. The death of Christ too is the first myrrh, which they specially preach. For he himself is the head of martyrdom, from whom martyrdom chiefly began, and through whom the faithful were strengthened to endure martyrdom. The first myrrh is the best; whence it signifies the death of Christ, which was best, because it was the redemption of the whole world. This these lips drop, that is, the preachers, and they set it before the faithful to be imitated, and always to be held in memory. This, thought upon, mitigates the goads of the flesh, extinguishes the temptations of the enemy, consoles in desperation, blots out [0513B] sins, mortifies vices, strengthens the mind, confers grace. The death of Christ, his griefs and labors, are our merits, not his own. For all the things which he did or endured, he merited not for himself, but for us. Coming also in death to the tribunal of Christ, we ought to place our confidence chiefly in his merits, and, for the evils committed or the goods omitted, to set these against the accusing enemy. And so the death of Christ is the first myrrh, the best myrrh, because it mortifies all our evils, so powerful that there is nothing so unto death, and unto eternal death, which it does not loose and quicken. There follows:

*His hands are turned on the lathe, golden, full of hyacinths.* The hands of the beloved are the works of the good, which are turned on the lathe, because they are straight and perfect. As a lathe-turning [0513C] is rolled straight and smooth, and suffers in itself nothing uneven, no obstacle; so the good work nothing reprehensible, doing nothing in which a neighbor may be offended, or scandalized, or weakened. And so the working of the good is straight and polished, because they seek heavenly things alone, not turning aside to the right or the left, but walking by the royal way. For they rule themselves, and direct themselves to that which is salutary and expedient, not to that which human cupidity seeks. They exercise themselves more where the advancement is greater, and less there where the profit is less. For many are exceedingly anxious about the least things and neglect the greater, performing works of the kind that are good, but not doing them in charity. They fortify indeed the castle of their soul with many works, [0513D] but they leave one opening for the enemy by which he may enter. Of which castle this will be the firm fortification, that a man always have the intention of not committing a mortal sin, and, if he shall have committed one, of repenting at once. And likewise the solicitude of knowing mortal sins, and of being secretly contrite for those which he is not able to know. The working of the good, therefore, is illuminated by divine wisdom. Whence these hands are called golden, because they shine through divine wisdom, and are not darkened through sin or through ignorance. They shine also before men to the glory of the heavenly Father, because they are not darkened by sins or by indiscretion, and all the things which they do are done for God's sake, and with a simple intention. Whence it is added that they are full of hyacinths, because they are done by hope and desire of heavenly things alone: for the hyacinth [0514A] is a stone of an airy and green color. These hands are full of hyacinths, because all the working of the good, every intention, seeks the supernal reward, and God's good pleasure. For this cause it grows bright after the likeness of the sapphire, and is fresh in good, that it may please God, and may enjoy God. They are full of hyacinths, therefore, so that no place lies open in them for vain praise; they do not seek it, they do not ever receive it when offered, but always look to this stone, to the heavenly beauty, to that precious pearl, and for its sake reckon all things cheap and despise them. This stone too is changed with the air; so are the good, because they are changed according to the time. For now they exercise rigor, now clemency; now they amend excesses, now they dissemble them. And these things they do not through negligence, but through discretion. For all things [0514B] have their time, and those who do not observe the opportunity of the time deprive themselves of the fruit of profit. For sometimes an evil is better tolerated than reproved. And so according to the time and the manners of men they change themselves, nor by this mutability do they become unstable, but firm and stable. Because, while they are changed that they may better bring their causes through to effect, in the things which they intend to do they always prosper, and suffer no mutability of impediment. For they change the manner, but not the mind, and the same intention remains, although not the same action. There follows:

*His belly is of ivory, set with sapphires.* The belly of the beloved is the good married, keeping themselves chastely and using this world as though they used it not. Now the elephant is a chaste animal and of a cold nature. Whence it also [0514C] signifies those who are joined, or know one another carnally, not in order to satisfy lust, but either for the sake of begetting offspring, or of avoiding incontinence. For the intention of offspring has merit; incontinence to be avoided, pardon: for matrimony excuses it; the cause of lust, damnation, because it is a mortal sin. And so married persons who serve their own pleasure are not truly the beloved's, but the possession of the devil; nor has Christ a dwelling in these, but the devil has power: as the angel taught Tobias, saying that the demon has power over those who so undertake marriage that they are at leisure for their own lust, like the horse and the mule [n: (Tob. VI)]. But the belly of the Bridegroom is set with sapphires. The sapphire is a stone having the appearance of a serene [0514D] sky. Whence it signifies the good married, who are steeped in heavenly disciplines, meditate on the supernal things and have a taste for them. These are set with sapphires, because amid worldly acts they do heavenly works also. For although they are divided, so that they think on the things that are of the world, how they may please their spouse, they nonetheless think on heavenly things, and how they may please their Maker. This is the setting of the sapphire, that walking in the flesh they war not according to the flesh, but their conversation is in heaven



## CHAPTER XXXIX. *A description of certain members of the beloved, and the praises of the Virgin Mary.*

*His legs are columns of marble, which are founded upon bases of gold [n: (Cant. V)]*. These are they who support religious men out of their own goods, who impart temporal things to them, that they may receive spiritual things from them. For he who receives a prophet in the name of a prophet shall receive [0515A] the reward of a prophet. He who supports a just man for the serving of God shall not only be a sharer of his reward, but shall have his reward together with him. These are the legs of the Bridegroom, because these are they who go about visiting the sick, and coming to those who are joined to them in the flesh; of these, not only does the door lie open to the traveler, as with Job, but they also go with Tobias to the natives, and divide to each one out of their means as they are able. Who, like columns of marble, are upright in knowledge and in work, and unbreakable by vices and sins. They are wise rightly and soundly, supplanted by no false doctrine or religion. For some teach false things under the appearance of religion. [0515B] Demons also suggest in the heart a false justice, and a false understanding in place of the light of truth. Certain men indeed bend the bow of Scripture, and prepare their arrows in their quiver, that with their arrows they may shoot in the dark at the upright of heart; and the things which the Lord has perfected, that is, has perfectly made, and has set forth above the teachers, they destroy and bend to their own error. Demons also work certain subtle things and certain revelations in some men. And likewise certain new and unwonted things, which the curious and the simple marvel at and venerate. The understanding of the good, therefore, is right and sound; their works are formed as the life proceeds out of the heart. They make straight steps with their feet, because they do all things with counsel, and they lay open in confession [0515C] not only their sins, but also the hidden suggestions of the heart. For he cannot err who lives with counsel, nor be deceived by the enemy who uncovers his suggestions. Again, the good are upright and unbroken, who are not bent that demons may pass over them, as it is written of the evil one: *Bow down, that we may pass over [n: (Isa. LI)]*. Nor are they broken by injustice, so as to consent to the temptations of demons and to fall into sin. They are therefore upright and erect, because they both keep justice in themselves, and raise themselves up for the desiring of justice and set themselves against injustice. Whose firmness and constancy is compared not to any stones whatever, but to marble, because it is not cheap and common, but precious and rare. Nor is the strength of stones their strength, because they have not grown hard through insensibility, but through the rightness of zeal. [0515D] These columns are founded upon bases of gold, that is, upon the faith of the prophets and of the apostles. For indeed the same faith was in the prophets and in the apostles. What those believed would come, these believed had come; what those hoped for and awaited, these beheld and possessed. Whence one of them speaks, saying: *My days have been swifter than a runner; they have fled away, and have not seen good [n: (Job IX)]*. These bases were golden, because they shone through works. For without works faith is idle, but through works it is precious and to be compared to gold, because it is informed by charity. Upon these bases are founded these legs and columns, because the merciful and the upright are built up upon the foundation of the prophets and of the apostles, Christ Jesus himself being the chief corner stone, [0516A] who joined two walls in himself, namely the Jews and the gentiles, the just and converted sinners, and has made men fellows of the angels in heaven. There follows:

*His beauty is as of Lebanon.* These are the virgins, having the comeliness and the likeness of Christ. For the soul which has charity has the likeness of Christ, and, advancing more fully in the correction of manners, more fully receives this likeness. When anyone has repented, straightway in the very contrition of the heart he receives the likeness on God's part, which little by little he will possess more fully, and in the future perfectly. By this likeness all the good are conformed to Christ, but more especially the virgins, who together with this likeness have the whiteness and the cleanness of life; whence it is compared to Lebanon, which signifies Christ, who is interpreted *white*; therefore cleanness is nearest [0516B] to the author of cleanness. Whence, because the virgins obtain this special comeliness, they are well called beauty. And because they are without the stain of pollution, they are more familiarly joined to the Lamb without stain. They also follow the Lamb whithersoever he shall go. They follow specially him to whom they have specially made themselves like. To live in the flesh beyond the flesh is a heavenly life, not an earthly one. Whence, when virgins tame the sin of the flesh, which above all prevails over the human race and clings to nature, in a certain manner they are outside the flesh and are made like to God, who is Spirit. They are therefore the beauty of Christ as the beauty of Lebanon. For Lebanon is Christ, nor is he only white, but also a whitening. For he makes the elect white and comely. Lebanon also is fruitful in noble trees; so Christ brings forth noble ones, [0516C] namely those whom he had regenerated through his Spirit into liberty, that they may be sons of God and truly free, that they may not commit sin, that they may not be servants of sin. Among these trees the loftier and nobler was the blessed Virgin, who was exalted like a cedar in Lebanon, that is, in Christ, because she is not lofty through herself, but through Christ, and was made lofty and chosen by him, so that she might thus grow up and herself give increase to her own Creator. A lofty and noble tree, which brought forth this lofty fruit, namely the Son of God. Finally, she is herself the cedar which grew in Lebanon, that is, in Christ; and while she herself begot Christ, the cedar as it were produced Lebanon, and the mountain came forth out of the tree when God came forth out of man. This is the new and unheard-of thing which [0516D] God did upon the earth, that a cedar should bring forth Lebanon, a tree a mountain, that a woman should compass a man, that is, God, and that him whom the world could not contain the Virgin should enclose in her womb. And so the virgins are the beauty of Christ, but the blessed Virgin is his special beauty, who never stained her beauty by any mortal or venial sin. Whence the heavenly King desired her beauty, and, taking flesh in her, adorned this beauty yet more, because he filled her with an ampler grace. And so, when the world was wrapped in darkness and deformed, he brought forth the Sun of justice, beautiful in form above the sons of men, and thus restored light for blindness, and a beautiful beauty for deformity. She therefore, singularly the beauty of Christ, beautiful above all, and most like to Christ, not only [0517A] having herself the beauty of Christ, but also restoring it in others. And so Mary is above men, in that she never committed sin, as men do, among whom none has been found who does good and does not sin. She is also above the angels, because she surpasses them in purity, while she contemplates the divinity more clearly than they. And she not only did not commit sin, but also blotted out the sins of others; nor was she only bright in herself, but also sent forth rays of light from herself, because through her the world received the light of grace.
