And so Mary is the middle and the mediatrix between things divine and things human, so that she surpasses human things, while she works things so lofty through her Son. O wonderful dignity, that from a woman there should proceed a sun enlightening the world, that light should shine out from earth, that through a woman the darkness of the world [0517B] should brighten into light! Through her that sun shone out of her upon the world, and gave a brighter light, because it more fully illumined hearts. Thus the eternal light rose new out of the temporal, and spread its rays more widely. Nor was the light of grace given through her on earth only, but also the vision of God to souls in heaven. This blessed one is that great sign in heaven, a woman clothed with the sun, because the sun of divinity shines in upon her, and shines round about her, and occupies her wholly, and after her, who is most of all enlightened, gives light to the citizens of heaven. She first, and above all, received the splendor of this sun. She is clothed with this sun, and is perfectly and pre-eminently flooded with the enjoyment of the divinity. And she as it were put it on, when she received so great a fullness of it. But she is clothed with this [0517C] sun in heaven for this reason, because on earth she had perfectly put on Christ, as the Apostle says: and she was *full of grace* [n: (Luc. I)]. But the moon is under her feet, that is, the Church triumphant, because it is lower, and, enlightened by this sun after her, the moon suffers a waning; and therefore, because every man except her has had some waning, he can be enlightened by this sun, but not equally clothed. This blessed one has been exalted like a cedar, has been made lofty like Christ, and if as much [cj: *tantum*; read *non tantum*, "not so much"] as Christ: Christ was the least in the kingdom of heaven, that is, in the present Church; Mary was the humblest of all who have been upon earth. Therefore she was made lofty unto this, that she might conceive the highest of all, and after him become the highest of all. Well had she humbled herself with Christ, who was to be made lofty with Christ. As [0517D] Christ is at the right hand of the Father, so is she at the right hand of the Son. Christ is above all the works of his hands; Mary is above those same works after Christ, because she has been exalted not only above the souls of the saints, but also above the choirs of the angels. In the most humble Mary, therefore, Christ humbled himself unto the taking of flesh, and thus exalted her humbled with himself. Lofty therefore is Mary, but loftier is Christ. Whence of him there follows:

*Chosen as the cedars.* To the beauty, the strength, the loftiness and the fragrance of the cedars all the glory of the woods yields; so neither the highest men on earth nor the angels in heaven can be made equal to Christ. I wish therefore to consider how lofty and pre-eminent he is in himself, and how greatly he humbled himself for my sake: whence, when [0518A] I fear the judge, I wish to consider the brother, and the partner of my nature. For he will judge in the humanity which he mercifully took for us. Let him bear the sentence of humanity and of mercy, of a brother, and not of a judge or of justice. If I shall wish to justify myself, let him be judge, and let him condemn me; if I confess myself wretched, let him bestow mercy. I myself wish to be judge in the judge's place, that he himself may not be judge, but a brother in the judge's place; and I wish to judge myself, lest he himself judge. If I come so to the judgment, and have with me in my cause the mother of mercy, who shall deny that the judge is favorable? Mary was made the Mother of God because of mercy; and this too I believe that she exercises unceasingly for the human race before the Father and the Son, and most of all will exercise this judgment in the judgment for those to be examined. [0518B] She is the gate in which Christ, the husband of the Church, will be noble, when he shall sit to judge with the senators of the earth. Then the loftiness of this cedar will appear, and how greatly he stands out above men, and how exalted he was reckoned, whose breath was in his nostrils, that is, breathed in the mouth of men. Let us therefore seek Mary, the mother of mercy and the gate of heaven, and let us hope for mercy in the gate of judgment, that through the gate of judgment we may enter unto eternal life.



## CHAPTER XL. *On Christ and his merits.*

*His throat is most sweet, and he is wholly desirable [n: (Cant. V)]*. The throat of the Bridegroom is his inward savor, and spiritual consolation, which sweetly affect those who taste and have experience of them. [0518C] For if by thinking thou comprehend the goodness of God, and, reaching unto the savor of him, thou wilt proclaim with the bride the sweetness of this throat. The taste also of his love is so sweet that it not only refreshes the mind, but even makes one forget the food of the body. If thou also contemplate spiritual and mystical things, whatever thou hast been able to comprehend of him thou wilt approve as sweet and delightful, so much so that thou wilt reckon all things except him but little sweet, nay none at all, and wilt judge that he alone is good, that he alone is to be embraced and loved. But if thou pass over in mind to the considering of things above, it will be sweet to meditate what it is to see God, and to be satisfied with the vision of him, that he himself is in us, and we in him, and that we are one with him, and to lack all misery, and to rejoice with him without end: the inward savor of these things [0518D] is the throat of the Bridegroom. But the heart which shall perceive this sweetness must be cleansed of vicious affections, because unless the vessel of the heart be pure, it less perceives, or retains, this sweetness of the spirit, because it has thus far drawn near out of the dregs of carnality. Let it therefore refuse to be consoled save in God; let it be mindful of God, and be delighted in him, and be exercised in this delight, so that his spirit may fail, that is, his own will, and that he may despise what delights outside God. As much as he shall have despised outward delights, so much will he be able to be delighted in God. Let him cast away the delight of worldly things; let him leave the world, that is, worldly things; let him approach the solitude of the mind; let him sit solitary, and let him lift himself above himself, let him lift the body above the body, that he may transcend bodily things, and comprehend the things above and the spiritual. All that is [0519A] in the world is concupiscence of the flesh, or concupiscence of the eyes, or pride of life. He who shall have despised these things, by vanquishing carnal concupiscence, by calling himself back from outward things to inward and invisible things, and by humbling himself with pride trodden underfoot, assuredly leaves the world and comes to solitude, so that he may be able to lay hold of the acquaintance and the savor of spiritual things. This is the Egypt out of which the people of God goes forth, and this is the solitude to which it ought to strive. Out of this solitude messengers are sent, and they bring back of the fruits of the land of promise. These messengers are spiritual meditations and holy desires. These are sent by the devout soul to consider this land. These, by searching out this land, enter it; they bring word of its abundance and sweetness; they carry of the fruit with them. This fruit is [0519B] the taste of that sweetness, the foretaste of that sweetness, a fruit sweet to the throat of the devout soul that tastes this fruit and experiences its sweetness. He who tastes this sees, that is, knows, how sweet is this most sweet throat of the Bridegroom, which not only excels all sweetness, but even expels it. The more the sweetness of this is felt, the more it is desired. It has hunger in this life, not satiety; refreshment, not fullness. For it is so sweet that it is always desired, so immense that it is never comprehended to the full; and he who is so affected as to call himself satisfied, and wholly filled, I know not whether he is refreshed from the throat of the Bridegroom, or from adultery and a false messenger: *They that eat me shall yet hunger,* says the Wisdom of God; *and they that drink me shall yet thirst [n: (Eccli. XXIV)]*. Here the goodness of God is a slender taste, [0519C] in the future satiety, wherewith we shall be satisfied when his glory shall be made manifest, and we shall be filled with the good things of the house of the Lord. The true savor is thus discerned from the false, because it is not vehement or impetuous; it does not titillate the flesh, but restrains and mitigates the motions of the flesh. It is modest, tranquil, light, sweet, peaceable; it strengthens the spirit, weakens the flesh, kindles and enlightens the mind; it does not lift up, but humbles. For its sweetness stirs up desire, and while it kindles ampler desires it always renders a man humble concerning his own advance. For since what he has is little, as he always reckons himself needy, so he always thinks humbly of himself. An imperfect or false savor shows a man rich to himself, because it does not penetrate unto the knowing of the riches of the goodness of God. But that savor is [0519D] imperfect which the new and the carnal as yet receive, and those who have not yet attained the perfect cleanness of the heart, but have the eyes of the heart bleared, so that they cannot see clearly, and comprehend spiritual things. Thence therefore they think themselves rich, whence they see little, and have not the true knowledge. If such men, being more enlightened, knew more, and laid hold of the immensity of this sweetness, they would rather be humbled than lifted up, because they would understand how scant is what they were able to perceive of it. False is the savor which the enemy works, and which sweetly affects and gladdens the mind; but it lifts up, it does not humble; it counterfeits fullness, but has it not: for how empty of true grace those are who perceive this will be plain if they be tried by adversities. Whence they may be compared to a bridal chamber [0520A] which is shining without, but empty within; such men shine and glitter outwardly, because they have a certain appearance of devotion, but within they are empty of virtues and have no fervor. In the secret place of this bridal chamber the enemy sleeps and has stillness, because holy desires and fiery prayers do not rouse or put him to flight, but the lukewarmness of the mind and the false brightness of religion endures him and cherishes him. Their glory is not from within, nor is it so much in conscience as in outward repute; and because they are poured out outwardly, therefore they do not know or perceive inward things. Their savor, even if it be sweet, yet is not most sweet, because it is not from the Bridegroom, but from the adversary. Most sweet is that which the good perceive, who through cleanness of heart have come to the true acquaintance of his goodness. These can proclaim his most sweet [0520B] throat, because, experience teaching them, they have tasted his sweetness. If we also consider his humanity, most sweet was his throat, through which the words of the divinity came forth, because they were spirit and life, as he himself says. Sweet therefore was the preaching, and those words with which he spoke of the kingdom of God. Most sweet therefore was his throat, and he wholly desirable: desirable was the vision of his humanity on earth, but much more desirable his glorified humanity in heaven, which is desirable even to the angels, who desire to look into it. For he is desirable in countenance, whose countenance the whole company of the citizens above desires; but most desired of all is the contemplation of his divinity, to which all that is desired cannot be compared. He is therefore [0520C] wholly desirable, who is also desirable in his members, alike in those still sojourning on earth and in those reigning in heaven, because their grace delights as much as their glory considered. For here they have the brightness of holiness, and there of glorification. Their holiness is beloved for imitating, and their joy for rejoicing with them and enjoying together with them. Sweet is the contemplation of their joy, which we hope will be ours also, and which we await. This awaiting has gladness, so that we may be rejoicing in hope of this, and patient in tribulations, or in the labors which we bear for this. Christ is therefore desirable even in his own, because out of his own glory and comeliness he made them comely and desirable, so that he may be desired not only for his own sake, but [0520D] for the sake of the elect, whom we shall have as companions and partners with him in glory, and in whose glory we shall rejoice. He is therefore wholly desirable, because in himself and in his members. And this is my beloved, O daughters of Jerusalem; this is he by whom I am loved and whom I love, not in word, nor with a human tongue, but in work and in truth, because I hear his commandments and keep them. Consider the beauty of him, desire his love, O daughters of Jerusalem, that is, souls desiring to come to the vision of the peace above: for through his love, and the fulfilling of his commandments, one comes through to the vision of his beauty, to the happiness and peace above, to which may Jesus Christ, the Bridegroom of the Church, deign to lead us, who lives and reigns world without end. Amen.



## CHAPTER XLI. *The Bridegroom invites the bride to receive her inheritance.*

[0521A]

*Come into my garden, my sister, my bride [n: (Cant. V)]*. Thou hast been made a garden for me, O soul, by working in thee for me the diverse perfumes of the virtues and of good work. Now also it is time that thou come into my garden, to receive the delights of my garden, because thou art a sister, thou art an heir; come to the inheritance, because thou art a bride; come to the embraces, come to sleep that thou mayest receive the inheritance; because, when I shall have given sleep to my beloved, this is their inheritance. Thou hast slept outwardly, but thou hast watched with thy heart and hast thought upon the inheritance; come to the joy for which thou hast labored, rest after labor, contemplate by sight what thou hast contemplated by faith. [0521B] Hope has refreshed thee; let vision now refresh thee. In the left hand thou hast been still; let the right hand embrace thee, and let earthly things not constrain thee and encompass thee; come into my garden. My garden is a garden of delights. In my garden the roses of the martyrs are red with wondrous comeliness; with wondrous whiteness the lilies of the confessors and of the virgins shine and give forth their fragrance. In my garden there are kinds of diverse fruits, and as many delights as there are merits of the blessed who reign in the kingdom. In my garden is every planting of the ancient Fathers, or of the new grace. Whatever was ever planted, whatever of good has been done from the beginning of the world, hither the fruit of all has ascended. Hither it has finally come; all these things have profited thee, they await thee, they will receive thee. This is the fruit of my elect, which always remains, and is not only the fruit of those who do it, but also of those who love it in [0521C] others; by loving it thou hast made it thine, for by loving me thou hast loved all that is mine; by loving thou hast made thine things mine, and the merits of my own are common to my elect. For single things are not only of single men, but all things are of all. Come therefore hither, to receive all things at once, because all things have been prepared for thee: new things with old have I kept for thee, namely the ancient and the recent merits of my own, that thou mayest be delighted with all, mayest possess all, mayest be satisfied out of all. I have reaped my myrrh with my spices: I reaped myrrh in the martyrs, spices in the confessors and in those who lived well. The myrrh of the martyrs has come to the harvest: for their bitterness passes away, and they have come through to the fruit; for they have received the rewards for their labor. The confessors, from the confession of their own [0521D] sins and of my praise, have passed over to confessing to my name unto praise without labor, unto the praise of peace and of the vision of me. The confessors were spices: for by the example of a good life they refreshed others and were fragrant with a good odor. My myrrh also have I reaped: for I consummated the bitterness and the punishment of my death, but through this I obtained for my members the sweet joys of salvation. The tastes of whose passion are savory to me through love, but that which is confected out of it, with the sweet spice of the redemption of man, refreshes me; neither the myrrh of the martyrs nor the spices of the confessors would have perceived these joys, if the myrrh of my death had not perceived them. My blood extinguished the flaming sword set before paradise; and this being extinguished, the cherubim also, whom it barred along with itself, does not deny to man [0522A] the entrance, since he knows him to have been redeemed by the blood of his own Creator. Nor does he receive him only into his own fellowship, but also unto the likeness of knowledge; for the cherubim is called *the fullness of knowledge*, and man receives the likeness of knowledge, that he may know perfectly, and even as he has been known, who here knew by experience. I have eaten the honeycomb with my honey, I have drunk my wine with my milk. In the passion I bore my bitter things, but now I perceive the sweet things of the resurrection. Then roasted as a fish, now I eat the honey of glorified humanity. And I who was as one despised and the last of men am now crowned with glory and honor and set above all the things of the Father, and not only above all the things of men, but also of angels; I eat the honeycomb with the honey, because in glorified humanity I enjoy in [0522B] myself the glory of the divinity, and in my members I perceive the same. This is my sweetness, these my delights with the sons of men, these the feasts, this the banquet. This is food and drink to me, that I eat these things more sweetly than honey, and drink them more pleasantly and delightfully than wine and milk. In these delights I take my joy, and with the same I feed and give drink to my members: for the honeycomb of honey were to me those sweet in manners and in works, the wine and the milk the wise and the simple; to which banquet come, O sister and bride, after thy labors.

## CHAPTER XLII. *On the assumption of Mary, and her praises.*

*Who is this that ascends, and crossing the deserts? [n: (Cant. III)]* [var: Vulg. Cant. 8:5 *de deserto*, "out of the desert"; Cant. 3:6, which the note cites, *per desertum*, "through the desert"]. Whither does she tend, or what does she portend, covered with flowers? *Papae,* filled with riches, she does not run upon the thief, the singular virgin. O robber, now art thou robbed, made the prey [0522C] of a virgin! This is the enemy of Holofernes, the Hebrew woman, why dost thou spurn the Jews, Aman, Esther is a Jewess, thou spreadest a cross for Mardochai, thou shalt be punished with it. The singular wild beast; the king is Assuerus, Esther of great name. This is that rod of Jesse, which Isaias reports to have been born of the root, whose flower is the Messiah, the harvest of whose passion has strewn the ways of the world with fruit a hundredfold, with sixtyfold and thirtyfold. This is that Sunamite who served David. This Jahel, so tiny, crushed Sisara. This is the queen of Saba, who came to the realms of king Solomon, whose wisdom she heard in the sanctuary; she saves her male child out of the hand of Pharaoh; this is the fleece of Gedeon, enriching the little vessel with dew; this is the singular *ferculum* of the true Solomon, and the ivory tower, [0522D] golden, shining from the whole, wholly delightful; this is she in whom the king reclines himself, the reclining-place of wayfarers while he turns aside; this is the inn, the King of eternal glory inclines his head upon her, having no hut. O of how sweet a memory is a mother so praiseworthy! This is the tree set aflame, but that knows not how to be burned up; the star begotten of Jacob, the herald of the true sun; the gate always barred, passable to the king alone; but also the ark of the covenant, borne upon the shoulders of David, carried over with great jubilation. She, holding the moon beneath her sole, stands compassed with the sun; such does she stand forth, and so holy, having so many charisms, so many figures, signs so great, bearing so many riddles, as there are stars of heaven. Consider how great she is, thou whoever thou art in jubilation: propped up with these little flowers, hedged about with these apples, she stands plucked out of perils, laurelled in the harbor, and, blessed, joined to the Trinity, she now enjoys the longed-for [0523A] kisses! O let the reborn now applaud! O the jubilation wherewith she jubilates, the mother made one with her son! O how brightly she glows red, clothed in double garments; the translator does not so winnow it, faith so alleges, that she now reigns with God! Hail, Mary, full of grace; may God be with thee. Of our corruption, which the Jew dreams. Let the seducer Sadducee waste away in his deceit: [0524A] for thou hast truly risen. O sweet mother of Christ, have mercy on the guilty! Hail, Mary, mother of grace, queen of the world, mother of mercy, save us from ruin, fountain of life, vein of pardon; hasten then to come when we draw near to death, that with thee we may be able to rejoice perpetually. Amen.
